**Summary.** This work extends the Peres lattice technique from stationary Hamiltonians to periodically driven quantum systems. It demonstrates that this method is highly effective for diagnosing complex dynamics, allowing researchers to map out resonances, track the onset of chaos, and identify critical points in Floquet spectra. The analysis confirms the utility of geometric tools to understand time-dependent many-body physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, geometric diagnostic tool (Peres lattices) to visualize complex phenomena like chaos and resonances in time-dependent quantum systems, bridging the gap between static and driven many-body physics relevant to cavity QED or ultracold atoms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is extending the Peres lattice method, originally for stationary systems, to analyze complex dynamics in periodically driven quantum systems.

**Main result.** The Peres lattice successfully diagnoses key features of driven systems, including detecting resonances, monitoring the onset of chaos, and characterizing critical points related to Floquet modes.

**Method.** The analysis involves mapping the time-periodic evolution onto an extended, effective stationary Hamiltonian whose eigenstates (Floquet modes) are then analyzed using the Peres lattice structure.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on periodically driven quantum systems, exemplified by a Lipkin–Meshkov–Glick type model involving collective spin operators ($\hat{J}_x, \hat{J}_z$). The dynamics are governed by a time-dependent Hamiltonian $\hat{H}(t) = \hat{H}_0 + \eta g(t) \hat{H}'$.

**Key observables.** Peres lattice coordinates (expectation values of observables), Floquet quasienergies, and the similarity measure $N$ comparing driven vs. stationary dynamics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Driving strength ($\eta$), time period ($T$), system size parameter ($j$), and critical coupling constants ($\kappa_c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximating the full Floquet evolution operator using truncated expansions (e.g., BCH or Dyson series) to construct effective stationary Hamiltonians.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate Peres lattices for both stationary and kicked systems, showing structural changes corresponding to resonances and chaos onset; they also compare Husimi distributions derived from different driving forms.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the applicability of Peres lattices to driven systems, then analyzing quantum phase transitions in the stationary limit, followed by detailed investigations into resonance detection, chaos growth, and comparisons between various continuous and kicked driving protocols using approximation techniques.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We demonstrate the strength of the method of Peres lattices in periodically driven quantum systems. The method, which has previously been used mostly in stationary systems, enables us to efficiently detect resonances in the driven system, to monitor the onset of chaos, and to recognize critical properties of the Floquet modes. It also allows quick comparisons of the spectra of Floquet modes for various driving Hamiltonians and transparent tests of the iterative approximation techniques based on effective stationary Hamiltonians.

</details>

**Summary.** The research demonstrates a novel method to coherently control nuclear spins in silicon quantum dots by leveraging the internal spin-orbit coupling (SOC). By engineering specific pulse sequences that induce dynamic nuclear polarization, the nuclear spins can be phase-locked to this internal electronic field. This breakthrough opens pathways for building solid-state quantum memories with enhanced controllability.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete demonstration of using intrinsic material couplings (SOC) as an internal clock or reference field for quantum memory elements, bypassing the need for external microwave infrastructure.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To achieve coherent control over nuclear spin ensembles without relying on external phase references, which is crucial for scalable quantum memory.

**Main result.** The internal electronic spin-orbit coupling (SOC) can be utilized to deterministically phase-lock the nuclear spins, enabling measurement of their coherence and precession using only internal couplings.

**Method.** The study combines experimental measurements on silicon quantum dots with detailed numerical simulations of the electron-nuclear many-body system dynamics.

**Model / system.** The platform is a double quantum dot in silicon hosting coupled electronic spins (S-T transitions) and nuclear spins ($	ext{}^{29}	ext{Si}$). The physics involves coupling between the electron spin, the SOC term ($u$), and the hyperfine interaction ($\Delta HF$).

**Key observables.** Coherent precession of nuclear spins, inhomogeneous dephasing time ($T_2^*$), and the electronic transition probability $\langle P_T angle$ as a function of wait times ($t_w$) or evolution time ($t$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** External magnetic fields ($	ext{B}_{	ext{ext}}$), SOC strength ($u$), hyperfine coupling ($\Delta HF$), and the ratio between these couplings.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis sometimes treats nuclei as classical spins for Hamiltonian derivation, and simulations account for various noise sources (e.g., white magnetic noise).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how SOC modifies polarization regions; subsequent figures show time evolution of $\langle P_T angle$ vs. $t_w$, demonstrating the stroboscopic screening effect when $t_w$ matches the Larmor period, and finally plot coherence decay envelopes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for internal control to detailing the mechanism (SOC-induced phase locking via screening), quantifying this control by measuring free precession and coherence times, and concluding with a comprehensive analysis of parameter dependence ($u$ vs $\Delta HF$).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Because they have such long coherence times, nuclear spins have extraordinary potential for use in quantum information processing devices. However, coherent nuclear spin control generally requires external phase references, such as microwave control fields. Here, we phase-lock a $^{29}$Si nuclear spin ensemble in a silicon quantum dot using only the internal electronic spin-orbit coupling as a phase reference. When driven with the quantum-dot electrons, the nuclear spins align themselves to a phase determined by the electronic spin-orbit coupling and the timing of the drive protocol. This enables us to measure the coherent precession and inhomogeneous dephasing of the nuclear spins. We corroborate our results with detailed numerical simulations of the many-body electron nuclear system. Our work opens new routes for coherently controlling solid-state nuclear spin ensembles.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces QMaxCal, a novel path-space regularization technique for open quantum control derived from Girsanov's theorem applied to quantum trajectories. It yields two regularizers ($	ext{KL}_W$ and $	ext{RDV}$) that guide the system away from decoherence effects by penalizing observable deviations in the noise process. These methods significantly boost final-state fidelity across diverse multi-qubit benchmarks compared to existing optimization techniques.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, mathematically rigorous framework for optimizing quantum controls in noisy environments by penalizing the *consequences* of noise on the trajectory distribution rather than just the control pulse amplitude itself. The computational advantage of using trajectory space over density matrices is also notable.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing reliable quantum control policies for open quantum systems that can effectively combat decoherence induced by environmental noise.

**Main result.** The proposed path-space regularizers significantly outperform unregularized gradient-based and reinforcement learning baselines, achieving up to 50% reduction in infidelity across various multi-qubit benchmarks.

**Method.** The method uses Girsanov's theorem applied to quantum trajectories (stochastic Schrödinger equation) to derive closed-form, differentiable estimators of the KL divergence between path measures. This forms the basis for two novel regularizers: Wiener KL ($	ext{KL}_W$) and Drift-Variance ($	ext{RDV}$).

**Model / system.** The systems are open quantum systems modeled by Lindblad master equations, simulated via stochastic Schrödinger equations (SSE) using diffusive unravellings. Benchmarks include single/multi-qubit chains, STIRAP processes, and the IBM Kingston processor.

**Key observables.** Final-state fidelity ($F$), time-integrated population variance ($\int 	ext{Var}[P_k(t)] dt$), occupation of lossy states, and path KL divergence terms.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Decoherence rates ($\kappa, \gamma$), noise mismatch factors (e.g., $2.5	imes$ mismatch), and regularization weights ($\lambda_{	ext{KLW}}, \lambda_{	ext{RDV}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the diffusive unravelling of the SSE, which is mathematically tractable via Girsanov's theorem; it does not directly apply to jump unravellings or non-Markovian environments.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare trajectory population variance and fidelity across different regularization schemes (Wiener KL vs. baseline) for tasks like $|+angle 	o |Yangle$ and STIRAP, demonstrating suppression of lossy state occupation.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the theoretical foundation using Girsanov's theorem to derive path-space regularizers ($	ext{KL}_W$, $	ext{RDV}$), then applies these methods sequentially across multiple physical benchmarks (qubit tasks, STIRAP, superconducting chains) while comparing performance against standard control optimization techniques.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Reliable quantum control in the presence of decoherence requires policies that combat the effect of environmental noise on the controlled dynamics. Open quantum systems under continuous monitoring generate classical measurement records whose drift depends on the noise experienced by the system; the records of two evolutions sharing the same decoherence channels differ only in this drift, so Girsanov's theorem yields a closed-form, differentiable estimator of the KL divergence between their trajectory distributions. We instantiate this estimator with two physically motivated reference measures, yielding two regularizers that both drive the system toward states where the effects of decoherence are minimal: the Wiener KL (KL_W), which is empirically more effective under certain conditions on the noise model, and the drift-variance regularizer (R_DV), which works for all noise models. Both are qualitatively distinct from existing penalties on control fluence or smoothness: they penalize the observable consequences of control on the decoherence channels rather than the control amplitude itself. The regularizers outperform unregularized gradient-based and reinforcement-learning baselines across a range of open quantum systems -- including single- and multi-qubit benchmarks and a multi-qubit chain calibrated to a published snapshot of the IBM Kingston processor -- along several axes of evaluation: final-state fidelity, robustness to mismatch in the assumed noise model (gains grow from +17 pp at training noise to +27 pp under 2.5x noise mismatch), and occupation of forbidden states. The regularizers reduce infidelity by up to 50%, with ~16% gains on the calibrated IBM Kingston chain.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a variational polaron theory to accurately calculate the ground states of systems strongly coupled between matter excitations and bosonic fields. By using an optimized, state-dependent transformation, it overcomes limitations faced by perturbative methods in the ultrastrong coupling regime. The framework is rigorously tested on both light-matter (Dicke) and electron-phonon (Holstein) models with high quantitative accuracy.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses nonperturbative quantum dynamics in cavity QED and molecular physics, providing a robust tool for calculating dressed ground states where standard perturbation theory fails.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a reliable ground-state theory for systems exhibiting strong coupling between matter degrees of freedom (like electrons or atoms) and bosonic modes (light or phonons).

**Main result.** The variational polaron framework provides an asymptotically correct, nonperturbative method that accurately models dressing effects in both weak and ultrastrong coupling regimes.

**Method.** It employs a state-dependent polaron transformation followed by variational optimization of the resulting product-state ansatz, with residual correlations treated via second-order perturbation theory.

**Model / system.** The theory is applied to strongly coupled light-matter systems (Dicke model) and electron-phonon systems (Holstein model), where the Hamiltonian includes matter ($\hat{H}_M$), bosonic ($\hat{H}_B$), and coupling ($\hat{H}_{MB}$) terms.

**Key observables.** Ground-state energies, fidelities, and superradiant transition characteristics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling strength ratio ($g/\omega$) is critical, testing regimes from weak to ultrastrong coupling.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The zeroth-order state is a dressed reference frame derived from the polaron transformation; residual correlations beyond this product state are treated perturbatively.

**Figures summary.** Figures benchmark the theory on the Dicke model (showing superradiant transition and error reduction) and the Holstein model (comparing energy vs. symmetry constraints).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the variational polaron framework, details the transformation and optimization procedure, and validates its accuracy by benchmarking against established models like the Dicke and Holstein Hamiltonians.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Strong light-matter and electron-phonon coupling generate ground states dressed by virtual bosonic excitations, making bare-state truncations and perturbative treatments unreliable in the ultrastrong-coupling regime. We introduce a nonperturbative variational ground-state framework based on a state-dependent polaron transformation, combined with a product-state ansatz and a second-order perturbative correction for residual matter-boson entanglement. We show that the optimized transformed frame becomes asymptotically decoupled at infinite coupling, because the leading linear coupling is canceled while off-diagonal matter transitions are suppressed by displaced-oscillator overlaps. The approach is asymptotically correct in both weak- and strong-coupling limits and remains accurate in the intermediate regime, where fixed polaron transformations are least reliable. Dicke-model benchmarks reproduce ground-state energies, fidelities, and the superradiant transition, with second-order energy errors below 0.2%. Holstein-model benchmarks yield errors below 0.5% and clarify how translational symmetry affects wave-function quality. This dressed-basis framework enables nonperturbative modeling of strongly coupled light-matter and electron-phonon systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously benchmarks ground state preparation algorithms (cooling, adiabatic, QAOA) for fermionic systems subject to environmental noise. It establishes that the optimal protocol is highly dependent on the system's underlying quantum phase, demonstrating superior robustness for cooling protocols in topologically non-trivial regimes compared to other methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, quantitative comparison of state preparation techniques under realistic noise models, directly informing the resource estimation for fault-tolerant quantum computation in condensed matter contexts.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper benchmarks various quantum algorithms—cooling, adiabatic evolution, and QAOA—to determine the most efficient method for preparing a ground state of a fermionic system when subjected to environmental noise.

**Main result.** The optimal algorithm depends critically on the phase transition of the Hamiltonian: cooling protocols are superior in the topological phase, while adiabatic methods show advantages in the trivial phase. Furthermore, cooling is shown to be more robust against parameter imperfections than QAOA.

**Method.** The analysis combines analytical derivations of scaling laws for achievable relative energy with numerical simulations across different noise regimes and system parameters.

**Model / system.** The model uses an exactly solvable family of quadratic fermionic Hamiltonians, $H_S(	heta)$, which exhibits a quantum phase transition between trivial ($	heta > \pi/4$) and topological ($	heta < \pi/4$) phases. Noise is modeled using depolarizing noise.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the achievable relative energy ($e = (E - E_{GS})/E_{GS}$), analyzed as a function of the noise rate $\kappa$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise rate ($\kappa$), Hamiltonian parameter ($	heta$) defining the phase, and algorithm-specific parameters like evolution time ($T$) or cooling cycles.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations such as weak coupling limits, low-noise regimes, and specific initial state assumptions for adiabatic/QAOA protocols.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the relative energy differences between algorithms (e.g., $e_{	ext{cool}} - e_{	ext{QAOA}}$) across varying noise levels ($\kappa$) and system phases ($	heta$), illustrating crossovers in performance dominance.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares three protocols—adiabatic, QAOA, and cooling—by deriving scaling laws for the relative energy $e(\kappa)$ in both trivial and topological regimes. It then analyzes the trade-offs between non-adiabatic excitations (time dependence) and noise accumulation ($\kappa$ dependence).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We compare the performance of representative cooling, adiabatic, and optimization algorithms for ground-state preparation in the presence of noise. Using an exactly solvable family of quadratic fermionic Hamiltonians subject to depolarizing noise, we derive the scaling of the achievable relative energy as a function of the noise rate and support these results with numerical simulations. The Hamiltonian exhibits two phases, separated by a quantum phase transition. As expected, the performance of the different algorithms depends on the phase: adiabatic evolution is favorable in the trivial phase, while a multi-frequency cooling algorithm, as proposed in [1], becomes competitive or superior in the topological phase, where gap-closing limits adiabatic protocols. We further present numerical results for the quantum approximate optimization algorithm [2], showing that it performs competitively with cooling in the trivial phase but is typically outperformed in the topological regime. Finally, we show that for this model the cooling protocol exhibits enhanced robustness to parameter imperfections, highlighting its potential advantage for realistic implementations of noisy quantum state preparation.   The analytical approach developed here, in conjunction with numerical validation, establishes an extendable approach to benchmarking ground-state preparation algorithms.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a novel quasiprobability measure to give physical meaning to Liouvillian eigenmodes in open quantum systems. By interpreting these modes as coherence distributions, the authors successfully generalize semiclassical quantization concepts, showing that quantized orbital structures persist even under damping from a thermal bath.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful theoretical tool—the quasiprobability measure—to bridge semiclassical quantization concepts (like invariant tori) from conservative Hamiltonian systems into the complex realm of open quantum dynamics governed by dissipation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is interpreting the physical meaning of Liouvillian eigenmodes for open quantum systems, where standard energy eigenstates are insufficient due to time-decaying right and left eigenstates.

**Main result.** The authors introduce a quasiprobability measure that successfully interprets an eigenmode as a coherence distribution, demonstrating that semiclassical quantization concepts (like quantized orbits) can be extended to dissipative open systems.

**Method.** They employ a physically motivated quasiprobability measure combining right and left eigenstates, mapping the dynamics onto a doubled phase space structure. This allows for analyzing spectral properties via generalized Husimi functions.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on open quantum systems governed by Lindblad master equations ($rac{d\hat{ho}}{dt} = L\hat{ho}$), specifically modeling damped harmonic oscillators and quadratic Liouvillians. The analysis uses Fock basis representations and superoperators.

**Key observables.** Coherence measure ($C_p$), return probability, Super Husimi function $C_{n_K, n_B}(\alpha_K, \alpha_B)$, and the persistence of quantized 'orbits' in phase space.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Damping rates ($\gamma$), thermal bath temperature (related to $eta$), and relative phases ($\phi$) between coherent components.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dynamics are assumed to be CPTP (Completely Positive Trace-Preserving) and Markovian. For quantization results, the damping is often assumed isotropic in phase space.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate quantized 'orbits' for damped oscillators, show the Liouville spectrum revealing coherence between Fock states, and map out sections of the super Husimi function in doubled phase space, demonstrating peak locations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the need for a new measure (quasiprobability) to interpret open system dynamics. It then applies this framework to simple models like the damped oscillator, deriving explicit formulas and asymptotic results for coherence distributions and return amplitudes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Unlike in closed quantum systems where individual energy eigenstates are understood as physical excitations, open quantum systems have distinct right and left eigenstates of the Liouvillian that decay with time and are difficult to interpret. Here we introduce a physically motivated quasiprobability measure combining the two types of eigenstates that interprets a Liouville eigenmode as a set of coherences. This coherence measure is intimately connected to the return probability and allows one to visualize the modes as quasiprobability distributions in a "doubled" phase space. Using this measure we show that, remarkably, an oscillator retains its quantized "orbits" in phase space for a large class of linear and nonlinear damping, thus providing a formulation of semiclassical quantization for open systems. The orbits have measurable dynamical signatures and are broadened in the presence of a thermal bath, similar to energy levels. For quadratic systems, our results yield an extension of the concept of invariant tori, which play a central role in Hamiltonian systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops sophisticated time-dependent control protocols using Shortcuts to Adiabaticity techniques to manipulate dipolar Bose-Einstein condensates across their superfluid-to-supersolid phase transition. By employing variational and direct optimization methods, the authors design smooth changes in the scattering length that minimize excitations and maintain high fidelity throughout the process.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work represents an advanced application of optimal control theory to highly interacting quantum many-body systems. The ability to precisely guide a system through a quantum phase transition while minimizing excitations is crucial for realizing novel quantum states in cold atom experiments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is designing smooth time-dependent control protocols for dipolar Bose-Einstein condensates (BECs) to manage the transition between superfluid and supersolid phases while minimizing excitations.

**Main result.** Variational approaches combined with direct numerical optimization successfully design control profiles, such as $a^{(
u)}_s(t)$, that achieve high fidelity ($F_{\min} \gtrsim 0.95$) and suppress unwanted probability currents during the phase transition.

**Method.** The study employs Shortcuts to Adiabaticity (STA) techniques, utilizing a variational approach with inverse engineering (VAIE) for superfluid regimes and direct optimization protocols for the challenging superfluid-supersolid transition.

**Model / system.** The system is dipolar BECs, modeled by an extended Gross-Pitaevskii equation (eGPE). The Hamiltonian includes kinetic energy, external potential, long-range anisotropic dipole-dipole interaction (DDI), and tunable contact interactions. Control is exerted via the time-dependent s-wave scattering length, $a_s(t)$.

**Key observables.** Time-dependent target-overlap fidelity ($F_T(t)$) and its minimum value ($F_{\min}$); probability density current ($\mathbf{j}_x(x, t)$); Inverse Participation Ratio (IPR).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical scattering length $a_c$ that separates the superfluid and supersolid phases; the control time duration ($t_f$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The variational ansatz used in the VAIE method is assumed to be separable, which limits its accuracy near the phase transition. Spherical truncation of the dipole interaction was also considered.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare current density snapshots ($\mathbf{j}_x$) showing flow suppression by optimized schemes versus oscillations from polynomial schemes; fidelity plots show $F_{\min}$ comparisons across various control protocols.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the eGPE model, details the use of STA techniques, applies a variational approach for superfluid evolution, and then uses direct optimization to design robust controls specifically for crossing the superfluid-supersolid phase boundary.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider protocols for control of dipolar Bose-Einstein condensates where the critical role is played by the long-range anisotropic interatomic magnetic dipole-dipole interaction. The phase diagram of such a condensate has been explored theoretically and experimentally with certain values of the interatomic scattering length corresponding to superfluid and supersolid phases, where supersolidity appears as a modulation in the ground state density. Preparation of this modulated ground state is challenging, since excitations appear as a result of a finite-time evolution required to produce qualitative changes in the wavefunction density. To solve this problem we consider the time-dependent control of a dipolar Bose-Einstein condensate using shortcuts to adiabaticity techniques, concentrating on design of the time-dependent scattering length, a parameter of the system easily tunable by contemporary experiments. The first technique is the variational approach based on the Euler-Lagrange equations for a separable ansatz describing the evolution of the superfluid state. Secondly, we study the transition from superfluid to supersolid using a direct optimization protocol. We discuss the fidelity of the developed protocols in terms of the evolution time.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper demonstrates that the Non-Hermitian Skin Effect can significantly enhance superconducting pairing correlations in moiré Hubbard systems. This enhancement occurs within a specific 'golden window' of non-reciprocity ($\gamma$), acting by selectively boosting on-site pairing while suppressing competing antiferromagnetic tendencies. The findings are robustly confirmed across multiple computational methods and suggest an experimentally measurable signature in driven quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects topological phenomena (NHSE) directly to strongly correlated physics (superconductivity), providing a mechanism where engineered boundary conditions can tune pairing interactions in quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Investigating whether the Non-Hermitian Skin Effect (NHSE) can enhance pairing correlations in Moiré Hubbard systems.

**Main result.** The NHSE causes a non-monotonic enhancement of pairing susceptibility, peaking in an intermediate 'golden window' of non-reciprocity ($\gamma$), which is confirmed to be a genuine many-body effect.

**Method.** Calculations utilize Exact Diagonalization (ED) and Non-Hermitian DMRG on the non-Hermitian Hubbard model. The results are cross-checked using BCS scaling estimates.

**Model / system.** The system is electrons hopping on a triangular moiré superlattice described by the non-Hermitian Hubbard model, incorporating nearest-neighbor hopping ($t$), on-site repulsion ($U$), and an engineered asymmetric hopping term ($\gamma$) creating non-reciprocity.

**Key observables.** Double occupancy $D(\gamma)$, pairing susceptibility $\chi_{SC}$ (decomposed into on-site/off-site), and the superconducting critical temperature $T_c(\gamma)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Non-reciprocity parameter $\gamma$, interaction strength $U$ relative to hopping $t$, and the intermediate 'golden window' regime ($\gamma \in [0.5, 1.2]t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on finite clusters (e.g., $3	imes3$) and does not claim long-range superconducting order; the enhancement is shown to be a finite-cluster effect.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the non-monotonic dome shape of $D(\gamma)$ in the golden window, the channel-selective decomposition of $\chi_{SC}$ showing enhanced on-site pairing and suppressed AFM correlations, and predicted dome-shaped $T_c(\gamma)$ fingerprints.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model (NHSE in moiré systems), calculates key observables like $D(\gamma)$ and $\chi_{SC}$ using ED/DMRG, identifies the non-monotonic 'golden window' as a many-body effect, and predicts testable experimental signatures for coherent vs. dissipative driving.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that the non-Hermitian skin effect (NHSE) can enhance pairing correlations in moiré Hubbard systems through a channel-selective mechanism: skin-induced localization amplifies the boundary density of states, strengthening local pairing tendencies within an intermediate ``golden window'' of non-reciprocity $γ\in[0.5,1.2]\,t$. Using exact diagonalization of the non-Hermitian Hubbard model on triangular lattices with open boundaries, we map the $(U,γ)$ phase diagram. The double occupancy $D(γ)$ exhibits non-monotonic behavior -- rising by up to 21\% then declining -- reflecting a competition between NHSE-enhanced boundary pairing and over-localization. A decomposition of the pairing susceptibility $χ_{\mathrm{SC}}$ on the $3\times3$ cluster reveals that the NHSE acts \emph{channel-selectively}: it enhances on-site pairing ($+21\%$) while simultaneously suppressing competing antiferromagnetic correlations (22\% reduction), so that the total pairing susceptibility, dominated by the on-site channel, grows by $+98\%$ on that cluster. These trends are corroborated by an independent non-Hermitian DMRG calculation and establish an enhancement of finite-cluster pairing correlations rather than trivial density redistribution. We do not claim long-range superconducting order. A BCS scaling estimate converts the same pairing-response signal into a dome-shaped $T_c(γ)$ fingerprint, suggesting an experimentally distinguishable response in coherent-drive versus reservoir-dominated moiré devices.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a novel computational framework to solve the multi-objective optimization problem of designing high-performance single-photon sources. By combining Liouville-space adjoint methods with simulated annealing, the authors can navigate conflicting design requirements for purity and brightness in open quantum systems. This methodology offers a general tool for engineering complex quantum optical devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a general, computationally rigorous recipe for inverse design in open quantum systems, which is crucial for engineering complex quantum devices like single-photon emitters where multiple physical constraints must be satisfied simultaneously.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is designing high-quality single-photon sources by simultaneously optimizing conflicting performance metrics like purity ($g^{(2)}(0)$) and brightness ($B$).

**Main result.** A computational framework combining Liouville-space adjoint methods with simulated annealing achieves a high success rate ($\approx 60\%$) for achieving low $g^{(2)}(0)$ while maintaining theoretically bounded brightness.

**Method.** The authors developed an optimization routine using the Liouville-space adjoint formulation to efficiently calculate parameter gradients, augmented by simulated annealing to escape local minima in the multi-objective design landscape.

**Model / system.** The study models single-photon sources using open quantum systems dynamics, specifically employing the Lindblad master equation applied to a cavity Quantum Electrodynamics (Cavity QED) setup described by the Jaynes-Cummings Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Second-order correlation function ($g^{(2)}(0)$), photon outcoupling rate/Brightness ($B$), and system parameters like coupling strength ($g$) and decay rates ($\kappa_C, \gamma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The optimization is performed over a multi-dimensional parameter space including cavity frequency ($\omega_C$), atomic transition frequency ($\omega$), coupling strength ($g$), etc.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Lindblad master equation framework and employs advanced mathematical techniques like adjoint methods to handle complex gradient calculations in open quantum systems.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate optimization landscapes showing conflicting gradients, successful two-stage transition trajectories (suppressing $g^{(2)}$ then recovering $B$), and the necessity of simulated annealing for high success probability.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the physical problem, details the mathematical machinery (Liouville-space adjoint formulation), presents the multi-objective optimization strategy (Jacobian descent + SA), and validates the method using the JC model to achieve quantitative design results.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

High-quality single-photon sources, realized through saturable emitters, photon blockade, or heralded pair generation, are indispensable building blocks for photonic quantum platforms. Although these mechanisms suppress multiphoton emission through distinct principles typically captured by analytical models, their practical implementation is constrained by conflicting requirements for purity, brightness, and indistinguishability, which must be balanced within high-dimensional design landscapes. Here, we propose a computational framework for optimizing competing metrics of single-photon sources. Building on a Liouville-space adjoint formulation that efficiently evaluates multiple objectives in Markovian open quantum systems, we develop a Jacobian-based update, which ensures first-order monotonic reduction of multi-objective costs. By incorporating simulated annealing to escape gradient-vanishing plateaus, our framework achieves a design success rate of nearly 60 % for photon blockade with g2(0) smaller than 0.1 and theoretically bounded brightness across a broad parameter space, without any analytical guidance. This framework provides a general recipe for multi-objective design of open quantum systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces QMCtwin, a quantum Monte Carlo simulation technique for solving the master equation of error-prone Quantum Error Correction (QEC) circuits. By simulating realistic superconducting noise sources, it demonstrates that standard Pauli noise models fail to capture crucial syndrome statistics. This provides a vital diagnostic tool for building more accurate decoders facing real-world hardware imperfections.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes beyond idealized noise models common in QEC theory by providing a rigorous computational tool to simulate complex, open-system dynamics that govern real hardware performance.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Standard quantum error correction (QEC) simulations often inaccurately model hardware noise by using simplified stochastic Pauli models, discarding crucial coherent phase or nonunital drift information.

**Main result.** The proposed QMCtwin framework successfully predicts syndrome-extraction biases and correlations that are entirely absent or strongly suppressed in conventional Pauli/Clifford noise descriptions.

**Method.** The authors developed QMCtwin, a sign-problem-suppressed quantum Monte Carlo method to simulate the full master-equation dynamics of QEC circuits, comparing these results against standard Pauli-twirled Clifford simulations.

**Model / system.** The study applies this framework to a distance-7 rotated surface code using 97 physical qubits, incorporating realistic superconducting noise mechanisms like relaxation, dephasing, and residual ZZ crosstalk into the master equation.

**Key observables.** Syndrome probabilities ($\Pr(m)$), syndrome-extraction biases ($\delta_k$), disagreement probability ($p_{
eq k}$), and mutual information quantifying information loss between ME and Pauli models.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Distance-7 surface code, 97 physical qubits, noise parameters ($T_1, T_\phi$, detuning $\Delta g/2\pi$), simulation time (300 ns).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The QMC estimator is assumed to be unbiased and ergodic; the Pauli model approximation for noise sources is explicitly noted as an approximation of the full master-equation evolution.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the stability of the QMC estimator diagnostics (trace/phase) over time, and spatial maps comparing syndrome biases ($\delta_k$) and disagreement probabilities ($p_{
eq k}$) between the realistic ME simulation and the Pauli-twirled Clifford model.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the limitations of current noise models. It then details the QMCtwin master-equation solver, applies it to a full syndrome extraction round on a surface code, and finally uses information-theoretic diagnostics (like KL divergence) to quantify the structural differences between the two simulation approaches.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

As quantum error correction moves toward large-scale experimental implementations, decoder performance increasingly depends on how faithfully hardware noise is translated into syndrome statistics. Standard stabilizer workflows achieve scalability by replacing device dynamics with stochastic Pauli or detector-error models, but this compression can discard coherent phase information, nonunital drift, continuous-time effects of always-on couplings, and correlations generated by simultaneous Hamiltonian and dissipative evolution. Here we present QMCtwin, a sign-problem-suppressed quantum Monte Carlo framework for master-equation simulation of QEC circuits, and apply it to a full syndrome-extraction round of a distance-$7$ rotated surface code with $97$ physical qubits. The open-system model includes realistic superconducting-device noise mechanisms such as relaxation, pure dephasing, coherent gate miscalibration, residual $ZZ$ crosstalk, and drive-qubit detuning. By directly estimating syndrome observables from the QMC-generated stochastic density matrix estimator, we compare the master-equation dynamics with their Pauli-twirled Clifford simulation counterparts. QMCtwin predicts syndrome-extraction biases and correlations between syndromes and proxies of logical-string-parity that are absent or strongly suppressed in the stochastic Pauli description. We introduce information-theoretic diagnostics that further quantify how information concerning syndromes versus string-parity proxies differs between the realistic master-equation simulation and the corresponding Pauli-twirled model. These results show that QMC-based master-equation digital twins can expose noise features hidden by conventional Pauli/Clifford noise models and provide a practical path toward more accurate decoder-facing syndrome models.

</details>

**Authors:** Taiqi Zhou, Weiyuan Gong  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.19486v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `quantum measurements` **4/5** · `measurement-induced transitions` **3/5** · `QC/QI experiment` **2/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **1/5**

**Summary.** The paper presents an optimal, control-free algorithm to reconstruct the unknown Hamiltonian of a quantum system from limited measurement data. By establishing matching upper and lower bounds, it proves that the required total experimental time scales as $O(\Lambda/\epsilon^2 \log (\Lambda/\epsilon))$. This result is crucial for advancing in situ characterization techniques on near-term quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, resource-optimal theoretical framework for characterizing quantum hardware parameters ($H$) using only basic, noise-resilient measurements available on current NISQ devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is learning an unknown, potentially complex Hamiltonian ($H$) governing a quantum system solely from time-evolution measurements accessible in near-term quantum hardware.

**Main result.** The authors derive the optimal scaling for total evolution time required for this task, establishing both an upper bound $O(rac{\Lambda}{\epsilon^2} \log rac{\Lambda}{\epsilon})$ and a matching lower bound $\Omega(rac{\Lambda}{\epsilon^2} \log rac{\Lambda}{\epsilon})$.

**Method.** The methodology involves developing sequential measurement protocols: first, using random time sampling (displacement sieve) to identify the support of $H$, followed by cross-Pauli measurements and parity sampling to estimate the coefficients.

**Model / system.** The system is an arbitrary quantum processor whose dynamics are governed by a Hamiltonian $H = \sum \lambda_u P_u$ in the Pauli basis. The focus is on implementing this learning process using only product state preparation and measurement (SPAM) access, mimicking near-term hardware constraints.

**Key observables.** Correlation functions derived from time evolution, specifically second derivatives of same-Pauli measurements ($C''_{P_v}(0)$) for support finding, and first derivatives of cross-Pauli measurements ($(F^{(c)}_{A,B})'(0)$) for coefficient estimation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** $\Lambda$ (Hamiltonian norm bound), $\epsilon$ (desired accuracy), $t_0$ (time resolution on a discrete grid).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol is analyzed under the assumption of local depolarization noise (SPAM errors) and relies heavily on mathematical tools like band-limited kernel sampling and summation rules for discretization.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes detail algorithmic steps involving sieves, random sampling, and complexity scaling comparisons.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from continuous time analysis (using displacement/cross-correlation measurements) to discrete time analysis (applying summation rules), culminating in establishing optimal lower bounds for the total required measurement time $T_{tot}$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Characterizing the features of a Hamiltonian that governs a quantum system serves as a fundamental subroutine of quantum device calibration, signal sensing, and error correction. Recent works proposed protocols have achieved the optimal Heisenberg-limited scaling learning ansatz-free Hamiltonians from their real-time evolutions without fully specifying interaction structures. However, these protocols rely on both deep circuits with interleaving probes and control, and extremely short time resolution, making them difficult to implement on near- and intermediate-term in situ quantum experiments. In this work, we propose a computationally efficient, control-free, and ancilla-free algorithm that uses only Pauli product state preparation and measurement, and learns an ansatz-free Hamiltonian $H$ with $||H||\leqΛ$ in total evolution time of $Θ(\fracΛ{ε^2}\log(\fracΛε))$. The evolution time cost of our algorithm is optimal for any control-free protocols as we further prove a lower bound of $Ω(\fracΛ{ε^2}\log(\fracΛε))$. Technically, our method introduces a randomized-sampling framework that combines band-limited kernel-based time sampling with a displacement sieve for Hamiltonian structure learning. The characteristic probe time resolution depends only on $Λ$ instead of $\varepsilon$, which makes our protocol especially appealing in the high-precision regime for sensing and calibration applications. We also show that the algorithm maintains the same asymptotic total evolution time in the presence of state-preparation-and-measurement (SPAM) noise when the Hamiltonian is local after calibration. Our results demonstrate the fundamental cost of experimentally friendly Hamiltonian learning and provide a practical route to rigorous in situ characterization of near-term quantum platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** This work models financial market dynamics by treating them through the lens of statistical mechanics, drawing parallels to lattice gas models near criticality. It quantifies how future volatilities and correlations depend non-linearly on current trends using regression techniques. The findings suggest that market risk can be significantly improved by incorporating these trend-dependent effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a direct, quantitative mapping of complex financial time series behavior—such as volatility clustering and trend dependence—onto established concepts from statistical physics, like critical phenomena and phase transitions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop a statistical-mechanical framework to model financial market dynamics by analyzing trends, volatility, and correlations.

**Main result.** It finds that variance and correlation exhibit predictable non-linear dependencies on current market trends ($\phi_t$), suggesting the system behaves near a critical point.

**Method.** The analysis uses nonlinear regression techniques (polynomial fitting) applied to historical financial return data, drawing analogies with lattice gas models near criticality.

**Model / system.** The model treats financial markets as analogous to a lattice gas or an Ising-like system near its critical point. The dynamics are governed by potentials involving trend strength ($\phi$) and variance ($\sigma^2$), leading to equations for $\langle 	ext{r}^2_{t+1} angle$ and $ho_{t+1}$.

**Key observables.** Next-day volatility ($\langle 	ext{r}^2_{t+1} angle$), next-week correlation ($ho_{t+1}$), trend strength ($\phi_t$), skewness, and kurtosis.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Trend horizon ($T$), coefficients from polynomial fits (e.g., $f>0$ for variance growth, $g<1$ for mean reversion).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The market dynamics can be effectively described by potentials derived from statistical physics models, such as the lattice gas model near criticality.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how expected return and variance change parabolically with trend strength ($\phi_t$), and contour plots show variance changes based on combinations of current variance and trend strength.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from modeling returns (cubic polynomial) to modeling variance (quadratic polynomial in $\sigma^2_t$ and $\phi_t$) and finally to modeling correlations, culminating in the analogy with a lattice gas near its critical point.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We forecast future volatilities and correlations of financial markets based on the current trends in these markets. This complements previous work that models future expected returns by a cubic polynomial of the current trend strength. Empirically, we observe that volatilities and correlations tend to increase day after day in times of strong up- or down-trends. This effect is particularly pronounced in down-trends. It can be accurately quantified by quadratic polynomials of today's trend strengths, which refine common mean-reversion models of volatilities and correlations. Our results improve the prediction of market risk by accounting for market trends. They also support a recent proposal to model financial markets by a lattice gas near its critical point.

</details>

**Summary.** The research investigates weak surface magnetism in $\mathrm{RuO_2}$ by studying its interaction with monolayer $\mathrm{WSe_2}$ using magneto-optical spectroscopy. Anomalous energy shifts and unique field responses observed below 55 K strongly suggest that the valley states are governed by interfacial magnetic exchange fields. This establishes a powerful, non-invasive optical tool for characterizing subtle magnetism in challenging material systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an optical method to probe weak magnetic interactions at interfaces without requiring the deposition of an additional ferromagnetic layer, which is highly relevant for understanding correlated electron systems in 2D heterostructures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to probe weak interfacial magnetic states at the surface of $\mathrm{RuO_2}$ using optical techniques, circumventing the controversy surrounding its bulk magnetic ground state.

**Main result.** Anomalous magneto-optical responses, including temperature-dependent energy shifts and unusual valley splitting behavior in $\mathrm{WSe_2}$, were observed, suggesting that interfacial exchange fields govern the electronic structure via weak surface magnetism.

**Method.** Temperature-dependent magneto-optical spectroscopy (magneto-photoluminescence) was performed on a van der Waals heterostructure ($\mathrm{WSe_2}$ atop $\mathrm{RuO_2}$) compared to an encapsulated control sample, using applied out-of-plane magnetic fields.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the $\mathrm{WSe_2/RuO_2}$ van der Waals heterostructure. The investigation focuses on emergent surface magnetism in $\mathrm{RuO_2}$ and its influence on the excitonic states of monolayer $\mathrm{WSe_2}$.

**Key observables.** Anomalous excitonic energy shift, deviation from Varshni behavior below 55 K, and field-dependent valley splitting ($\sigma^+$ vs. $\sigma^-$ polarization measurements).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T < 55$ K), applied magnetic field (up to $\pm 6 	ext{ T}$ out-of-plane).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The anomalous optical response is attributed to interfacial exchange fields associated with weak surface magnetic states in $\mathrm{RuO_2}$, rather than conventional Zeeman splitting or non-magnetic effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the temperature dependence of the excitonic peak energy, showing a deviation from Varshni behavior only in the $\mathrm{WSe_2/RuO_2}$ sample below $T_1$. They also show polarization-resolved measurements demonstrating negligible Zeeman splitting in the heterostructure compared to the control.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem of probing weak surface magnetism, details the use of magneto-optical spectroscopy on the $\mathrm{WSe_2/RuO_2}$ system, presents evidence for anomalous temperature and field dependencies (deviation from Varshni behavior), and concludes by positioning this technique as a tool for studying emergent interfacial magnetism.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Ruthenium dioxide ($\mathrm{RuO_2}$) has been proposed as an altermagnetic candidate, although its magnetic ground state remains controversial. Here, we probe weak interfacial magnetic states at the surface of (001)-oriented $\mathrm{RuO_2}$ films using the magnetic proximity effect (MPE) in a van der Waals heterostructure consisting of monolayer tungsten diselenide ($\mathrm{WSe_2}$) atop $\mathrm{RuO_2}$. Temperature-dependent magneto-optical spectroscopy reveals an anomalous excitonic energy shift and a deviation from conventional Varshni behavior below 55 K that are absent in an encapsulated $\mathrm{WSe_2}$ control sample. The anomalous shift reverses sign upon field cooling with opposite magnetic field polarity, indicating a magnetic origin. Polarization-resolved measurements further show a nearly field-independent and fluctuating valley splitting in $\mathrm{WSe_2 / RuO_2}$ in strong contrast to the conventional linear Zeeman splitting observed in the control bare $\mathrm{WSe_2}$ sample. These results suggest that the valley states are governed predominantly by interfacial exchange fields associated with weak surface magnetic states in $\mathrm{RuO_2}$, which do not produce a conventional linear Zeeman response within the applied magnetic field range. Importantly, this approach enables direct optical probing of emergent surface magnetism without introducing an additional ferromagnetic layer, positioning MPE-based optical probing as a tool for investigating weak surface magnetism and offering new possibilities for studying magnetic materials with controversial magnetic states.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops comprehensive protocols to distinguish between genuinely nonlocal sets of orthogonal product states in multipartite quantum systems. By classifying these sets into Type I and Type II based on local reducibility, the authors determine the minimal entanglement resources required for perfect discrimination. The results provide a general framework applicable from bipartite up to $n$-partite systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical framework for quantifying the minimum quantum resources needed to verify fundamental properties like genuine nonlocality, which is crucial for quantum cryptography and foundational tests of quantum mechanics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of perfectly discriminating between sets of quantum states that exhibit genuine nonlocality in multipartite systems.

**Main result.** It establishes robust, resource-efficient protocols showing that discrimination requires minimal entanglement resources, such as a single EPR pair or GHZ state, depending on the type and dimensionality of the nonlocal set.

**Method.** The core method involves constructing multi-step measurement sequences using local operations and classical communication (LOCC), supplemented by shared ancillary entangled states to overcome inherent nonlocality barriers.

**Model / system.** The system consists of multipartite orthogonal product quantum states defined over tensor products of qudit spaces ($\mathbb{C}^{d_1} \otimes \cdots \otimes \mathbb{C}^{d_n}$). The sets are classified as Type I (reducible) or Type II (irreducible) based on local indistinguishability.

**Key observables.** The outcomes of sequential local projective measurements ($M_k$) performed by different subsystems (Alice, Bob, Charlie) serve as the primary diagnostics for state identification.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The required entanglement resources are key parameters, including single EPR pairs ($\vert\phi^+(2)angle$), GHZ states ($\vert G(3)angle$), and maximally entangled states in higher-dimensional systems.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocols assume the existence of specific maximally entangled resource states and that the sets being discriminated are indeed genuinely nonlocal but locally distinguishable given sufficient entanglement assistance.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining genuine nonlocality, then presenting specialized discrimination schemes for low-dimensional bipartite systems (Type I/II), generalizing these protocols to tripartite and $n$-partite systems using increasing levels of shared entanglement resources.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Genuine nonlocality arises when a set of multipartite orthogonal states is locally indistinguishable under any bipartition of the subsystems. The entanglement-assisted discrimination of such genuinely nonlocal orthogonal product sets has attracted significant attention in quantum information. Based on the criterion of local irreducibility, genuine nonlocality is classified into Type I (reducible) and Type II (irreducible). We present entanglement-assisted discrimination schemes for both types of genuinely nonlocal sets that use minimal resources. For low-dimensional cases, Type I sets require only a single EPR pair, whereas Type II sets necessitate only one GHZ state. We extend these protocols to higher-dimensional systems: the discrimination of Type I sets requires only one maximally entangled state in a two-qutrit system, while that of Type II sets similarly demands a single maximally entangled state in a three-qutrit system. For $n$-partite ($n > 3$) systems, Type I sets continue to require only one maximally entangled state, whereas Type II sets necessitate just one additional EPR pair compared to their Type I counterparts. These results provide a robust framework for the efficient discrimination of genuinely nonlocal sets using minimal quantum resources.

</details>

**Summary.** The research reveals a fundamental vulnerability in high-dimensional entanglement detection: small, systematic measurement errors allow an attacker to trick verification protocols. By perturbing measurements on photonic spatial modes, the authors successfully demonstrated faking strong entanglement signals from simple product states. This mandates developing new, robust verification techniques resilient to bounded experimental discrepancies.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly impacts the trustworthiness of quantum hardware characterization protocols, suggesting that device imperfections can be exploited to mislead users into believing a system possesses valuable entanglement resources.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper demonstrates that arbitrarily small, adversarially encoded measurement errors can lead to the false certification of high-dimensional entanglement in systems that are fundamentally separable.

**Main result.** Even with minor imperfections (e.g., 0.7% error), current entanglement verification methods fail spectacularly by falsely certifying significant levels of entanglement ($K$) for product states, especially as system dimensionality ($d$) increases.

**Method.** The authors analyze the violation of an entanglement witness ($W_d$), showing that introducing $\epsilon$-perturbations to the measurement bases allows separable states to mimic the expected correlations of entangled states.

**Model / system.** The study uses high-dimensional bipartite quantum states, experimentally realized using classical photonic states encoded in the spatial degree of freedom (macro-pixels). The theoretical framework relies on entanglement witnesses tested against product states.

**Key observables.** Entanglement witness value ($W_d$), falsely certified Schmidt number ($K$), and the measurement error parameter ($\epsilon$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System dimension ($d$) up to 61, small perturbation $\epsilon$ (as low as 0.7%), and crosstalk ratios.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption being challenged is that quantum measurements can be realized exactly; the analysis explores models based on average or worst-case fidelity approximations for measurement errors.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how the faked entanglement ($K$) scales with both the error ($\epsilon$) and the dimension ($d$), showing a rapid increase in vulnerability as $d$ grows.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops theoretical models comparing different fidelity measures (average vs. worst-case) to quantify the attack, followed by detailed experimental implementation using photonic spatial modes to demonstrate the faking effect across increasing dimensions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum entanglement is a central resource underpinning emerging quantum technologies, enabling capabilities beyond those of classical systems. Accurate verification of entanglement is therefore crucial. However, experimental schemes usually rely on the assumption that quantum measurements can be realized exactly. As the complexity of a quantum system grows, this assumption typically becomes increasingly unrealistic, therefore leading to a widening mismatch between theoretical models and experimental implementations. Here we demonstrate that arbitrarily small measurement errors, when adversarially encoded in the measurement apparatus, can lead to the false certification of high-dimensional entanglement in systems that are, in fact, separable. This is achieved by introducing explicit hacking attacks to measurement devices in well-established entanglement verification tests. We further experimentally demonstrate this effect using classical photonic states encoded in the spatial degree of freedom, spanning up to 61 dimensions with measurement fidelity errors as low as 0.23%. Our results uncover a fundamental vulnerability in current methods for high-dimensional entanglement detection, highlighting the susceptibility of complex quantum devices to small adversarial perturbations. The findings underscore the need for developing secure verification of quantum information that is robust to bounded discrepancies between theory and experiment.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a symmetry-based error mitigation protocol designed to counteract the symmetry-breaking errors inherent in Trotterized quantum simulations. By averaging measurement results over symmetry transformations—either at the initial state or between simulation layers—the method effectively filters out unphysical dynamics while preserving the true, ideal time evolution. This offers a powerful, depth-preserving tool for simulating complex condensed matter and gauge theories on current quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This is highly relevant as it provides a systematic, hardware-agnostic way to correct for fundamental algorithmic errors in quantum simulation that plague near-term devices, particularly when simulating lattice gauge theories or systems with known symmetries.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Trotterization, used for quantum simulation, inherently breaks the symmetries of the target Hamiltonian, leading to unphysical algorithmic errors in simulating complex many-body systems.

**Main result.** A symmetry-based post-processing protocol can systematically project out these symmetry-violating Trotter errors by averaging measurement outcomes over symmetry transformations. This provides a depth-preserving method to substantially improve simulation fidelity.

**Method.** The core technique involves classical post-processing: either preparing the initial state in a symmetrically transformed basis or interleaving symmetry operations between discrete Trotter layers, followed by ensemble averaging of measurements.

**Model / system.** The work applies to general quantum many-body systems, benchmarking on the one-dimensional XY model (spatial reflection symmetry) and the 1D Schwinger model (local gauge invariance). The underlying physics involves Hamiltonians with known symmetries that constrain the accessible Hilbert space.

**Key observables.** Expectation values of physical observables $\langle O(t) angle$, specifically monitoring for violations of spatial reflection or local Gauss's law.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The error scaling is compared between naive Trotterization (e.g., $O(t^{1.9})$) and the symmetry-mitigated protocol ($O(t^{4.4})$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the observable respects the underlying symmetries, allowing for projection onto symmetric subspaces; it is also shown to handle non-local or anti-unitary symmetries.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare naive Trotter error scaling versus symmetry-mitigated error scaling for the XY model and illustrate the circuit structure involving gauge transformations interleaved between Trotter layers for the Schwinger model.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem of symmetry breaking in Trotterization, then details the post-processing protocol (initial state vs. interleaving), and finally benchmarks this method on specific models like the XY and Schwinger models to demonstrate error suppression.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum simulation is a powerful tool for exploring complex quantum many-body systems such as condensed matter physics and gauge theories. Trotterization, which approximates the ideal time evolution operator by decomposing it into a sequence of local gate operations, is one of the most widely used quantum simulation algorithms. However, such Trotterized implementations generally fail to preserve the symmetries of the target Hamiltonian during compilation. As a result, they can drive quantum states out of symmetrically allowed subspaces, leading to unphysical dynamics and symmetry-violating algorithmic errors. In this work, we propose a symmetry-based Trotter error mitigation protocol using classical post-processing. By applying symmetry transformations to the initial state or interleaving them between discrete Trotter layers, and then averaging an ensemble of the resulting measurement outcomes via classical post-processing, our method systematically projects out the symmetry-violating components of the Trotter error while leaving the ideal dynamics unchanged. Importantly, this framework naturally accommodates non-local spatial symmetries and anti-unitary operations such as time reversal, which are difficult or impossible to implement directly with hardware-native quantum gates. We benchmark our protocol on the one-dimensional XY model and the one-dimensional Schwinger model. In the XY model, enforcing reflection symmetry suppresses the leading-order Trotter error, whereas in the Schwinger model, interleaving gauge transformations between Trotter layers enables gauge-twirling effectively to reduce unphysical violations of local Gauss's law. These results demonstrate that symmetry-based post-processing provides a depth-preserving route to substantially improving the fidelity of Trotterized quantum simulations on near-term devices.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces Mirror Quantum Awesomeness (MQA), an advanced benchmarking technique combining MRB's global error estimation with QA's structural correlation analysis. By tracking per-edge mutual information, MQA provides a more detailed picture of noise accumulation than standard methods. The results validate this approach on large quantum processors, successfully locating a critical circuit depth where current error mitigation techniques are expected to fail.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides advanced diagnostic tools that move beyond simple average gate fidelity measurements, offering insights into how structured correlations decay in large-scale quantum devices, which is crucial for understanding noise mechanisms relevant to AMO platforms like trapped ions or superconducting qubits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing comprehensive benchmarking techniques for large Quantum Processing Units (QPUs) to accurately quantify performance metrics beyond simple gate fidelities, especially in the presence of accumulating errors.

**Main result.** The proposed Mirror Quantum Awesomeness (MQA) protocol successfully tracks per-edge correlation dynamics and locates a critical circuit depth ($\sim 50$ for ibm fez), offering two complementary methods to estimate device limitations.

**Method.** The authors introduce MQA, a hybrid benchmarking protocol that integrates the structured entanglement analysis of Quantum Awesomeness (QA) into the established framework of Mirror Randomized Benchmarking (MRB). They use Mutual Information (MI) to quantify correlation degradation across varying circuit depths.

**Model / system.** The work is demonstrated on physical 156-qubit processors (ibm fez, ibm kingston) and simulated using custom noisy backends. The analysis focuses on qubit connectivity graphs, particularly those admitting perfect matchings for topological variants.

**Key observables.** Average entanglement infidelity ($\epsilon_{\Omega}$), Mutual Information (MI), Effective Polarization (EP), and the critical circuit depth where structured correlations decay due to noise.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit depth ($d$), initial entangling angle ($	heta$), two-qubit gate density ($\xi$), and hardware error rates ($p_1, p_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The topological variant (TMQA) assumes the coupling graph admits a perfect matching and is embeddable in two dimensions. For MQA analysis, parity-based measurement approximations are used.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate MMI dependence on depth for different edge types (paired, spectator, isolated), compare EP decay between MRB and MQA across various backends, and show TMQA performance metrics ($P_{topo}, P_{pairs}$) vs. noise levels.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from standard MRB to the hybrid MQA protocol, introducing MI analysis for per-edge dynamics. It then extends this to Topological MQA (TMQA), which uses graph theory and decoding problems to find a second critical depth estimate, validating both on real hardware.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Mirror randomized benchmarking (MRB) is an established technique that provides a global error metric at the scale of a whole QPU. To expand upon this we introduce Mirror Quantum Awesomeness (MQA), a hybrid protocol that adds a structured entangling layer to MRB circuits. This enables per-edge correlation dynamics to be tracked via mutual information while preserving the MRB infidelity estimate. The resulting analysis of the injected entangled pairs locates a critical circuit depth, beyond which rudimentary error mitigation techniques can be expected to fail. A topological variant, Topological MQA, supplies a second critical depth via a decoder based on the surface-code decoding problem. Both are validated in simulation and demonstrated on the 156-qubit \texttt{ibm\_fez} and \texttt{ibm\_kingston} processors, where MQA closely agrees with MRB on the entanglement infidelity and the critical depth for \texttt{ibm\_fez} is found to be $\sim 50$.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a rigorous concept of 'many-body chirality' by defining it as an obstruction to transforming a quantum state into its complex conjugate via local operations. It proves this diagnostic is sensitive to the underlying anyon theory's mirror symmetry, revealing forms of chirality invisible to standard entanglement measures like the modular commutator.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides powerful, non-local diagnostics (like 4-partite chirality) for topological order that are robust against local noise or simple entanglement measures, which is crucial for understanding fault-tolerant quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a quantum information theoretic characterization of chirality in many-body quantum states, moving beyond insufficient diagnostics like the modular commutator.

**Main result.** LO-chirality for $Z(k)_d$ stabilizer theories is equivalent to the underlying anyon data being mirror invariant, and this obstruction is intrinsically four-partite. Furthermore, these systems exhibit intrinsic 'imaginarity' if $d>2$.

**Method.** The authors introduce LO-chirality as an obstruction to transforming a state into its complex conjugate via local operations (LO), contrasting it with conventional entanglement measures.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on topological phases realized by abelian anyons, specifically $Z(k)_d$ stabilizer mixed and pure states, often modeled on lattices like the honeycomb lattice.

**Key observables.** LO-chirality, LU-imaginarity (intrinsic complex phase structure), four-partite entanglement measures, and the chiral central charge ($c_-$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The anyon level $d$ (especially $d>2$), the number of parties involved in chirality detection (four-partite vs. tripartite), and the nature of local operations (LO vs. LU).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on establishing equivalences between different notions of chirality (e.g., LO-chirality $\iff$ four-partite chirality) for specific model classes.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes suggest comparisons in tables summarizing various topological models against multiple chirality diagnostics ($c^-$, LO chiral, Four-partite chiral).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops concepts sequentially: first defining LO-chirality and linking it to mirror invariance for $Z(k)_d$ theories; then establishing the four-partite nature of this obstruction; subsequently introducing LU-imaginarity as a deeper resource property; and finally applying these tools to analyze specific lattice realizations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A defining feature of chirality is the distinction between a system and its mirror image. Despite extensive experimental observations of chiral phases and theoretical advances, a quantum-information theoretic characterization of chirality based solely on the entanglement structure of many-body quantum states remains elusive. Here, we introduce the notion of many-body chirality by formulating it as an obstruction to transforming a quantum state into its complex conjugate through finite-depth local operations. We rigorously establish many-body chirality for stabilizer realizations of $\mathbb{Z}_d^{(k)}$ anyon theories, proving that complex conjugation can be implemented by local quantum channels if and only if the underlying anyon data are mirror invariant. This reveals forms of chirality that evade conventional diagnostics, including examples with vanishing modular commutator, vanishing chiral central charge, and commuting-projector realizations. We further show that this obstruction is intrinsically four-partite, while invisible to tripartite entanglement structure. Finally, we prove that $\mathbb{Z}_d^{(k)}$ states with $d>2$ possess intrinsic many-body imaginarity: their complex phase structure cannot be removed by finite-depth local unitaries. Remarkably, this includes states that are not many-body chiral.

</details>
