**Summary.** The paper presents a deterministic algorithm to optimize the spatial phase mask applied to a local oscillator beam in waveguide-based quantum optics experiments. By treating the visibility as a sum of pixel contributions, they achieve superior mode overlap compared to previous methods. This optimization directly translates into measurable improvements in multi-spectral squeezing levels, advancing practical quantum measurement techniques.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an interpretable, physically grounded methodology to enhance quantum state measurements (squeezing) in integrated photonic platforms by deterministically controlling spatial mode matching, which is crucial for scaling quantum technologies.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is optimizing multi-spectral squeezing for quantum protocols by maximizing the mode overlap efficiency between a local oscillator (LO) beam and the quantum signal beam.

**Main result.** A deterministic phase shaping algorithm increased the measured visibility from 76% to 84%, leading to a quantifiable improvement in quantum readout, specifically increasing squeezing from -2.08 dB to -2.64 dB.

**Method.** The authors developed and implemented a deterministic sequential optimization algorithm that treats the problem as maximizing the sum of contributions from individual spatial pixels on an SLM mask.

**Model / system.** The experiment uses a waveguide-based setup employing homodyne detection to measure squeezing. The LO's spatial profile is shaped using a Spatial Light Modulator (SLM) whose pixel phases are optimized relative to a reference pixel.

**Key observables.** Visibility ($V$), Mode Overlap Efficiency ($\eta_{mod}$), and Squeezing level (measured in dB).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The optimization scales linearly with the number of pixels, providing an efficient alternative to black-box machine learning techniques.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The visibility function is assumed to decompose additively across individual pixel contributions, allowing for sequential single-parameter optimization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the algorithm flow (sequential phase optimization), the experimental setup involving LO shaping on an SLM, and comparisons of measured squeezing with and without the adaptive mask.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of mode overlap in homodyne detection, details the theoretical decomposition of visibility into pixel contributions, presents the novel deterministic sequential algorithm for phase optimization, validates this method experimentally using a waveguide setup, and concludes by quantifying the resulting quantum enhancement.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Optimization routines are ubiquitous in quantum information technologies and essential to reach the resource levels required by quantum protocols. Specifically, multi-spectral squeezing for use in such protocols requires that losses be kept minimal at every stage, including coherent detection, which is performed by interfering the signal with a classical local-oscillator beam. This in turn requires control over all optical degrees of freedom of the beam in order to optimize the detection. The most general framework for this optimization relies on agnostic, off-the-shelf machine-learning techniques. Here we take the opposite approach: by focusing on a physical description of the specific optical process, we develop a deterministic sequential algorithm that provably reaches the global maximum of the visibility in a pixel basis and scales linearly with the number of pixels, thereby offering an efficient and theoretically grounded alternative to black-box optimization. In our waveguide-based setup, the optimized mask increases the visibility from 76% to 84%, corresponding to a 20% gain in mode-matching efficiency. Multi-spectral squeezing measurements confirm that this improvement translates directly into quantum readout: for the most squeezed spectral mode, the squeezing increases from $-2.08$ dB to $-2.64$ dB, consistent with the inferred efficiency gain. These results establish deterministic spatial phase shaping as an effective, interpretable route to enhanced multimode squeezing in waveguide platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** The researchers investigated electric field noise limiting quantum operations in a surface Paul trap. They found that the noise is strongly correlated with micron-sized particles on the electrode surface. By modeling these particles as lossy dielectrics, they successfully explained the magnitude and frequency dependence of the measured noise, offering a key insight into improving ion trap fidelity.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides crucial experimental grounding for open quantum system modeling in trapped ions. By quantifying environmental noise sources (surface defects) using established electromagnetic theory, it helps refine decoherence models essential for scaling up ion-trap quantum computers.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Electric field noise originating from the surface of ion trap electrodes degrades the fidelity of quantum computing operations, but its microscopic origins are unclear.

**Main result.** The measured electric field noise correlates strongly with the presence of micron-sized particles on the trap surface, and this noise can be successfully modeled by treating these particles as lossy dielectrics.

**Method.** The authors measured the heating rate ($\dot{n}$) of a single trapped ion's motion to quantify the spectral density of electric field noise ($S_E(\omega)$).

**Model / system.** The experiment uses a linear surface Paul trap. The theoretical model treats the noise source as dielectric particles on the electrode surface, whose dissipation is governed by an effective loss tangent ($	an	heta$).

**Key observables.** Electric field noise levels (measured via ion heating rate $\dot{n}$), frequency dependence of noise ($S_E(\omega) \propto 1/\omega$), and particle presence visualized via SEM/CCD.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The effective loss tangent was determined to be $	an	heta = 0.33(0.06)$. Noise levels varied by three orders-of-magnitude across a $600\,\mu	ext{m}$ section.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Particles are modeled as lossy dielectrics, and low-noise regions are assumed to be limited by mechanisms similar to other low-noise traps.

**Figures summary.** Figures show trap fabrication details, measured heating rates across different locations (L1-L7), frequency dependence fits for selected sites, and microscopy images confirming particle presence near high-noise areas.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem of noise in ion traps, presents measurements showing strong spatial variation correlated with particles, develops a theoretical model linking this noise to dielectric loss, and validates the model against measured frequency and spatial dependencies.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electric field noise produced by the surface of ion trap electrodes reduces the fidelity of quantum computing operations. Despite decades of investigation its microscopic origins remain unclear. Here, we measure electric field noise at trapping locations along the symmetry axis of a linear surface Paul trap. We find that noise levels vary by three orders-of-magnitude in one 600$\,μ$m section of the trap. Optical and scanning electron microscope images show micron-sized particles close to the trapping locations with the highest noise levels. We find that modeling the particles as a lossy dielectric with a effective loss tangent $\tanθ=0.33(0.06)$ describes the magnitude of the noise, as well as its spatial and frequency dependence. Our observations may explain the large variation of reported noise levels in literature.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes two complementary techniques—Variational Quantum Algorithms (VQAs) and Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs)—to estimate the von Neumann entropy of multi-qutrit quantum states. It finds that while VQAs are accurate for small systems, CNNs provide superior scalability and robustness when analyzing larger qudit systems by learning features directly from measurement data.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses resource estimation in quantum information processing. The comparison between physically constrained variational methods and data-driven machine learning estimators provides crucial guidance on which computational paradigm is best suited for scaling quantum characterization tasks beyond small proof-of-concept systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is estimating the von Neumann entropy of quantum states in multi-qutrit systems, which is computationally hard due to the exponential scaling of full state tomography.

**Main result.** A trade-off exists between VQAs and CNNs: VQAs are effective for small systems due to physical constraints, while CNNs offer superior scalability and robustness for larger qutrit systems by learning directly from measurement statistics.

**Method.** The study compares two methods: Variational Quantum Algorithms (VQAs) using parameterized unitaries on the quantum hardware side, and classical Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) trained on tensor-product measurements of mutually unbiased bases.

**Model / system.** The system under study is multi-qutrit quantum states ($d=3$ per qutrit), analyzed for systems ranging from two to five qutrits. The methods are tested using ideal, noise-free quantum simulation environments.

**Key observables.** Von Neumann entropy $S(ho)$, estimation error (e.g., 90th-percentile absolute error in nats), and measurement probabilities derived from various bases.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of trainable parameters ($\approx 120$ fixed for VQAs) and the fraction of measurements used ($12.5\%$ sufficient for high accuracy in large systems).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is primarily conducted within simulated, noise-free settings; full state tomography is assumed to be infeasible.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate specific VQA ansatzes (A1-A11) and the CNN architecture. Key plots show error dependence on parameters/gates for VQAs, and error vs. system size/measurement bases for both methods, demonstrating scalability differences.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces entropy estimation in multi-qutrit systems, details the VQA approach (using GMR gates to align basis with eigenbasis), presents the CNN approach using MUB measurements, and concludes by comparing their performance across different system sizes, establishing a clear methodological guideline.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a systematic study of von Neumann entropy estimation in multi-qutrit quantum systems using two complementary approaches: variational quantum algorithms (VQAs) and classical convolutional neural networks (CNNs), evaluated using an ideal (noise-free) quantum simulator. For systems up to three qutrits, we construct and evaluate 11 hardware-efficient SU(3)-inspired ansatzes. A parameter sweep shows that estimation accuracy is primarily determined by the number of trainable parameters, provided sufficient entanglement is present. Based on this study, we fix the parameter count to approximately 120 for subsequent experiments, observing that increasing entangling-gate counts beyond a threshold yields only marginal improvements. For larger systems (two to five qutrits), we use a CNN trained on measurement outcomes from tensor-product mutually unbiased bases. The model achieves accurate and stable predictions and exhibits a systematic improvement in performance with system size, with the highest errors for two-qutrit systems and the lowest for five-qutrit systems. Notably, using only 12.5% of the measurements required for full state tomography is sufficient to reach 90th-percentile absolute errors of approximately 0.13-0.16 nats for both four- and five-qutrit systems. The CNN model is also robust to shot noise and generalizes well to out-of-distribution states. Overall, within the simulated settings studied here, our results indicate a transition in practical methods: VQAs are effective for small systems, while CNN-based estimators offer improved scalability and robustness for larger qutrit systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work rigorously analyzes the measurement resources needed to estimate unknown quantum properties via shadow estimation. It proves a fundamental complexity gap: achieving optimal performance in the worst case requires $\Theta(d^2)$ measurements, while average-case optimality is achieved with only $\Theta(d)$ measurements using state 2-designs.

**Why it may be interesting.** The clear separation between worst-case and average-case resource requirements is crucial for designing practical quantum protocols, suggesting that simple, structured measurements are sufficient for most real-world applications.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is determining the minimal measurement complexity required for achieving optimal shadow estimation of quantum properties from randomized measurements.

**Main result.** A fundamental separation is established: worst-case optimality requires $\Theta(d^2)$ measurement bases, whereas average-case performance only needs $\Theta(d)$ bases.

**Method.** The authors use the framework of $t$-designs (state and unitary) to analyze the shadow norm variance ($\lVert O Vert^2_E$) under different averaging procedures (worst-case vs. Haar random).

**Model / system.** The system involves estimating properties of an unknown quantum state $ho$ in a $d$-dimensional Hilbert space using measurement ensembles $\{|\phi_iangle, w_i\}$ forming a POVM.

**Key observables.** Shadow norm ($\lVert O Vert^2_E$), mean squared shadow norm, and the required number of measurement bases ($N$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** $d$ (system dimension), $t$-design order ($t=2$ or $t=3$), and the complexity gap between worst-case ($\Theta(d^2)$) and average-case ($\Theta(d)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes that the measurement ensemble forms a state 2-design to simplify expectation value calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare mean squared shadow norms for fidelity estimation using different measurements (SIC-POVMs vs. MUBs) and show convergence towards theoretical bounds, illustrating the performance gap.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the shadow norm variance, establishing necessary conditions via $t$-designs, proving the $\Theta(d^2)$ lower bound for worst-case scenarios using explicit constructions (MUBs), and then demonstrating the $\Theta(d)$ sufficiency for average-case performance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Shadow estimation is a powerful framework for predicting quantum properties from randomized measurements. While $3$-design protocols achieve optimal worst-case performance, the minimal number of measurement bases required for such optimality has remained open. Here we prove that $Θ(d^2)$ measurement bases are both necessary and sufficient for worst-case optimal shadow estimation and construct an explicit basis family. In stark contrast, any state $2$-design already suffices for average-case optimality: the mean squared shadow norm of normalized observables is bounded by a universal constant, and we prove strong concentration for Haar-random states, yielding constant sample complexity for generic pure-state fidelity estimation. Easily implementable $2$-designs -- from mutually unbiased bases, cyclic measurements, or shallow $\mathcal{O}(\log n)$-depth circuits -- enable optimal average-case protocols with remarkably simple measurement strategies. Our results establish a fundamental complexity separation: worst-case estimation requires $Θ(d^2)$ bases, whereas average-case performance requires only $Θ(d)$ bases, with broad implications for quantum information theory and near-term experiments.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously compares the entanglement scaling between QAOA and AQC when solving MaxCut on various graphs. It demonstrates that while both methods show volume-law scaling, their specific entanglement profiles differ based on the algorithm's structure and the annealing schedule chosen. The findings establish a strong link between quantum resource theory (entanglement) and the fundamental performance characteristics of near-term quantum algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep insights into the resource theory aspect of quantum computation by quantifying exactly *how* much entanglement is required and generated by different algorithmic approaches for optimization problems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how entanglement scaling relates to problem structure when comparing two major quantum optimization paradigms: QAOA and AQC, applied to the MaxCut problem.

**Main result.** QAOA exhibits entanglement scaling consistent with fermionic Gaussian states under optimal parameter training, while AQC shows distinct, schedule-dependent entanglement profiles. The study highlights that classical optimization protocols significantly impact the observed entanglement profile in variational algorithms.

**Method.** The authors use numerical simulations to calculate and compare entanglement entropy across various bipartitions for QAOA and AQC applied to MaxCut on different graph structures (k-regular, complete).

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using qubits encoding the MaxCut problem Hamiltonian ($H_C$). The algorithms analyzed are the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) and Adiabatic Quantum Computation (AQC), which operate in a Hilbert space of dimension $2^N$.

**Key observables.** Entanglement entropy ($S$), approximation ratio ($r$), required entanglement ($	ext{S}_{	ext{req}}$), and the peak depth ($p^*$) at which maximum entanglement is reached.

**Important parameters / regimes.** QAOA depth ($p$), graph edge density ($\alpha$), system size ($N$), and the specific annealing schedule function $f(s)$ for AQC.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on sampling an ensemble of bipartitions to estimate average entanglement entropy, which is assumed accurate enough for scaling studies. It also compares results against analytical bounds like the Page Value and FGS estimates.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare required entanglement ($	ext{S}_{	ext{req}}$) vs. depth ($p$) or edge density ($\alpha$) across different graph types (k-regular, complete), contrasting QAOA/AQC behavior with theoretical benchmarks like fermionic Gaussian states.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares the entanglement scaling of QAOA and AQC for MaxCut on various graphs, detailing how initialization strategies affect results, establishing connections to FGS bounds, and analyzing the dependence of AQC entanglement on the annealing schedule path.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entanglement is widely regarded as a key resource underlying the power of quantum algorithms and their potential to achieve quantum advantage. With the emergence of variational quantum algorithms, however, questions have arisen regarding how entanglement relates to problem structure and algorithmic performance in near-term quantum applications. Here, we examine this relationship through the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA), a specific class of variational algorithms, applied to the MaxCut problem. We show that suboptimal variational parameter training can significantly modify the observed entanglement profile, obscuring its scaling behavior. By employing a high-performance optimizer, we find empirical evidence that QAOA exhibits entanglement scaling consistent with that of fermionic Gaussian states (up to a scaling factor) across a broad range of MaxCut instances. We further compare these results with adiabatic quantum computation, observing annealing-schedule-dependent entanglement profiles whose scaling behavior differs markedly from that of QAOA. Together, these findings provide new insight into how entanglement manifests in and distinguishes these two algorithmic paradigms, highlighting its connection to both computational performance and application structure.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically investigates a hybrid superconducting ladder featuring both nearest-neighbor and long-range pairing interactions. By analyzing the system's energy spectrum using the BdG Hamiltonian, the authors map out a complex topological phase diagram controlled by three parameters: chemical potential ($\mu$), inter-leg coupling ($t_
u$), and the LR exponent ($\alpha$). The key finding is that these parameters allow for precise engineering of Majorana zero modes, enabling transitions between different numbers of localized edge states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it provides a concrete theoretical framework for realizing tunable topological superconductivity by incorporating non-local (long-range) pairing effects into established models like the Kitaev chain. The ability to control mode numbers via $\alpha$ is key for designing robust quantum hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how incorporating long-range (LR) pairing interactions modifies the topological phase diagram of superconducting systems, specifically focusing on Majorana bound states (MBSs). The goal is to demonstrate controlled tuning of these exotic quantum states.

**Main result.** The system exhibits a rich topological phase diagram where varying the LR pairing exponent ($\alpha$) and inter-leg coupling ($t_
u$) allows for transitions between phases hosting two or four Majorana zero modes. This confirms that long-range interactions provide an additional, tunable mechanism to engineer topological superconductivity.

**Method.** The analysis relies on constructing and solving the Bogoliubov-de Gennes (BdG) Hamiltonian in momentum space to determine the energy spectra and identify gap closings corresponding to topological phase transitions.

**Model / system.** The model is an inter-leg coupled hybrid Kitaev ladder, consisting of two parallel superconducting chains. One chain has conventional nearest-neighbor ($p$-wave) pairing, while the other incorporates algebraically decaying long-range (LR) pairing interactions.

**Key observables.** Majorana zero modes (MZMs), Massive Dirac modes (MDMs), topological phase boundaries, and the number of localized edge states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Long-range pairing exponent ($\alpha$), inter-leg coupling strength ($t_
u$), chemical potential ($\mu$), and superconducting pairing strengths ($\Delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system is modeled using spinless fermions obeying standard fermionic anti-commutation relations, and the analysis maps topological transitions to bulk gap closing points.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate energy spectra showing how changing $t_
u$ or $\alpha$ shifts transition points and controls the coexistence of MZMs and MDMs. Phase diagrams map out the regions in $(\mu, t_
u)$ and $(\alpha, \mu)$ space corresponding to different topological states.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the Hamiltonian by first establishing the model (hybrid Kitaev ladder), then using BdG theory to calculate spectra across varying parameters ($\mu, t_
u, \alpha$), leading to detailed phase diagram construction and concluding with implications for quantum engineering.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate an inter-leg coupled hybrid Kitaev ladder composed of two parallel superconducting chains with distinct pairing interactions. The upper chain of the ladder hosts conventional $p$-wave pairing, while the lower chain exhibits long-range pairing that decays algebraically with distance. We demonstrate that the mutual influence of long-range pairing exponent, chemical potential, and inter-leg coupling strength gives rise to a rich topological phase diagram characterized by multiple Majorana zero modes and massive Dirac modes. In particular, we show that the inter-leg coupling renormalizes the effective energy scales, leading to a systematic shift of the topological phase boundaries and enabling controlled tuning of the Majorana modes. Furthermore, we identify a transition from a two Majorana zero mode phase to a phase encapsulating four Majorana zero modes, as the long-range pairing exponent is varied. This transition is accompanied by a crossover regime in which Majorana zero modes coexist with massive Dirac modes, reflecting hybridization between edge and bulk excitations. This ladder thus provides a minimal and attractive platform for realizing the impact of a long-range pairing on topological phases. Our results highlight the potential of long-range hybrid systems for engineering tunable topological states relevant for quantum information applications.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a general graph-theoretic framework to optimize the compilation of quantum circuits for Neutral Atom Quantum Computers (NAQC). By modeling physical qubit transfers as a combinatorial optimization problem solved with a genetic algorithm, it minimizes required atom movements and maximizes parallel operations. This results in hardware-aware mappings that promise higher fidelity and better efficiency on NAQC devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses hardware limitations—specifically qubit mobility and associated error rates—in emerging quantum computing platforms based on neutral atoms, providing crucial algorithmic tools for realizing scalable quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is developing an efficient, architecture-aware algorithm to compile quantum circuits for Neutral Atom Quantum Computers (NAQC) by minimizing the physical movement and required transfers of qubits.

**Main result.** The proposed graph-theoretic framework consistently finds fewer qubit transfers compared to state-of-the-art compilers, providing a practical tool that reduces movement-induced errors on NAQC devices.

**Method.** The authors model the circuit as a sequence of stages and use graph-theoretic combinatorial optimization (encoding it as a nonlinear integer program) solved via a genetic algorithm to optimize qubit mapping.

**Model / system.** The system is an NAQC platform using ultracold atoms trapped by optical tweezers. The compilation process involves decomposing the circuit into stages, modeling transfers between these stages using graphs, and optimizing for minimal physical movement.

**Key observables.** Total number of qubit transfers ($\lambda_q$), total traveled distance ($\mu$m), and the count of Parallel Transfer Operations (PTOs).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Qubit coherence time, Rydberg blockade interaction range ($\sim 2-4 \mu	ext{m}$), and physical zone constraints.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The optimization relies on decomposing the problem into local maximum weighted matchings at each stage transition, assuming independence between these stages for the global optimum calculation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the decomposition of a quantum circuit into stages and transfer decision graphs; they also show the stick encoding, the process of breaking cycles in transfer digraphs, and benchmark comparisons against existing mappers.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from identifying the physical constraints (qubit movement) to proposing a graph-theoretic framework. It details how the problem is formulated as an integer program solved by a genetic algorithm, culminating in comparative results showing performance improvements over existing compilers.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Neutral atom quantum computers (NAQC) are emerging as a promising, scalable quantum computing platform because of their long qubit coherence, flexible qubit arrangement, and multiqubit gate capabilities. However, circuit execution often requires physically moving qubits, making compilation a critical optimization challenge. We propose a circuit independent mathematical framework built on graph-theoretic combinatorial optimization that determines the minimal number of required qubit transfers. This model captures spatial constraints specific to NAQC platforms with zone-limited gate operations and multi-qubit gates. From this framework, we encode the qubit mapping problem as a nonlinear integer program and solve it using a genetic algorithm, enabling trade-offs between minimizing the total traveled distance and the number of parallel transfer operations. Compared to the state-of-the-art scalable compiler for zoned architectures, our approach consistently finds fewer transfers. Depending on the optimization focus, our method produces shorter traveled distances or fewer parallel transfer operations. This work provides both theoretical guaranties and a practical tool for efficient, architecture-aware quantum circuit compilation. As a result, practitioners can generate hardware-aware mappings that reduce movement-induced errors and better exploit atom transfer parallelism, directly improving execution efficiency on NAQC devices.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper treats High-Harmonic Generation as a form of attosecond interferometry, defining the two dominant electron paths (short and long) as an Attosecond Path Qubit (APQ). It rigorously distinguishes between decoherence caused by classical laser intensity fluctuations (dephasing) and fundamental information loss due to unobserved transverse momentum (trace-out). This establishes HHG as a powerful tool for quantum state characterization in the attosecond regime.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges attosecond physics (HHG) with fundamental concepts of open quantum systems theory, providing measurable signatures ($V$ vs. $P$) to diagnose coherence loss in an extreme non-equilibrium quantum process.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a reduced-state framework for diagnosing coherence loss in High-Harmonic Generation (HHG) by treating the dominant short and long electron trajectories as an experimentally addressable two-level subsystem: an attosecond path qubit (APQ).

**Main result.** The study successfully distinguishes between classical dephasing (ensemble averaging) and fundamental quantum decoherence (trace-out of unobserved transverse momentum), establishing HHG as a platform for engineering trajectory-based quantum states.

**Method.** The authors formulate a trajectory-resolved density matrix for the APQ, analyzing its evolution under both environmental coupling models: classical noise and partial tracing over transverse degrees of freedom.

**Model / system.** The physical system is High-Harmonic Generation (HHG), modeled using the Strong-Field Approximation (SFA). The core concept involves mapping the electron's continuum dynamics onto an effective two-level Hilbert space spanned by short and long quantum trajectories, forming the APQ.

**Key observables.** APQ purity ($	ext{Tr}[ho^2]$), Bloch vector components ($r_x, r_y, r_z$), Interferometric Visibility ($V$), and Predictability ($P$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Attosecond timescale dynamics, laser intensity fluctuations (Gaussian distribution), transverse momentum integration limits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Strong-Field Approximation (SFA) and assumes that short and long trajectories define an approximately orthogonal basis for the APQ.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate Bloch vector dynamics for both pure states and mixed states under dephasing (intensity fluctuations) and decoherence (transverse momentum integration), showing the reduction of coherence magnitude in both cases.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the APQ basis from HHG trajectories, then quantifying coherence loss via two distinct channels—classical dephasing and quantum trace-out decoherence—to establish a comprehensive diagnostic framework for attosecond interferometry.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

High-harmonic generation (HHG) is governed by interference between electron trajectories. We propose that the dominant short and long trajectories define an experimentally addressable two-level subsystem: an attosecond path qubit (APQ). We formulate a trajectory-resolved density matrix to identify two distinct coherence-loss mechanisms: classical dephasing from ensemble averaging and quantum decoherence arising from the trace-out of unobserved degrees of freedom. By investigating shot-to-shot fluctuations and unresolved transverse momentum, we demonstrate that while dephasing suppresses coherence through averaging, the ``trace-out'' channel produces mixed states even for fixed driving parameters. We explore how these mechanisms modify APQ purity and show that mode selection and conditioning provide operational routes to isolate them. These results establish a reduced-state framework for diagnosing coherence loss in HHG and for engineering trajectory-based quantum states in attosecond interferometry.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates how fast an open quantum system can evolve under non-Markovian conditions by calculating the Quantum Speed Limit using time-fractional equations. It rigorously compares two theoretical frameworks, concluding that Wei's Time-Fractional Schrödinger Equation is both more accurate and significantly more computationally efficient than existing methods for modeling these complex dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This is highly relevant as it tackles open quantum dynamics, non-Markovian effects, and speed limits—all central themes in modern AMO physics and quantum optics. The comparison between different mathematical formalisms (TFSE) adds a layer of methodological rigor.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the simulation of non-Markovian quantum accelerated dynamics and characterizing the Quantum Speed Limit (QSL) time for such open quantum systems.

**Main result.** Wei's Time-Fractional Schrödinger Equation (TFSE) is shown to be superior to Naber's TFSE because it accurately captures non-Markovian acceleration over all fractional orders and offers significant computational efficiency.

**Method.** The study employs the TFSE framework, comparing two formulations (Wei's Co-FD vs. Naber's Ca-FD) to simulate dynamics in an open quantum system.

**Model / system.** The analysis is performed on a single-qubit open system modeled by the Resonant Dissipative Jaynes-Cummings (RDJC) model, incorporating non-Markovian memory effects from the environment.

**Key observables.** Quantum Speed Limit time ($	au_{QSL}$), $	au_{QSL}/	au$ ratio, and the excited-state probability $P_e(	au)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fractional order ($eta$), coupling strength ($\lambda$), photon number ($n$), and evolution time ($	au$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The QSL calculation utilizes a Margolus-Levitin-type bound for a pure initial state.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare $	au_{QSL}/	au$ vs. $\lambda$, $	au_{QSL}/	au$ vs. $	au$, and $P_e(	au)$ across different TFSE formulations, highlighting the superior performance of Wei's method.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem by defining QSL in non-Markovian systems. It then compares two TFSE approaches (Wei's vs. Naber's) using the RDJC model to calculate $	au_{QSL}$. The core of the work involves demonstrating Wei's method's superior accuracy across all fractional orders and its computational efficiency.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Time-Fractional Schrödinger Equation (TFSE) is an effective tool for simulating the dynamics of non-Markovian quantum systems. The Quantum Speed Limit (QSL) time characterizes the minimum time required for the evolution of a non-Markovian quantum system. In this paper, Wei's TFSE is employed to simulate the non-Markovian quantum accelerated evolution process in the Resonant Dissipative Jaynes-Cummings (RDJC) model. By solving the QSL time of a time-fractional single-qubit open system, the enhancement mechanism of the system evolution speed induced by the non-Markovian memory effects of the environment is revealed. Further studies show that the optimized acceleration of the system evolution can be achieved by jointly regulating the fractional order, coupling strength, and photon number. Comparative analyses indicate that Wei's TFSE can accurately capture the non-Markovian accelerated dynamical features of the system over the entire fractional order range, whereas Naber's TFSE is applicable only within a limited fractional order interval. In addition, the comparisons of the average simulation time for calculating the dynamical trajectory of the excited-state probability demonstrate that Wei's TFSE has a significant simulation advantage in computational efficiency. Therefore, Wei's TFSE is more accurate and efficient for simulating the accelerated dynamics of non-Markovian quantum systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates how magnetic interactions and particle shape dictate the collective organization of self-propelled microswimmers. By modeling the particles with a dumbbell charge structure, the authors map out five distinct phases—including flocking and vortex alignment—by tuning parameters related to magnetization and geometry.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical active matter, the concept of controlling emergent collective states via tunable interaction potentials (magnetic/steric) is analogous to designing synthetic quantum Hamiltonians or engineered many-body systems in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on characterizing the collective dynamics and phase transitions of self-propelled spherocylinders carrying magnetic dipole moments in two dimensions.

**Main result.** The system exhibits a rich landscape of five distinct collective states—gas, polar flock, chain, vortex-alignment, and locked dimer—controlled by particle elongation and distributed magnetic charge.

**Method.** The dynamics are modeled using overdamped equations of motion for position and orientation, solved via numerical simulations across parameter space.

**Model / system.** The system consists of self-propelled spherocylinders interacting magnetically (modeled as $\pm Q$ monopoles separated by $\ell$) and sterically in 2D. The dynamics are governed by competition between propulsion, repulsion, and magnetic torques.

**Key observables.** Collective phases (gas, polar flock, chain, vortex-alignment, locked state), pair contact energies ($ar{\epsilon}_{	ext{chain}}$, $ar{\epsilon}_{	ext{blocked}}$), polarization ($P$), and vortex order parameters ($\Phi_V, \Phi_A$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Three dimensionless groups control behavior: $\Pi_1$ (charge separation ratio), $\Pi_2$ (magnetic vs. propulsion angular velocity comparison), and $\Pi_3$ (steric force vs. self-propulsion).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dumbbell model is used to regularize the magnetic torque, avoiding divergences associated with point-dipole approximations at short range.

**Figures summary.** Figures map collective behavior across $(\Pi_1, \Pi_2)$ space, illustrating phase boundaries and showing how order parameters change between the five identified states.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the governing equations of motion, introduces dimensionless control parameters ($\Pi_1, \Pi_2$), analyzes pair contact energies to define state transitions, and maps out the resulting phase diagram across varying parameter regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the collective dynamics of self-propelled spherocylinders carrying magnetic dipole moments in two dimensions. Magnetic interactions are modeled as two opposite monopoles $\pm Q$ separated by a distance $\ell$ along the particle director, a dumbbell model that remains well-defined at short range and introduces an explicit geometric lever arm for the magnetic torque. This approach, combined with the elongated particle geometry, produces a torque that competes with steric alignment in a manner inaccessible to point-dipole or disk models. By independently varying monopole separation and dipole strength (parameters that map directly onto the geometry and magnetization of cylindrical magnets) we show that the system navigates a rich landscape of collective states: gas, polar flock, chain, vortex-alignment, and locked-dimer phases. Our results establish that particle elongation and distributed magnetic charge together provide a minimal, experimentally accessible set of tuning knobs for controlling coherent states in magnetic active matter, with direct implications for the design of self-organized magnetic microswimmers and active colloidal assemblies.

</details>

**Summary.** This work explores the physics of twist solitons on magnetic skyrmion tubes, treating the twist as a crucial degree of freedom beyond simple stacking. By combining numerical simulations with analytical theory, the authors demonstrate that the soliton's motion is nonlinearly driven by current and depends critically on its chirality. This paves the way for designing advanced spintronic devices based on 3D spin textures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study deals with topological defects (solitons) in magnetic materials, which are analogous to excitations studied in quantum field theory or condensed matter systems exhibiting strong correlations. The coupling between topology, current drive, and emergent fields is highly relevant to spintronics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how introducing a 'twist' along the axis of a magnetic skyrmion tube affects its complex dynamics and emergent physical responses.

**Main result.** The twist degree of freedom is shown to be essential, enabling current-driven nonlinear motion where the out-of-plane velocity scales quadratically with the driving current and depends on the soliton's chirality.

**Method.** The research combines large-scale numerical simulations solving the LLG equation with analytical calculations using a collective coordinate approach (Thiele equation).

**Model / system.** The system is a magnetic skyrmion tube, modeled as a 3D topological soliton in a chiral magnet incorporating exchange interactions, DMI, and Zeeman coupling. Dynamics are driven by spin-transfer torque.

**Key observables.** Twist soliton velocity components ($v_x, v_y, v_z$), the associated emergent electric field (EEF), and the sign of the chirality ($\chi$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Current density ($j_e$), magnetic field component perpendicular to the tube, and the twist chirality ($\chi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The Thiele analysis relies on a rigid-body approximation for steady-state dynamics, which is noted to cause discrepancies with direct LLG simulations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the formation of twisted tubes via quench dynamics, show snapshots of motion under current drive (lateral and out-of-plane), and plot velocity dependence on current density and magnetic field tilt angle.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical system (skyrmion tubes) to introducing the twist degree of freedom. It then analyzes the dynamics using both numerical simulations (LLG) and analytical models (Thiele equation), culminating in demonstrating control via external fields and detection via emergent electric fields.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A magnetic skyrmion tube is a three-dimensional topological soliton formed by stacking two-dimensional skyrmions along the out-of-plane direction. Recent real-space observations of skyrmion tubes have stimulated growing interest in their dynamics and emergent properties. Here, we go beyond simple skyrmion stacking and investigate how a ``twist" introduced along the tube direction affects the dynamics and emergent responses of skyrmion tubes. We find that such a twist can be created as a localized texture, termed a twist soliton, through thermal quench dynamics. By complementarily combining large-scale numerical simulations with analytical calculations based on collective coordinates, we clarify its current-driven nonlinear motions that depend on its twist chirality. Remarkably, its velocity can be substantially enhanced by a magnetic-field component perpendicular to the tube. Furthermore, the associated emergent electric field enables identification of the twist soliton, including the sign of its chirality, through Hall measurements. Our results reveal the twist degree of freedom as an essential ingredient of skyrmion-tube physics and pave the way for the development of spintronic devices exploiting the three-dimensional nature of spin textures.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates the time evolution of observables in WZW CFTs subjected to environmental coupling via a Lindbladian. It finds that while Abelian models allow for broad classes of exactly solvable dissipative dynamics, non-Abelian structures severely restrict solvability, requiring high degrees of symmetry in dissipation rates to maintain algebraic closure.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep insights into how algebraic structures (like current algebras) constrain the dynamics of open quantum systems, offering powerful theoretical tools applicable to understanding dissipation and thermalization in complex field theories.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is determining the exact time evolution of physical observables in open quantum many-body systems coupled to environments, specifically within Wess-Zumino-Witten (WZW) conformal field theories.

**Main result.** Exact solvability for operator dynamics is highly restricted: it holds generally in the Abelian $U(1)_k$ case but requires specific symmetries (like symmetric dissipation rates) in the non-Abelian setting, leading to a loss of general algebraic closure.

**Method.** The authors analyze the Lindbladian master equation by checking for the closure of the Heisenberg equations of motion for current operators and their products, rather than solving the full Liouvillian spectrum.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using WZW CFTs whose dynamics are governed by a Lindblad master equation. The Hamiltonian is derived from the Sugawara construction, involving affine Kac-Moody currents ($J^b_p$).

**Key observables.** Operator dynamics (e.g., $\langle J^p J^r angle(t)$), closure of Heisenberg equations for current modes, and mixing time ($	au_{mix}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The level $k$ of the WZW theory, the structure constants of the underlying Lie algebra ($g$), and the specific rates/symmetries of the Lindblad jump operators ($\gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often focuses on chiral holomorphic sectors or assumes initial states are Gaussian to simplify expectation value calculations. The closure of the Heisenberg equation is a necessary but not sufficient condition for full solvability.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the structure implies comparisons between Abelian and non-Abelian dynamics under dissipation.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the problem by first establishing the general framework (WZW models and Lindbladians), then contrasting the solvable Abelian case ($U(1)_k$) with the constrained non-Abelian case ($SU(2)_k$), culminating in a discussion of necessary symmetry conditions for exact solvability.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding the time evolution of physical observables in open quantum many-body systems coupled to external environments is a natural and difficult problem, and exact results are still rare. In this work, we study this problem for Wess-Zumino-Witten (WZW) conformal field theories with Lindblad jump operators linear in Kac-Moody current modes. We investigate the exact operator dynamics generated by these Lindbladians, identifying classes of current operators whose Heisenberg equations close and can therefore be solved analytically using the underlying current algebra. In Abelian $U(1)_k$ WZW theories, this closure of operator dynamics holds for arbitrary settings of jump rates and includes exactly tractable cooling dynamics. In contrast, for non-Abelian WZW theories, exact closure occurs only for symmetric current-mode dissipation, where upward and downward current-mode transitions occur with equal rates, and even then it leads to a simple closed evolution only for a single current operator. Generic imbalances, including those needed for cooling, produce additional non-Abelian terms and prevent closure of the opeartor dynamics. Consequently, the current algebra gives rise to a broad family of exactly solvable dissipative dynamics in the Abelian setting, whereas in the non-Abelian case it singles out only a special exactly solvable dynamics corresponding to an infinite-temperature bath.

</details>

**Authors:** B. Vigneshwar, Farhaan Khan, R. Sankaranarayanan  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.19945v1>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theoretical framework to understand the maximum extractable work (daemonic gain) from quantum batteries using ancilla assistance. It establishes that this gain is bounded by the system's bound energy and proposes a computationally simpler metric based on global purity ($P_g$). The research further shows how environmental dissipation and intrinsic interactions critically modulate this thermodynamic advantage.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-equilibrium thermodynamics in quantum systems, providing tools to quantify energy harvesting efficiency beyond simple correlation measures by incorporating information theoretic concepts like purity.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates ancilla-assisted work extraction from quantum batteries by characterizing how bound energy and purity govern the achievable daemonic gain.

**Main result.** Daemonic gain is governed not only by correlations but also critically by the spectral structure of the Hamiltonian, with a purity-based gain ($P_g$) serving as an effective predictor for this advantage.

**Method.** The authors derive theoretical bounds relating daemonic gain ($\delta W$) to bound energy ($E_b$), and introduce $P_g$ to bypass computationally intensive measurement optimization protocols.

**Model / system.** The model involves quantum batteries, treated as bipartite system-ancilla states ($ho_{SA}$), whose dynamics are analyzed under unitary evolution or Lindblad master equations incorporating dissipation.

**Key observables.** Daemonic Gain ($\delta W$), Bound Energy ($E_b$), Purity-based Gain ($P_g$), Ergotropy ($\xi$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dissipation strength ($\gamma_d$), DMI coupling strength ($D$), initial correlation parameters ($\alpha, eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes the validity of $P_g$ as a proxy for $\delta W$, and later sections analyze specific regimes like weak-DMI or strong-DMI interactions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate comparisons between $P_g$ and $\delta W$ across pure, mixed, and Werner states; another figure shows the effect of dissipation on gain, and a final set maps $P_g$ dependence on DMI strength ($D$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the upper bound ($\delta W \le E_b$) to introducing the predictive quantity ($P_g$), then analyzing open system dynamics (dissipation), and finally examining how intrinsic interactions modify this gain via spectral restructuring.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate ancilla-assisted work extraction in quantum batteries from the perspective of bound energy and purity. We show that the bound energy of the reduced system provides a tight upper bound to the daemonic gain and that this bound is saturated for globally pure system--ancilla states. Motivated by this relation, we introduce a purity-based gain that qualitatively predicts the daemonic gain without requiring explicit optimization over measurements. We further introduce a protocol to analyze the role of dissipation and intrinsic interactions on daemonic gain. Under a collective environment, dissipation can dynamically generate and stabilize finite daemonic gain through environment-induced correlations. In interacting systems, level crossings and spectral restructuring strongly modify the attainable gain through their influence on the accessible bound energy. Our results demonstrate that daemonic gain is governed not only by correlations, but also by the spectral structure of the underlying Hamiltonian and information loss captured by bound energy and purity.

</details>

### [Thermodynamic Measure of Intelligence](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20231v1)

**Authors:** Ishanu Chattopadhyay  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.20231v1>

The rare-valid fidelity (bΦ) measures how accurately the simulation identifies targetable rare-valid futures. **Summary.** This work proposes a universal thermodynamic measure of intelligence based on how an agent amplifies the probability of rare but physically valid futures. It mathematically links this amplification ('rare-valid lift') directly to the necessity of recursive self-simulation within the agent's internal model. The framework provides quantitative metrics applicable across diverse systems, from classical controllers to advanced AI models.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about quantum systems, the use of fluctuation theorems and information geometry provides powerful mathematical tools for analyzing non-equilibrium processes, which are central to open quantum system dynamics and measurement theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To define and measure intelligence as a physical, thermodynamic quantity by quantifying its ability to amplify rare but valid futures.

**Main result.** Recursive self-simulation is necessary and nearly sufficient for high thermodynamic intelligence, which is quantified by the 'rare-valid lift' relative to passive dynamics.

**Method.** The framework uses non-equilibrium fluctuation theorems and information theory to compare induced path laws ($P$) against a passive baseline law ($P_0$).

**Model / system.** The model involves abstract systems ranging from passive matter and feedback controllers to LLMs, analyzed via their ability to generate or select rare trajectories in the space of possible futures.

**Key observables.** Rare-valid lift ($\mathcal{I}_\delta$), Path-deviation divergence ($	ext{DKL}$), and the stabilized double-log scale ($\Lambda$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The measure is highly dependent on specified parameters: baseline law, validity criterion, level of description, and trajectory resolution.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes applicability of fluctuation theorems; requires explicit definition of baselines and validity sets for measurement to be meaningful.

**Figures summary.** Figures conceptually illustrate recursive self-simulation across modeling levels and define the thermodynamic intelligence measure ($\mathcal{I}_\delta$) as a lift relative to passive dynamics.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining intelligence via rare-valid amplification, establishing mathematical links between this concept and simulation fidelity (Theorems 1 & 2), deriving bounds using path deviation measures (Theorem 3), and finally applying the framework to symbolic generation and physical engines.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Can intelligence be measured? We propose that intelligence can be defined as the lawful amplification of rare but valid futures: a system increases the probability of outcomes that would be unlikely under passive dynamics but remain admissible under the constraints of the domain. We start with the premise that an intelligent system must model the world and its own place within it. Because the system is part of the world it models, this leads naturally to recursive self-simulation: the system represents futures in which its own actions are part of the trajectory. Our central results give a necessity statement and a conditional near-sufficiency statement connecting this architecture to a precise thermodynamic measure of lawful amplification of rare-valid futures: high rare-valid lift is impossible unless the internal simulation identifies rare-valid futures with high fidelity; conversely, when rare-valid fidelity is high and the simulation contains an effective policy, the achievable lift approaches the actuation-limited optimum. Thus recursive self-simulation is not merely a plausible feature of intelligence but, under the stated assumptions, is necessary and nearly sufficient for high thermodynamic intelligence. The resulting framework makes intelligence measurable on a universal scale, from passive matter and feedback controllers, large language models, and humans as text generators to Maxwell-demon-like information engines.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a hybrid method combining Pauli propagation with noise-canceling inverse channels to estimate ideal quantum observables from noisy hardware measurements. By constructing a modified observable ($	ilde{O}$), the authors mitigate errors and reduce sampling overhead compared to standard error correction techniques. This framework offers a pathway toward realizing quantum advantage by efficiently extracting accurate physical information from imperfect quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides advanced theoretical tools for error mitigation that directly address the resource scaling issues inherent in simulating complex quantum dynamics, which is highly relevant to understanding noise effects in open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is mitigating accuracy limitations in quantum simulation arising from hardware noise (error rates/sampling overheads) and the exponential complexity of classical methods like Pauli propagation.

**Main result.** A hybrid framework embedding Pauli propagation within a noise-canceling inverse channel successfully estimates ideal observables by measuring a modified observable ($	ilde{O}$) on noisy hardware, outperforming traditional methods.

**Method.** The authors propose two specific techniques—Propagated Noise Absorption (PNA) and Euclid—to construct $	ilde{O}$ by classically backpropagating the target observable through noise-canceling inverse channels derived from Pauli evolution rules.

**Model / system.** The work is benchmarked on canonical quantum models, specifically using superconducting qubits (up to 56 qubits) whose dynamics are modeled via Trotterized Hamiltonian evolution under a sparse Pauli-Lindblad noise model.

**Key observables.** Noise-canceling observables ($	ilde{O}$), the expectation value $\langle 	ilde{O} angle$, and diagnostic quantities like truncation bias relative to the ideal noiseless value of $+1$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Error Per Layered Gate (EPLG) target values ($\approx 2 	imes 10^{-3}$); Pauli order ($k$) and Dyson order ($p$); approximation thresholds ($\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core approximations involve truncating the exponentially complex Pauli expansions based on coefficient magnitude or limiting the number of terms/steps, and assuming validity in the weak noise regime for certain derivations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between PNA, Euclid, and naive lightcone reductions; they show diagrammatic representations (Steps and Bubbles) used in the perturbative expansion of $	ilde{O}$ coefficients.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first introducing the limitations, then detailing the mathematical framework for constructing $	ilde{O}$ via Pauli propagation rules (diagrammatically), followed by numerical benchmarking on canonical models, and concluding with experimental validation on a superconducting quantum processor.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The pursuit of quantum advantage is driving the co-evolution of quantum processors and classical simulation methods. Despite advances in scale and quality, the accuracy of quantum simulation is ultimately limited by error rates and sampling overheads. Similarly, while classical simulation methods such as Pauli propagation have made remarkable progress, their accuracy is ultimately limited by the exponential growth of operator paths and the truncations needed to control memory and runtime. Here we show that these complementary limitations can be mitigated by embedding Pauli propagation within a hybrid error-mitigation framework that reduces quantum sampling overhead while achieving lower truncation errors with fewer classical resources than traditional Pauli propagation alone. In this framework, a target observable is classically propagated through noise-canceling inverse channels, producing a modified observable that is measured directly on a quantum processor. We prototype two implementations and benchmark their performance numerically on canonical models that challenge traditional Pauli propagation. We also perform experiments on a quantum processor using 56 superconducting qubits, revealing the tradeoffs of their respective truncation strategies. These results illustrate how classical and quantum resources can be orchestrated to extend observable estimation beyond the limits of either approach alone, providing a foundation for quantum-centric supercomputing and future demonstrations of quantum advantage.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates if quantum batteries can power parametric amplifiers by replacing continuous microwave drives. The authors show that while different pump states can deliver similar amounts of energy, only a truly phase-coherent pump state generates the necessary sub-vacuum interference signature. This establishes phase coherence as the essential resource for efficient, battery-powered quantum amplification.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a clear quantum resource theory for superconducting circuit components, demonstrating that mere energy storage is insufficient; phase information must be preserved for optimal operation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central question is whether a quantum battery can replace or strongly duty-cycle the continuous microwave pump tone required for phase-locked parametric amplification.

**Main result.** Phase coherence, not just stored energy, is the critical resource; only a coherent pump state generates the necessary anomalous two-mode coherence and sub-vacuum interference dip ($I_{\min} < 1$).

**Method.** The analysis uses quantum optical diagnostics (interference visibility $I(\phi)$) applied to open-system dynamics modeled by master equations, comparing different initial pump states.

**Model / system.** The system is a nondegenerate parametric amplifier driven by a finite bosonic quantum battery acting as the pump. The process is analyzed in both closed and cascaded open-system models.

**Key observables.** Phase-sensitive interference minimum ($I_{\min}$), anomalous two-mode coherence ($\langle \hat{a}\hat{b} angle$), and collected energy ($n_f$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pump state type (coherent vs. phase-randomized), squeezing parameter ($r$), and detection bandwidth/time ($	au$ or $\gamma_f$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on the assumption of a Markovian evolution for leakage, and some parts use approximations like the stiff-pump limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare $I_{\min}$ across pump types (coherent vs. phase-randomized), showing that only the coherent pump achieves sub-vacuum interference ($I_{\min} < 1$) while depositing similar energy to the randomized pumps.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the fundamental resource distinction using simple models, extending this to open systems with leakage, and finally exploring how nonclassical states like squeezing can optimize the required phase coherence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum batteries have been proposed as locally precharged work sources for superconducting quantum technologies, suggesting a route to reduce continuously supplied microwave drives. Here we ask whether the pump tone of a quantum-limited parametric amplifier can be replaced, or strongly duty-cycled, by a finite bosonic quantum battery. Quantizing the pump of a nondegenerate parametric amplifier exposes a resource distinction hidden in the classical description: stored pump energy can generate signal-idler photons, but pump phase coherence is required to generate a phase-locked amplifier field. In a closed trilinear model, coherent and phase-randomized coherent pumps with the same photon-number distribution produce comparable pair numbers, yet only the coherent pump produces anomalous two-mode coherence and an EPR-squeezed interference dip. Including leakage, we collect the emitted fields into cascaded temporal modes. At matched collector bandwidth, the coherent pump gives \(I_{\min}^{(f)}=0.553\), whereas the phase-randomized pump gives \(I_{\min}^{(f)}=1.94\) at nearly identical collected energy. Weak amplitude squeezing slightly improves the dip by reducing finite-pump number fluctuations while preserving the coherent displacement. Thus battery-powered parametric amplification requires phase-coherent stored energy, possibly assisted by number-noise reduction, rather than stored energy alone.

</details>

**Summary.** This research advances the understanding of nuclear spin dynamics in highly polarized Helium-3 gas by incorporating the metastable alignment tensor into the theoretical description of metastability-exchange collisions. By developing a full model and comparing it to FID measurements, the authors demonstrate that these tensorial effects cause measurable deviations in relaxation rates and frequency shifts at high polarizations. This has direct applications for improving systematic error correction in high-accuracy magnetometry.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes the boundaries of precision measurement in atomic physics by quantifying systematic errors (tensor effects) that limit magnetometry accuracy in polarized noble gases.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the metastable alignment tensor, previously ignored, significantly affects the dynamics of highly polarized Helium-3 ensembles during metastability-exchange collisions.

**Main result.** The full theoretical model incorporating these tensorial effects accurately predicts polarization-dependent changes in both the relaxation rate and the frequency shift observed experimentally at high polarizations.

**Method.** The authors developed a linearized, mean-field model to describe the evolution of collective angular momentum variables, which was then compared against Free-Induction-Decay (FID) measurements.

**Model / system.** The physical system is highly polarized Helium-3 gas undergoing Metastability-Exchange Optical Pumping (MEOP). The theoretical framework models the coupled dynamics between ground and metastable Zeeman sublevel populations, including rank-2 tensorial contributions.

**Key observables.** ME-induced relaxation rate ($\Gamma_{	ext{ME}}$) and frequency shift ($\delta\omega_{	ext{ME}}$) measured via FID signals.

**Important parameters / regimes.** High nuclear polarization ($P > 0.5$), external magnetic field strength ($B_0$), and the comparison between simplified (orientation-only) and full (tensor-inclusive) models.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses a linearized model under mean-field approximation, and comparisons are made across different polarization regimes where the simple model breaks down.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the dependence of relaxation rates and frequency shifts on both external magnetic field strength and nuclear polarization, comparing simplified vs. full model predictions.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a theoretical framework by extending previous models to include alignment tensors, derives linearized evolution equations for tensorial components, and validates these predictions by analyzing experimental FID measurements of relaxation rates and frequency shifts across varying polarizations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Highly polarized 3He ensembles prepared by metastability-exchange optical pumping (MEOP) have been widely used in precision measurements and fundamental physics. Metastability-exchange (ME) collisions, serving as the basis of MEOP, are traditionally described in terms of atomic orientation, while the significant contributions of metastable alignment tensor at high polarization remain unexplored. In this work, we develop a linearized model under mean-field approximation to investigate alignment tensor effects in highly polarized 3He , which originate from the metastable F = 3/2 manifold and are revealed through ME-induced relaxation and frequency shift. By means of free-induction-decay (FID) measurements, a pronounced dependence on nuclear polarization is experimentally observed in the response of the ground-state-metastable hybrid 3He ensembles to the external magnetic field. Furthermore, after obtaining the characteristics of tensor-induced phenomena, we demonstrate good agreement between the experiment and the theory. This work advances the understanding of nuclear spin dynamics in highly polarized 3He using MEOP. It further provides applications in systematic error correction of high-accuracy magnetometry, as well as in optimal protocol for the generation of nuclear spin-squeezed states.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the dynamics of a Bose polaron—an impurity moving through a Bose gas condensate—by examining the role of interaction range. Using mean-field theory, the authors find that long-range forces lead to significantly different relaxation dynamics and slower equilibration compared to short-range interactions. The findings are crucial for understanding momentum transfer in strongly coupled quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides detailed insights into non-equilibrium transport phenomena in quantum gases, particularly highlighting how the spatial nature of interactions dictates relaxation timescales and effective mass renormalization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the interaction range between an impurity particle and a host Bose gas affects the mean-field dynamics of the resulting Bose polaron.

**Main result.** Relaxation dynamics exhibit damped oscillations, and the equilibration process is highly sensitive to whether the interaction is long-range (like ion-atom forces) or short-range (local potentials).

**Method.** The analysis employs mean-field theory in the impurity's reference frame, utilizing hydrodynamic equations derived from the Gross-Pitaevskii equation.

**Model / system.** The system is a 3D Bose polaron model involving an impurity interacting with a degenerate Bose gas. The Hamiltonian includes terms for kinetic energy and various interaction potentials (e.g., power-law or Gaussian).

**Key observables.** Impurity velocity oscillations, effective mass ($m^*$), Polaron Residue ($Z$), and Tan contact ($C$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength, interaction range (long vs. short), and the coupling regime (strong coupling).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The Lee-Low-Pines transformation is used, assuming Galilean invariance, and a mean-field decoupling for bosons is applied.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare ground state density profiles, show scattering length dependence on interaction type, plot polaron residue vs. strength, and illustrate impurity momentum decay over time for different potentials.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the dynamics using hydrodynamic equations, linearizes them to study perturbations ($\delta n, \delta S$), derives a Langevin equation for impurity momentum, and compares results obtained from long-range versus short-range interaction models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider the three-dimensional Bose polaron problem in the regime of finite range interactions and competing length scales. Working in the reference frame of the impurity, we study both static and out of equilibrium properties of the system, in particular the transfer of momentum between the impurity and the host gas. We find that relaxation dynamics can occur via damped oscillations of the impurity velocity with simple dependence on the interaction strength. Furthermore, the equilibration process is sensitive to the type of the impurity-bath interaction. Specifically, interatomic forces describing ion-atom systems lead to much longer timescales and more pronounced oscillations in the strong coupling regime with respect to local interaction potentials. We also find that the effective masses can differ by a large amount between the two scenarios, even if the number of atoms in the polaron cloud remains similar for both cases.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces an advanced 'Operational Tube-Sector Theory' to measure quantum state distinguishability beyond standard methods. It shows that the true physical measurement capability is governed by a boundary tube algebra, which captures conditional information invisible to coarse symmetry measurements. This provides a powerful tool for understanding how entanglement and generalized symmetries constrain what can be experimentally measured.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous mathematical framework for quantifying the operational advantage gained by measuring fine-grained quantum correlations (like those encoded in tube sectors) that are otherwise lost or averaged out when only symmetry-protected observables are measured.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes a refined operational theory for quantum-state distinguishability that surpasses standard fidelity or symmetry-resolved diagnostics, especially in systems with generalized symmetries.

**Main result.** The optimal measurement structure is uniquely determined by the center of the boundary tube algebra, $A_{	ext{phys}} = Z(	ext{Tube}C(M_A))$, which quantifies information lost during coarse-graining operations.

**Method.** It develops an 'Operational Tube-Sector Theory' using variational principles and resource theory concepts to characterize physically admissible quantum instruments under symmetry constraints.

**Model / system.** The framework applies to general many-body systems characterized by generalized symmetries, including noninvertible cases described by fusion categories. Specific benchmarks include the doubled-Ising product Kramers–Wannier model.

**Key observables.** Tube-sector probabilities ($p_\alpha$), restricted discrimination functional ($F_P(A)$), and the tube total variation distance ($	ext{D}_{	ext{tube}}	ext{TV}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Generalized symmetries, entanglement cuts, fusion categories, and boundary module structure.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory assumes admissibility constraints (complete positivity, locality at the cut) define the physical measurement cone, and that standard diagnostics are coarse-grained limits of the full tube distribution.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the hierarchy of measurement algebras, showing how the tube readout provides a strictly higher success probability ($\Delta P > 0$) compared to scalar or symmetry-resolved readouts when conditional information is present but ignored by coarser measurements.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the operational structure via admissible instruments (Chunk 1), deriving the optimal measurement algebra $A_{	ext{phys}}$ (Chunk 2), proving that tube sectors capture non-redundant information invisible to coarse readouts (Chunks 3 & 4), and finally formalizing this as a resource theory of measurement (Chunks 5, 6, & 7).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A variational principle for quantum-state distinguishability is established in many-body systems with generalized symmetries, including noninvertible cases described by fusion categories. Standard fidelity and symmetry-resolved diagnostics emerge as coarse-grained limits of a more refined operational structure. When symmetry actions terminate at entanglement cuts, distinguishability is governed by boundary tube algebras within a symmetry-constrained measurement resource theory. The physically admissible instruments are characterized by complete positivity, entanglement-cut locality, boundary-module covariance, and sequential stability. The resulting optimal measurement structure is uniquely fixed by the center of the boundary tube algebra, $\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{phys}} = Z\!\left(\mathrm{Tube}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{M}_A)\right)$, whose primitive idempotents define tube-sector probabilities that refine fidelity-based and symmetry-resolved descriptions. The associated tube positive-operator-valued measures (POVM) are extremal and yield optimal one-shot hypothesis-testing distinguishability under symmetry constraints. The construction is universal across fusion categories and independent of microscopic realization.

</details>
