**Summary.** The paper proves that standard, physically regular Hamiltonian descriptions cannot exactly reproduce the linear short-time decay characteristic of Markovian dissipation described by the GKLS equation. This forces the conclusion that such dissipative processes must be understood as effective approximations rather than exact reduced dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a fundamental constraint linking the mathematical structure of open quantum evolution (GKLS) to the physical constraints imposed by energy conservation and boundedness on the underlying microscopic dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether irreversible quantum dissipation, described by the GKLS equation, can be exactly modeled as the reduced dynamics of a physically regular Hamiltonian dilation.

**Main result.** Exact Markovian dissipation requires singular energy resources (e.g., unbounded Hamiltonians), implying that such dissipative processes must be treated as effective descriptions rather than exact reductions from standard physical Hamiltonians.

**Method.** The authors compare the short-time decay of the survival probability ($\ell(t)$) predicted by physically regular Hamiltonian evolutions ($o(t)$ or $O(t^2)$) against the linear initial decay ($\Gamma t$) characteristic of non-unitary GKLS semigroups.

**Model / system.** The system involves open quantum dynamics governed by the Gorini–Kossakowski–Lindblad–Sudarshan (GKLS) equation. The analysis contrasts this with unitary evolution generated by a total Hamiltonian $H = H_S + H_E + H_{int}$ acting on an environment.

**Key observables.** Survival probability loss ($\ell(t)$), and the linear decay coefficient $\Gamma$ derived from the GKLS generator.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The regularity conditions, specifically requiring the total Hamiltonian to be bounded below or having finite short-time interaction energy moments.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes 'physically regular energy conditions' (e.g., lower bound on $H$ or finite second moment of $H_{int}$).

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the core comparison is between $\ell(t) \sim t^k$ for $k\ge 2$ (regular case) and $\ell_{	ext{GKLS}}(t) \sim t$ (dissipative case).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes necessary conditions on the short-time behavior of survival loss ($\ell(t)$) under various regularity assumptions derived from Hamiltonian dilation theory, contrasting these bounds with the known linear decay rate of GKLS generators.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Gorini--Kossakowski--Lindblad--Sudarshan (GKLS) equation describes irreversible quantum dynamical semigroups. We show that this description cannot be exact under physically regular energy conditions. We prove that the open-system survival probability under physically regular energy conditions has sublinear decay, whereas any dissipative GKLS semigroup has a linear short-time decay. Hence exact Markovian dissipation requires singular energy resources: an unbounded-below total Hamiltonian or infinite initial energy, and a divergent interaction-energy moment. Therefore, a dissipative time-independent GKLS equation should be regarded as an effective description rather than the exact reduced dynamics of a Hamiltonian dilation satisfying physically regular energy conditions.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the non-equilibrium dynamics of biclique quantum spin glasses using the Truncated Wigner Approximation (TWA). The authors demonstrate that TWA accurately captures crucial physical signatures, such as sample fluctuations and critical exponents, across large system sizes. This validates TWA as a powerful, scalable tool for studying quantum phase transitions in disordered magnetic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a computationally inexpensive yet accurate semiclassical tool (TWA) to study complex many-body dynamics in disordered systems, which is relevant for understanding quantum criticality beyond mean-field treatments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how accurately the Truncated Wigner Approximation (TWA) captures the near adiabatic dynamics of biclique quantum spin glasses, particularly concerning sample-to-sample fluctuations.

**Main result.** TWA successfully recovers sample-to-sample fluctuations of the Edwards-Anderson order parameter and extracts critical exponents consistent with theoretical expectations across increasing system sizes.

**Method.** The authors use the Truncated Wigner Approximation (TWA), a semiclassical method, to simulate the time evolution of the quantum spin glass Hamiltonian using Trotterization.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a biclique quantum spin glass modeled by a time-dependent Ising Hamiltonian: H(t) = J(t/ta)H_0 + g(t/ta)H_x, involving $\sigma^z$ and $\sigma^x$ terms.

**Key observables.** Edwards-Anderson order parameter ($\langle q^2 angle$), Binder cumulant ($U$), and critical exponents ($\mu$, $r$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Annealing time range, system size (up to tens of thousands of qubits), and the fidelity/correlation coefficient ($ho$) between TWA and exact results.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the TWA remains accurate in the large-N limit; discrepancies are noted at late times or for small systems with large order parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare TWA to exact simulations, showing good agreement ($ho > 99\%$) and demonstrating data collapse of the Binder cumulant consistent with theoretical predictions across varying system sizes.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by introducing the model and method (TWA). It then presents results showing that TWA captures key non-equilibrium features, such as sample fluctuations and critical exponents, validating its use for studying quantum phase transitions in spin glasses.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum spin glasses are often considered testbeds for studying quantum optimization algorithms and as such have been the subject of various quantum advantage claims. Here we investigate the near adiabatic dynamics of biclique quantum spin glasses within the (discrete) truncated Wigner approximation (TWA). Benchmarks on small systems show that TWA recovers sample-to-sample fluctuations of the Edwards-Anderson order parameter, over a wide range of annealing times, with increasing fidelity when the system size increases. We extract critical exponents from the Binder cumulant in line with theoretical expectations, reproducing recent quantum experiments. The computational cost of the method is minimal and it can easily be applied to tens of thousands of qubits.

</details>

**Summary.** The research reports on observing three-body correlations in Bose polarons by studying an impurity atom within a BEC. By performing rf spectroscopy and developing a three-level theoretical model, the authors demonstrate that the condensate environment coherently hybridizes molecular bound states (dimer/trimer). This work provides strong evidence for complex many-body interactions in ultracold atomic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an excellent example of how complex many-body physics (three-body correlations) can be mapped onto a simple, experimentally verifiable effective Hamiltonian (three-level model), drawing parallels to fundamental concepts in particle physics like CKM mixing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates strong correlations, specifically three-body interactions, within Bose polarons formed by an impurity immersed in a Bose-Einstein condensate (BEC). The goal is to understand how the BEC environment coherently hybridizes different molecular bound states.

**Main result.** Clear signatures of three-body correlations are observed spectroscopically, confirming that the ground state eigenstate is a superposition of dimer and trimer components driven by strong BEC coupling. The simple three-level model successfully captures these hybridization effects without requiring free parameters.

**Method.** The work combines theoretical modeling using a three-level Hamiltonian with experimental radiofrequency (rf) spectroscopy on trapped atoms. Variational calculations are used to derive the matrix elements for this effective Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The system involves an impurity atom interacting with a BEC of bosons, leading to polaronic hybridization between bare atomic states, $	ext{NaK}$ dimers, and $	ext{Na}_2	ext{K}$ trimers. The physics is modeled using a three-level Hamiltonian derived from microscopic interactions.

**Key observables.** Spectroscopic features (energy shifts and broadening), state composition of the ground state eigenstate, and impurity lifetimes ($	ext{1/}\Gamma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system operates in a strong-coupling regime where BEC coupling exceeds intrinsic molecular splittings; key parameters include BEC density and magnetic field tuning near Feshbach resonances.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the assumption that the BEC coupling is strong enough to induce hybridization comparable to or exceeding intrinsic molecular splittings. Theoretical models use approximations like the Born approximation for interboson repulsion.

**Figures summary.** Figures show spatially resolved spectra confirming signal dependence on density, energy eigenvalue plots illustrating state superposition vs. density, and lifetime measurements distinguishing between attractive and repulsive polaron branches.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical problem (three-body correlations in polarons) to developing a simple three-level model Hamiltonian. It details experimental spectroscopy using rf pulses and compares theoretical predictions of hybridization energies and state weights against measured spectral features across varying BEC densities.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Bose polaron problem of an impurity immersed in a Bose-Einstein condensate (BEC) has been predicted to feature strong correlations arising from bound states of multiple bosons with the impurity. While direct experimental evidence has so far remained elusive, here we observe clear signatures of three-body correlations in Bose polarons. We perform radiofrequency spectroscopy on $^{40}$K impurities in a BEC of $^{23}$Na and identify polaronic hybrid states that can be understood as superpositions of the bare atom, a NaK dimer and a Na$_2$K trimer, coupled through coherent particle exchange with the condensate. We show that the main spectroscopic features are captured by a simple three-level model without free parameters. Our work shows how a condensate environment can coherently hybridize bound states of different composition and mass, reminiscent of quark-flavor mixing described by the Cabibbo-Kobayashi-Maskawa (CKM) matrix in particle physics.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a chiral generalization of the FHP lattice gas automaton to model fluids exhibiting odd viscosity. By introducing asymmetric scattering rules, the authors show that microscopic parity violation leads directly to macroscopic Hall viscosity ($\eta_H$). The methodology successfully bridges discrete particle dynamics with continuum hydrodynamics via rigorous theoretical expansion and simulation.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical fluid dynamics, the methodology—deriving macroscopic symmetries (or lack thereof) from explicit micro-rules involving broken parity—is highly relevant to understanding symmetry breaking in quantum many-body systems or non-equilibrium quantum field theories.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to understand how microscopic chiral properties, encoded in local particle interactions within a lattice gas automaton, manifest as macroscopic odd-fluid behavior.

**Main result.** The authors successfully constructed a parity-breaking cellular automaton that yields continuum hydrodynamic equations exhibiting non-zero Hall viscosity ($\eta_H$), linking discrete chirality rules to measurable transport coefficients.

**Method.** The derivation involves constructing a chiral generalization of the FHP lattice gas model and employing a Chapman-Enskog expansion on the associated Boltzmann equation to extract macroscopic transport coefficients.

**Model / system.** The system is a 2D triangular lattice modeled by a cellular automaton, which undergoes discrete time evolution via collision (including asymmetric two-body chiral scattering) and streaming steps, with an optional rotation mimicking a magnetic field.

**Key observables.** Odd viscosity ($\eta_H$), shear viscosity ($\eta$), and the full stress tensor components ($\sigma_{ij}$), measured through Poiseuille flow simulations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chirality parameter ($p$) governing collision asymmetry, external magnetic field strength ($B$), and the small expansion parameter ($\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on a Chapman-Enskog expansion in multiple scales ($\epsilon$) and assumes that the system can be coarse-grained into continuum hydrodynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution rules (streaming, 3-body, chiral 2-body collisions) and compare simulated vs. analytically predicted values for shear and Hall viscosities as a function of the chirality parameter $p$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the microscopic chiral automaton dynamics to deriving the continuum hydrodynamic equations via Chapman-Enskog expansion, culminating in analytical predictions and numerical verification of both standard and odd transport coefficients.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Cellular automata are discrete dynamical systems defined on a lattice, in which each site carries a finite set of states that evolve in time according to local deterministic rules. An important application of cellular automata is in lattice gas models of fluids, where the cellular automaton framework provides a particle-based microscopic description of hydrodynamic behavior. The macroscopic fluid equations emerge after coarse-graining over many lattice sites and time steps, offering a bottom-up route to hydrodynamics. A celebrated example is the Frisch-Hasslacher-Pomeau (FHP) model, an automaton defined on a two-dimensional triangular lattice that yields the two-dimensional Navier-Stokes equations upon coarse-graining. In this work, we construct a parity-breaking generalization of the FHP model through two modifications: introducing chiral two-body collision rules and systematically rotating particle velocities to mimic the effect of a background magnetic field. We show that this automaton yields a hydrodynamic model with odd viscosity, a transverse transport coefficient that is a hallmark of odd fluids. We verify the analytical transport coefficients using Poiseuille-flow simulations of the chiral FHP automaton. Our results demonstrate that the chiral automaton introduced here provides a bridge between microscopic parity-breaking scattering processes and macroscopic odd-fluid hydrodynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** The research presents a robust platform for multifunctional quantum sensing using single spins in hexagonal boron nitride (hBN). By independently monitoring the shift of the Zero-Phonon Line (ZPL) with temperature and the Optically Detected Magnetic Resonance (ODMR) frequency with magnetic field, the authors achieve simultaneous, cross-talk-free measurement of both parameters on a chip. This establishes hBN as a promising material for next-generation quantum sensing devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the development of solid-state quantum sensors by engineering an environment (hBN/microcircuit) that allows for orthogonal readout channels, which is crucial for building complex quantum measurement devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The challenge of simultaneously measuring multiple physical parameters, such as temperature and magnetic field, using a single quantum spin sensor due to cross-sensitivity.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated independent dual sensing by showing that the Zero-Phonon Line (ZPL) position shifts with temperature while the Optically Detected Magnetic Resonance (ODMR) frequency changes predictably only with the applied magnetic field.

**Method.** They utilized single quantum emitters hosted in hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) placed on a chip equipped with an integrated microheating circuit to create controlled thermal gradients, while simultaneously measuring both ZPL and ODMR spectra.

**Model / system.** The system involves optically addressable spin defects in hBN. The sensing relies on two distinct physical responses: the temperature dependence of the ZPL position ($\lambda$ vs $T$) and the magnetic field dependence of the ODMR frequency ($
u_{	ext{ODMR}}$ vs $\mathbf{B}$).

**Key observables.** ZPL redshift ($\lambda$ vs $T$), ODMR resonance frequency ($
u_{	ext{ODMR}}$ vs $\mathbf{B}$), single-photon emission ($g^{(2)}(	au)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature uncertainty limits ($\sim 13.4^\circ	ext{C}/\sqrt{	ext{Hz}}$); Magnetic field sensitivity ($\sim 3.0 \mu	ext{T}/\sqrt{	ext{Hz}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The magnetic field is assumed uniform across the measured area, although localized temperature gradients can be generated via Joule heating.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the concept of dual sensing using ZPL and ODMR shifts. Figure 2 details the microscale heating circuit fabrication and simulated temperature/magnetic field distributions. Figure 3 characterizes the emitters and shows ZPL vs T fits, while Figure 4 presents the independent thermal (ZPL) and magnetic (ODMR) responses.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need for multifunctional quantum sensors; it details the experimental setup using hBN on a microheater; it presents measurements showing the distinct temperature dependence of ZPL and the field dependence of ODMR; finally, it demonstrates simultaneous local sensing capability.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nanoscale thermometry and magnetometry are in high demand across a wide range of scientific and technological applications. In this context, optically addressable spins in solids have emerged at the forefront of on-chip quantum sensing. However, simultaneous quantum sensing of multiple parameters (e.g., temperature and magnetic field) using the same spin sensor remains challenging due to cross-sensitivity to multiple physical quantities. Here, we demonstrate independent dual sensing of temperature and magnetic field using single quantum emitters in hexagonal boron nitride (hBN). We experimentally verify the independent response of the zero-phonon line (ZPL) position to temperature and of optically detected magnetic resonance (ODMR) to magnetic fields. Furthermore, we demonstrate local temperature sensing of a microcircuit while simultaneously measuring an external magnetic field. Our results establish quantum emitters in hBN as a robust platform for multifunctional quantum sensing under realistic operating conditions.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes a quasi-1D chain derived from the Kitaev honeycomb model to study its topological phases. The authors find a transition controlled by anisotropy, leading to symmetry-protected zero-energy edge modes in the topological phase. Key diagnostics include measuring the winding number and observing characteristic broad continua in the spin correlation function.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, reduced-dimensional model exhibiting chiral topological order and fractionalization, offering clear spectroscopic fingerprints that can be compared against experimental measurements on quantum materials or engineered spin systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates fractional excitations and topological properties within a quasi-one-dimensional spin chain derived from truncating the 2D Kitaev honeycomb model.

**Main result.** The system exhibits a chiral topological phase transition characterized by the emergence of zero-energy edge modes in the topological regime, alongside broad continua indicative of fractionalized excitations.

**Method.** The analysis employs Majorana fermion mapping to solve the Hamiltonian and uses DMRG simulations to compute the dynamical spin correlation function for spectral diagnostics.

**Model / system.** The model is a quasi-1D Kitaev-like spin chain built from coupled hexagonal plaquettes, retaining essential features of the 2D Kitaev liquid. It is analyzed using an anisotropic nearest-neighbor coupling Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Zero-energy edge modes (protected by chiral symmetry), quantized winding number ($
u$), and broad continua in the dynamical spin correlation function $S_{+}(k, \omega)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The anisotropy parameter ($J_r$) controls the topological phase transition at a critical value of $\sqrt{2}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that truncating the honeycomb lattice yields an integrable model solvable via Majorana fermion representation and that fixing gauge configurations allows diagonalization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic chain structure, the band dispersion showing gap closing at $J_r = \sqrt{2}$, and the emergence of zero-energy edge states in the topological regime under open boundary conditions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the quasi-1D Hamiltonian and its conserved quantities to mapping it onto Majorana fermions. It then uses topological invariants (winding number) and numerical simulations (DMRG) to characterize the phase transition, culminating in spectral analysis of fractionalized excitations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Kitaev honeycomb model has attracted significant interest due to its quantum spin liquid ground state, fractionalized Majorana excitations, and topological properties. Motivated by these features, we introduce a quasi-one-dimensional Kitaev-like spin chain derived from a truncated honeycomb geometry. The resulting Majorana band structure contains both dispersive and flat bands, with a gap closing at a critical anisotropy that separates trivial and chiral topological phases. Under open boundary conditions, the topological regime hosts zero-energy edge modes protected by chiral symmetry, placing the system in the BDI symmetry class with a quantized winding number. In the excited spectrum, localized plaquette modes emerge near zero energy and can be tuned by introducing domains of negative plaquettes. The dynamical spin correlation function reveals broad continua associated with fractionalized excitations, together with characteristic low-energy spectral weight from edge Majorana modes. These features distinguish the present system from conventional Heisenberg spin chains and provide experimentally relevant fingerprints of chiral topological order. Our model thus establishes a conceptual bridge between the two-dimensional Kitaev spin liquid and Kitaev's one-dimensional quantum wire.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops generalized analytical methods to calculate the fidelity loss of quantum gates when dissipation rates vary with time. By applying these techniques to superconducting qubits, they provide a quantitative measure showing that dynamic flux-dependent noise significantly limits gate performance in tunable architectures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides essential theoretical tools for quantifying error budgets in modern tunable quantum hardware by rigorously accounting for how noise sources change over time during computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is deriving rigorous fidelity bounds for quantum operations, particularly adiabatic gates, when the system undergoes time-dependent dissipation.

**Main result.** The authors generalize previous analytical formulae to incorporate time-varying dissipation rates, demonstrating that flux-dependent noise significantly degrades the fidelity of superconducting CZ gates.

**Method.** They employ perturbative expansions (like Dyson series) on the Lindblad master equation formalism and utilize frame transformations to simplify the calculation of the average gate fidelity ($ar{F}$).

**Model / system.** The work models quantum computing platforms, specifically using a three-mode superconducting circuit with tunable couplers. The evolution is governed by a time-dependent Hamiltonian and a Lindblad master equation incorporating time-dependent dissipation rates $\Gamma_k(t)$.

**Key observables.** Average Gate Fidelity ($ar{F}$), fidelity reduction rate ($	ext{d}ar{F}/	ext{dt}$), leakage metrics ($\Lambda_{	ext{total}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time-dependent dissipation rates $\Gamma_k(t)$, coupling strengths ($g_{ij}$), detunings ($\Delta_{ic}$), and the adiabatic evolution time scale ($	au$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on weak dissipation approximations (perturbative expansion) and, in some sections, assumes that the computational basis aligns with the instantaneous eigenstates of the Hamiltonian.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup, comparing fidelity decay using different methods, showing coupler frequency modulation effects, eigenstate hybridization patterns, and quantifying flux-noise sensitivity.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from generalizing the master equation to include time-dependent rates, deriving the general first-order fidelity formula, applying this to adiabatic evolution via frame transformations, and finally demonstrating the results on a superconducting CZ gate model.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

As quantum-computing platforms are susceptible to noise, the fidelity of quantum operations is limited by decoherence. Understanding this limitation is crucial for building utility-scale quantum processors. In previous works [Phys. Rev. Lett. 129, 150504 (2022); Quantum 9, 1684 (2025)], we presented analytical formulae for the average gate fidelity of multi-qubit operations under static Markovian noise processes, including operations that temporarily leave the computational subspace. However, some quantum-computing architectures dynamically modulate qubit or coupler frequencies to implement two-qubit gates, e.g., baseband flux gates; such modulation can lead to dissipation rates varying in time. In this Letter, we therefore generalize the fidelity-reduction formulae to encompass time-dependent dissipation. Applying our generalized formula, we obtain a fidelity bound for adiabatic operations and demonstrate that flux-dependent noise sensitivity, combined with qubit-coupler hybridization, significantly reduces the fidelity of adiabatic controlled-Z (CZ) gates in superconducting quantum computers. Our work thus provides essential theoretical tools for evaluating error budgets and optimizing the design of quantum operations in tunable quantum-computing architectures, and may also find applications in quantum-sensing and quantum-communication protocols that are affected by time-dependent dissipation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reconstructs quantum dynamics by starting only with the axioms of Hilbert spaces and the principle that time evolution must preserve the possible outcomes (spectra) of measurements. By applying Lax pair theory, the authors prove that this single assumption is enough to necessitate the existence of a Hamiltonian and derive the standard Schrödinger and Heisenberg equations as necessary consequences.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly abstract, foundational re-derivation of quantum mechanics that bypasses the need to postulate the Schrödinger equation directly, focusing instead on symmetries implied by measurement consistency.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to reconstruct the axioms of unitary quantum dynamics from a minimal set of principles, specifically relying on the preservation of measurement outcomes (spectra) during time evolution.

**Main result.** It proves that assuming isospectral evolution is sufficient to force the dynamics into the Lax pair form and derive fundamental equations like the Heisenberg equation and Schrödinger equation as theorems rather than postulates.

**Method.** The derivation uses axiomatic quantum mechanics combined with the mathematical machinery of Lax pair theory, showing how spectral preservation constrains the time evolution operator to be unitary.

**Model / system.** The model is general quantum systems described by a complex Hilbert space ($\mathcal{H}$) and governed by self-adjoint observables. The dynamics are analyzed through the lens of isospectral flow rather than assuming a Hamiltonian from the start.

**Key observables.** Observables ($A$), energy eigenvalues ($E$), good quantum numbers, purity ($	ext{Tr}ho^2$), and von Neumann entropy ($S$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Planck's constant ($\hbar$) and time ($t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is Axiom A3: physical time evolution must preserve the spectrum of every observable, $	ext{spec} \, A(t) = 	ext{spec} \, A(0)$.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds logically by establishing axioms (Hilbert space, Hermitian observables, isospectral evolution), deriving the Heisenberg equation via spectral theory, and finally using Stone's theorem to identify the Hamiltonian as the generator of the unitary flow.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We reconstruct unitary quantum dynamics from a minimal axiomatic foundation built on Hilbert-space observables and isospectral evolution. The only dynamical assumption is that physical time evolution is a continuous one-parameter flow of Hermitian observables that preserves their spectra, i.e. the possible outcomes of measurement. We show that this assumption is already sufficient to force the Lax form of quantum dynamics. The Heisenberg equation, the time-dependent and time-independent Schrödinger equations, conservation laws, and good quantum numbers then follow as theorems rather than postulates. In this formulation, Lax pair theory supplies the missing dynamical bridge between the measurement structure of a Hilbert space and standard quantum evolution: the Hamiltonian is not assumed, but emerges as the generator required for an isospectral observable flow.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically investigates how the All-In-All-Out (AIAO) magnetic order in pyrochlore iridates realizes a noncollinear analogue of altermagnetism. Using microscopic models and group theory, it demonstrates that this specific octupolar ordering enforces unique symmetry constraints on the electronic band structure. This results in characteristic spin-polarized band splittings at both finite momentum and zero momentum ($\Gamma$ point).

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical framework linking complex noncollinear magnetism (octupolar moments) to observable electronic signatures like momentum-dependent band splittings, which is crucial for understanding correlated topological materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine if noncollinear magnetic orders can realize the momentum-dependent spin splitting characteristic of altermagnets in strongly SOC materials.

**Main result.** The All-In-All-Out (AIAO) state in pyrochlore iridates acts as a noncollinear counterpart to $d$-wave altermagnets, exhibiting both momentum-dependent and zero-momentum band splitting due to symmetry constraints.

**Method.** The study employs microscopic tight-binding models combined with rigorous group-theory analysis and mean-field calculations to analyze the electronic structure under specific magnetic symmetries.

**Model / system.** The system is pyrochlore iridates ($	ext{A}_2	ext{Ir}_2	ext{O}_7$) featuring $	ext{Ir}^{4+}$ ions with strong SOC, modeled using a $j_{	ext{eff}} = 1/2$ tight-binding Hamiltonian on the pyrochlore lattice.

**Key observables.** Spin-splitting patterns in electronic bands (pseudospin splitting), local magnetization density ($\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{r})$) decomposed into multipole moments, and symmetry constraints derived from magnetic space groups.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Strong Spin-Orbit Coupling (SOC), the AIAO order parameter transforming as an $	ext{A}_{	ext{g}}^{-2	ext{g}}$ octupolar moment, and hopping parameters ($t, t'$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes strong SOC leading to $j_{	ext{eff}} = 1/2$ physics, and utilizes mean-field approximations for the electronic structure calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the AIAO magnetic configuration on a tetrahedron, compare band structures (non-magnetic vs. ordered), show local magnetization density isosurfaces with multipole decomposition, and present low-energy effective Hamiltonians derived from symmetry constraints.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the physical model and the AIAO state's symmetry properties; it then uses group theory to derive the symmetry-allowed low-energy Hamiltonian in the $\Gamma_{10}$ subspace; finally, it calculates the resulting electronic band structure splitting and the real-space multipolar character of the magnetic order.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Altermagnets are collinear magnetically ordered states that exhibit momentum-dependent spin splitting in the absence of net magnetization and spin-orbit coupling (SOC). Related spin-splitting patterns, however, can also emerge in noncollinear magnetic systems with large SOC. Here we show, via a microscopic model, that the all-in-all-out (AIAO) state in pyrochlore iridates constitutes a noncollinear counterpart of a $d$-wave altermagnet stabilized by strong SOC. Starting from a microscopic $j_{\mathrm{eff}} = 1/2$ tight-binding model on the pyrochlore lattice, we demonstrate that electronic interactions favor the AIAO phase and analyze its symmetry properties. We show that the AIAO order parameter transforms as an $A_{2g}^{-}$ octupolar magnetic moment, breaking time-reversal symmetry while preserving inversion and zero net magnetization. Using group-theory analysis and mean-field calculations, we demonstrate that this symmetry enforces both a spin-polarized momentum-dependent lifting of band degeneracies that is similar to that of a collinear $d$-wave cubic altermagnet, but also a band splitting at zero-momentum. We show that the latter feature is captured by a low-energy model similar to the   Luttinger-Kohn model for cubic semiconductors. Our results identify pyrochlore iridates as a platform for noncollinear counterparts of altermagnetism and provide a general symmetry framework for spin-split phenomena in spin-orbit coupled materials.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates the topological phase transitions in multilayer graphene moiré superlattices formed with hBN substrates. Using Hartree-Fock calculations, the authors demonstrate that the alignment of the hBN substrate acts as a remote electrostatic template. This template dictates whether the system stabilizes a topologically non-trivial Chern insulator or a trivial state by shaping the valence band charge texture.

**Why it may be interesting.** This research provides a concrete example of how substrate engineering (hBN alignment) can exert long-range control over emergent quantum topological properties (Chern insulators) in 2D materials, relevant to designing novel correlated electronic devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To understand how charge imprinting biases the topology of correlated insulators in rhombohedral multilayer graphene (RMG) when aligned with hexagonal boron nitride (hBN), particularly when active carriers are displaced from the moiré interface.

**Main result.** The hBN alignment dictates the topological state: specific alignments favor a honeycomb-like charge network associated with Chern insulators, while others favor triangular patterns linked to trivial insulators. This establishes valence-band charge textures as a remote control mechanism for correlated topology.

**Method.** Systematic Hartree-Fock (HF) calculations are performed across various parameters (layer number, twist angle, alignment type) to map out the correlated phase diagram and analyze real-space charge densities.

**Model / system.** The system is RMG aligned with hBN, modeled using a continuum Hamiltonian incorporating moiré potentials ($V_{	ext{hBN}}$). The electronic structure is analyzed via HF calculations that treat electron-electron interactions self-consistently.

**Key observables.** Topological state (Chern number $C=0$ vs $C=1$), real-space charge density patterns (honeycomb vs. triangular), and the energy difference ($\Delta E$) between competing topological phases.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Layer number ($N \simeq 6$), small twist angles ($	heta$), filling factor ($
u=1$), and the hBN alignment parameter ($\xi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on HF approximations, treating long-range Coulomb interactions via a remote electrostatic template mechanism. The study assumes specific alignments dictate charge texture.

**Figures summary.** Figures show phase diagrams mapping topological states across $	heta$ and $D$, alongside real-space charge density profiles comparing the honeycomb pattern ($\xi=0$) to the triangular pattern ($\xi=-1$).

**Paper structure.** The work progresses by first defining the Hamiltonian incorporating moiré potentials, then systematically varying parameters ($N, 	heta, D, \xi$) using HF calculations. The core argument builds from observing that charge imprinting dictates topology in the $	ext{moiré}$-distant regime.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Rhombohedral multilayer graphene aligned with hexagonal boron nitride (RMG-hBN) hosts correlated Chern phases, but the microscopic role of hBN stacking remains unclear, especially when the active carriers are displaced away from the moiré interface. Using Hartree-Fock calculations over layer numbers, twist angles, displacement fields, fillings, and hBN alignments, we show that correlated insulators are most robust at small twist angles and intermediate layer number ($N\simeq 6$), where bandwidth suppression is balanced by layer delocalization of the wavefunctions of the active carriers. Under moiré-distant conditions at filling $ν=1$, the topology of the insulating state is strongly biased by charge imprinting: the hBN alignment shapes the occupied valence-band charge texture near the interface via moiré potential, which acts through long-range Coulomb interactions as a remote electrostatic template for doped conduction electrons. Depending on the alignment, this template favors either triangular charge localization associated with trivial insulators or honeycomb-like charge networks compatible with Chern insulators. Our results identify valence-band charge textures as a microscopic route by which a remote moiré interface controls correlated topology in multilayer graphene.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces topological quantum interferometry using the exchange Berry phase (BPX) to characterize high-dimensional quantum states generated by structured light. It shows that the resulting spatial interference patterns contain topological invariants that can non-tomographically estimate the state's dimensionality. This offers a scalable and robust method for quantum metrology and network applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, device-independent tool for characterizing complex quantum states (high-dimensional entanglement) using purely geometric principles derived from interference patterns, which is highly relevant for scalable quantum network characterization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing methods to characterize high-dimensional quantum states using topological principles, overcoming limitations of traditional characterization techniques that assume ideal experimental conditions.

**Main result.** The exchange Berry phase (BPX) acts as a deterministic control parameter whose associated phase singularities serve as a non-tomographic witness for estimating the state's dimensionality.

**Method.** The authors establish a formalism based on topological quantum interferometry, generalizing $q$-plate state generation and characterization to arbitrary detuning conditions. They calculate Bell state probabilities and visibility using geometric phases derived from the BPX.

**Model / system.** The system involves structured light interacting via two-photon interference at a beamsplitter, utilizing q-plates whose action is described by unitary transformations involving topological charges ($q$) and detunings ($\delta$).

**Key observables.** Bell state probabilities ($\mathcal{P}_{\Phi^\pm}, \mathcal{P}_{\Psi^\pm}$), interference visibility ($
u$), and the net topological winding number ($W$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Topological charges ($q_a, q_b$), detunings ($\delta_a, \delta_b$), and spatial coordinates ($	heta_c, 	heta_d$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on decomposing the state into fundamental modes governed by geometry, and some calculations simplify the radial dependence of the field components.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the conceptual framework of interference patterns dictated by BPX, show phase evolution across Bell basis states under varying detuning/charge, and map out spatial probability distributions for different input polarizations.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the physical model (q-plates and structured light) to deriving the mathematical formalism using geometric phases ($	ext{BPX}$). It then applies this framework to calculate observable quantities like Bell state probabilities and visibility, culminating in demonstrating how topological invariants provide a dimensionality witness.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Structured light provides high-dimensional Hilbert spaces holding tremendous potential for fundamental quantum optics and quantum technologies. However, existing characterization methods, like Hong-Ou-Mandel (HOM) interference, typically assume perfectly tuned conditions, overlooking the geometric physics governing spatial mode evolution. Here, we establish topological quantum interferometry driven by an interaction-based geometric phase, the exchange Berry phase (BPX). Our formalism generalizes $q$-plate state generation and characterization to arbitrary topological charges and (de)tuning conditions, demonstrating that BPX acts as a geometric marker governing spatial interference. We show BPX serves as a deterministic control parameter, decomposing two-photon spatial patterns into geometry-dictated fundamental modes. This mapping reveals topological invariants and phase singularities that function as a non-tomographic witness for state dimensionality estimation, circumventing full-state reconstruction. Being device-independent and highly scalable, this approach enables scalable high-dimensional characterization and topologically protected state selection, with direct applicability to quantum metrology and high-capacity quantum networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically explores unconventional superconductivity in the extended Hubbard model by incorporating multiple nonlocal interactions. Using exact diagonalization and PQMC, the authors demonstrate that these interactions dictate a complex interplay between s-wave and d-wave pairing symmetries. The findings emphasize that realistic descriptions of strongly correlated materials require going significantly beyond the minimal Hubbard Hamiltonian.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides detailed theoretical insight into how extending the Hubbard model with realistic nonlocal terms can fundamentally alter the predicted superconducting pairing symmetry and strength, which is crucial for understanding high-Tc superconductors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how various nonlocal interactions influence superconducting correlations (s-wave and d-wave) within the extended Hubbard model.

**Main result.** Nonlocal interactions have a highly nontrivial, symmetry-dependent effect: $t_n$ enhances d-wave pairing with on-site repulsion, while correlated hopping ($t_c$) strongly promotes s-wave correlations.

**Method.** The research employs Lanczos exact diagonalization on small clusters and supplements results using Projector Quantum Monte Carlo simulations to analyze pair correlation functions.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by the extended Hubbard model, incorporating terms for nearest/next-nearest neighbor hopping ($t, t_n$), on-site repulsion ($U$), and several nonlocal interactions like nearest-neighbor Coulomb interaction ($V$), correlated hopping ($t_c$), exchange ($J$), and pair hopping ($J_c$).

**Key observables.** Equal-time pair–pair correlation functions for s-wave ($C_s$) and d-wave ($C_d$) symmetries, as well as vertex correlation functions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strengths $U, V, t_c, J, J_c$, hopping amplitudes $t, t_n$. The analysis explores regimes where these terms are varied relative to each other.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitations include the small system size accessible via exact diagonalization ($4	imes 4$ cluster), though PQMC helps extend the regime of study. It assumes that a comprehensive treatment beyond the minimal Hubbard model is necessary for realism.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of $C_s$ and $C_d$ on various parameters (e.g., $U, t_n, J_c$), showing non-monotonic behavior, enhancement, or suppression based on the specific interaction term being varied.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the effect of each nonlocal interaction term ($V, t_c, J, J_c$) individually on pairing correlations. It culminates by examining the combined effects of all interactions to map out a complex phase diagram showing competition between symmetries.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the influence of nonlocal interactions on superconducting correlations within the extended Hubbard model. In addition to the on-site Coulomb interaction and nearest-neighbor hopping, we include next-nearest-neighbor hopping together with several physically relevant nonlocal terms, namely nearest-neighbor Coulomb interaction, correlated hopping, exchange interaction, and pair hopping. Using Lanczos exact diagonalization on a $4\times4$ cluster, supported by projector quantum Monte Carlo simulations for selected parameter regimes, we analyze pairing correlations in both the s- and d-wave channels. We demonstrate that nonlocal interactions exert a highly nontrivial and symmetry-dependent influence on superconducting correlations. While the on-site repulsion in cooperation with next-nearest-neighbor hopping enhances d-wave pairing tendencies, correlated hopping and nearest-neighbor Coulomb interaction strongly promote s-wave correlations, whereas exchange and pair-hopping interactions can efficiently suppress superconductivity beyond relatively small critical strengths. When all nonlocal interactions are considered simultaneously, the resulting phase diagrams reveal a complex interplay and competition between different pairing symmetries. Our results highlight the crucial role of extended interactions in shaping the pairing landscape of strongly correlated systems and demonstrate that a comprehensive treatment beyond the minimal Hubbard model is essential for a realistic description of unconventional superconductivity.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a rigorous theoretical framework to measure how well heralded quantum linear optical circuits can generate target quantum states. By analyzing the Jacobian rank and ensemble properties (frame potentials), it quantifies the added expressivity gained by incorporating conditional measurements over simple passive optics. This provides crucial, systematic criteria for designing high-dimensional photonic quantum state engineering protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides powerful mathematical tools—Jacobian rank and frame potentials—to quantitatively assess the fundamental limits of quantum state preparation using linear optics, which is a cornerstone platform in photonic quantum computing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work investigates the local controllability of photonic states generated by linear optical networks, specifically comparing passive vs. heralded schemes.

**Main result.** The authors establish systematic criteria using Jacobian rank analysis to quantify how ancillary resources in heralded optics enlarge the accessible state space beyond what is possible with passive optics.

**Method.** The primary method involves analyzing the rank of the Jacobian matrix of the output state with respect to the underlying unitary circuit, which measures the dimension of the accessible tangent space.

**Model / system.** The system under study is photonic linear optical networks, encompassing both purely passive evolution and heralded schemes involving conditional measurements on auxiliary modes. The analysis uses concepts from Lie algebra and geometric measure theory applied to quantum state manifolds.

**Key observables.** Jacobian rank ($R_{HLO}$), Frame Potentials ($F(\mu)_t$), Infidelity ($L(U')$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of modes ($m, m'$), number of photons ($n, n'$), and the nature/availability of ancillary resources (heralding patterns $|hangle$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes generic conditions for unitary matrices or relies on topological arguments that suggest global reachability if local rank is maximal.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematics comparing passive and heralded linear optics setups, and numerical results show the relative difference in frame potentials between PLO and HLO settings.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the limitations of passive optics to developing a rigorous Jacobian rank analysis for heralded schemes. It establishes upper bounds via symmetry arguments, derives lower bounds through specific configurations, and finally uses ensemble methods (frame potentials) to test global expressivity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Photonic linear optical networks provide a versatile platform for quantum information processing and quantum state engineering. However, the set of states that can be generated using passive linear optics alone is fundamentally constrained by bosonic symmetries. Heralding, based on conditional measurements on auxiliary modes, is a widely used technique to overcome these limitations and effectively enlarge the set of accessible states. Despite the widespread use of heralding, it is often unclear how specific ancillary resources impact the overall reachability of the target space. In this work, we investigate the local controllability of photonic states in linear optical networks by analyzing the rank of the Jacobian of the output state with respect to the underlying unitary circuit, which provides a quantitative measure of the dimension of the accessible tangent space at a given configuration. Our analysis ranges from passive linear optics to heralded linear optics, where auxiliary resources and conditional measurements are included. Within this framework, we quantify how different resources enlarge the locally accessible state space beyond that of passive linear optics and determine the resources required for the Jacobian rank to reach its maximal value, thereby achieving full local controllability. As maximal local rank is a necessary condition for global reachability, our framework offers a systematic tool to assess and compare the accessible state space of measurement-based photonic architectures, and to establish practical criteria for the resources needed in high-dimensional quantum state engineering.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses SQUID microscopy and STM on Kagome superconductors ($	ext{CsV}_3	ext{Sb}_{5-x}	ext{Sn}_x$) to map supercurrent flow. The key finding is that supercurrents are confined to narrow, stable channels, suggesting the superconducting state is governed by an intrinsic Josephson junction network competing with charge density wave order. This provides new local insight into strongly correlated electron behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong spatial localization of supercurrents and the evidence for competing orders (CDW) provide a concrete experimental platform for studying complex many-body interactions beyond simple BCS theory, which is highly relevant to correlated electron physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to understand the anomalous transport properties of Kagome superconductors, particularly how supercurrents are spatially distributed and influenced by competing orders like Charge Density Waves (CDW).

**Main result.** Supercurrent flow is found to be highly confined to narrow, stable channels that emerge below the critical temperature, suggesting an intrinsic Josephson junction network rather than simple edge effects.

**Method.** The research employs advanced nanoscale characterization techniques including Superconducting Quantum Interference Device (SQUID) microscopy and Scanning Tunneling Microscopy (STM).

**Model / system.** The physical system is the $	ext{AV}_3	ext{Sb}_5$ ($	ext{A} = 	ext{Cs}, 	ext{K}, 	ext{Rb}$) family of Kagome superconductors, specifically doped flakes like $	ext{CsV}_3	ext{Sb}_{5-x}	ext{Sn}_x$. The analysis compares observed transport to models involving Josephson junctions and competing CDW order.

**Key observables.** Supercurrent interference patterns, spatially confined current channels, superconducting energy gap ($\Delta^*$), and non-linear current-voltage characteristics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Doping strength ($x$), temperature (especially near $T_c$), and applied bias/injected current ($I_{	ext{inj}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The observed supercurrent anomalies are attributed to Josephson coupling within a network of narrow filaments, ruling out simple depairing mechanisms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the confinement of supercurrents and Meissner screening to narrow channels using SQUID microscopy across temperature changes, alongside STM spectra confirming the superconducting gap structure.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying anomalous transport features in Kagome superconductors, then using SQUID/STM imaging to map current flow spatially, arguing that this confinement points toward an intrinsic Josephson junction network linked to charge order physics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Superconductors are many-body quantum states in which current flows without dissipation. Theory predicts that supercurrents follow a relatively simple spatial pattern in both conventional and unconventional superconductors. Recent studies into the AV3Sb5 (A = Cs, K, Rb) family of Kagome superconductors indicate that CsV3Sb5 has unconventional transport properties that cannot be accounted for with these simple theories, including reports of intrinsic Josephson junctions, higher order Cooper pairing and the zero field diode effect. Attempts to interpret these findings have focused on the interplay of superconductivity with the unconventional charge density wave (CDW) order in these materials, with which superconductivity competes. A current roadblock to understanding how these kagome superconductors give rise to their intriguing properties is the lack of spatially resolved information about transport. Here we show, using a recently developed superconducting quantum interference device (SQUID) microscope, that flakes of CsV3Sb5-xSnx host a network of narrow supercurrent channels. These supercurrent channels emerge at the critical temperature and remain stable for all temperatures and currents. Their non-linear behaviour is consistent with a network of Josephson junctions linked by narrow supercurrent filaments, which naturally leads to the observed transport anomalies. Intriguingly, these observations are much weaker in undoped samples, which suggests links to the physics of charge density waves, disorder, and electronic correlations, all of which are greatly influenced by the doping strength. These results establish new frontiers for the local investigation of charge transport and competing orders in strongly correlated electron systems, and shine a new light on the anomalous transport properties of the AV3Sb5 kagome superconductors.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes four distinct 1+1D quantum models—including Ising, Schwinger, Blume-Capel, and clock models—to study the Yang-Lee phase transition. Using advanced field-theoretic methods, the authors demonstrate that these diverse physical systems all map onto a universal critical theory described by an i\phi^3 massless bosonic field. This confirms deep connections between symmetry protection, quantum criticality, and conformal field theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous connection drawn between diverse physical lattice models (Ising, clock) and a single universal critical theory ($i\phi^3$ CFT) provides powerful insights into how symmetry constraints dictate quantum criticality in condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates quantum models that exhibit the Yang-Lee (YL) phase transition under deformations that preserve PT symmetry.

**Main result.** In all four studied quantum models, the YL criticality is consistently described by a massless Euclidean i\phi^3 field theory in the continuum limit.

**Method.** The authors employ advanced theoretical techniques including state-operator correspondence, bosonization, and the Polyakov-Hubbard transformation to map microscopic Hamiltonians onto effective bosonic field theories.

**Model / system.** Four specific 1+1D quantum models are analyzed: the antiferromagnetic Ising spin chain (with transverse/longitudinal fields), the massive Schwinger model, the Blume-Capel model, and the three-state quantum clock model. These systems are deformed to induce YL criticality.

**Key observables.** Scaling dimensions ($\Delta$), effective central charge ($c_{	ext{eff}}$), two-point correlation functions $\langle \phi(0)\phi(n) angle$, and pseudo-critical points ($h^*$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality $1+1$D, the imaginary coupling constant (related to the YL singularity), and system size $N$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Approximations include using the state-operator correspondence and assuming that low-energy physics is governed by an effective continuum field theory description.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams, spectral flow showing crossings between different critical points (Ising vs. YL), and numerical results for correlation functions comparing theoretical power laws to exponential decays.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes multiple models, deriving effective LGW actions via transformations, calculating scaling dimensions using finite-size scaling on the low-energy spectrum, and confirming the underlying CFT description of the critical point.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this article, we present four different $1+1$D quantum models that realize the Yang-Lee (YL) phase transition under a deformation that preserves $PT$ symmetry. These are the antiferromagnetic Ising spin chain in transverse and longitudinal magnetic fields, the massive Schwinger model, the Blume-Capel model, and the three-state quantum clock model. Using the state-operator correspondence, we identify the YL critical point, compute the scaling dimensions of the lowest operators in each model, and find perfect agreement with the exact results for the YL criticality in two dimensions. Using bosonization for the Schwinger model and the Polyakov-Hubbard transformation for the other models, we show that in all of these quantum models the YL critical point is described, as expected, by a massless bosonic field with an $i φ^3$ interaction. In the quantum clock model, this critical field interacts with a massive bosonic field, and we identify the massless and massive states in the Hamiltonian spectrum. In addition, we numerically compute the two-point function of $φ$ at the Yang-Lee critical point and show that it grows with distance, in agreement with theoretical expectations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates how two types of quantum algebraic deformations ($q$ and $h$) modify the states of multi-qubit systems governed by the Kittel–Shore model. By calculating quantum fidelity, the authors show that while $q$-deformation causes a smooth decay in overlap, $h$-deformation leads to rapid state orthogonality. This comparison offers crucial theoretical guidance for interpreting experimental results on highly entangled quantum resources.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous mathematical bridge between abstract quantum group theory ($\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{sl}(2, \mathbb{R}))$ deformations) and concrete, measurable quantities (quantum state fidelities) relevant for benchmarking current multi-qubit quantum hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work explores how standard ($q$-deformation) and non-standard ($h$-deformation) quantum deformations of the $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{sl}(2, \mathbb{R}))$ Hopf algebra affect multi-qubit systems.

**Main result.** The $q$-deformation smoothly modifies quantum states while maintaining residual overlap with undeformed configurations, whereas the $h$-deformation rapidly drives these states toward orthogonality. Both require specific parameter rescalings to maintain fidelity in the macroscopic limit.

**Method.** The authors construct multi-qubit states using Clebsch-Gordan coefficients associated with the deformed algebras and quantify state deviation by computing quantum fidelity ($F$).

**Model / system.** The study focuses on multi-qubit systems, analyzing states related to the Kittel–Shore (KS) model Hamiltonian. The algebraic framework involves Hopf algebra deformations of $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{sl}(2, \mathbb{R}))$ using parameters $q$ and $h$.

**Key observables.** Quantum fidelity ($F$), Dicke states $|D(k)_Nangle$, component probabilities in the computational basis.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Deformation parameters $q$ (standard) and $h$ (Jordanian); system size $N$; thermodynamic limit ($N 	o \infty$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that comparing fidelities provides a valid benchmark for operational errors, and specific scaling limits ($\eta = 	ilde{\eta}/N$) are required to obtain finite results in the thermodynamic limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare raw experimental data against theoretical predictions for both $q$- and $h$-deformations across 2-qubit and 3-qubit systems, illustrating fidelity decay versus deformation parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the algebraic framework (Hopf algebra deformations) to deriving deformed Hamiltonians and states. It then compares theoretical predictions for few-body and macroscopic limits using fidelity calculations, culminating in a discussion of which deformation best models observed quantum correlations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This work explores the effects of both the standard quantum $q$-deformation and the non-standard $h$-deformation of the Hopf algebra $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{sl}(2, \mathbb{R}))$ on multi-qubit systems. By constructing the states of a Hilbert space of $N$ qubits through the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients associated with the deformed algebras, we show that these states naturally coincide with the eigenstates of the Hamiltonian of the $q$- and $h$-deformed Kittel-Shore models. We compare the resulting deformed states with those typically targeted in quantum information experiments, providing a bridge between algebraic constructions and experimentally relevant quantum resources. Fidelities with respect to the undeformed states are computed to establish how the quantum correlations are affected, both for few-qubit systems (including Dicke and non-Dicke states), and in the macroscopic limit ($N \to \infty$) through closed-form formulas derived for arbitrary Dicke states. The results reveal different behaviors between the two deformations. The $q$-deformation smoothly modifies the states and maintains a residual overlap with the original configurations, while the $h$-deformation rapidly makes the states orthogonal to their undeformed counterparts. Both models demand a standard $N^{-1}$ rescaling to preserve fidelity stability in the macroscopic limit.

</details>

### [Scaling, fractal dynamics, and critical exponents in the equilibrium phase transition](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20460v1)

**Authors:** Adauto F. Souza, Henrique A Lima, Anderson L. R. Barbosa, Fernando A. Oliveira  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.20460v1>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the deep geometric connections between scaling behavior, critical exponents, and fractal dimensions at equilibrium phase transitions. Using fractional calculus applied to correlation functions, the authors establish quantitative relationships between these quantities for models like the Ising and Potts systems. It provides a powerful theoretical framework that unifies several key concepts in critical phenomena theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the use of fractal geometry and generalized differential operators provides advanced mathematical tools for characterizing critical phenomena that can inspire analogous treatments in quantum many-body systems near continuous phase transitions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish a unified geometric interpretation connecting scaling behavior, critical exponents, and fractal geometry in equilibrium phase transitions.

**Main result.** The study derives relationships between critical exponents ($\eta$, $
u$) and fractal dimensions ($d_R$, $d_f$), successfully reproducing known results for several lattice models like the 2D Ising system.

**Method.** The authors employ fractional differential analysis, extending Fisher's correlation function approach to incorporate Riesz fractional derivatives, linking it to renormalization group concepts.

**Model / system.** The research analyzes thermodynamic systems undergoing second-order phase transitions, testing its framework on the q-state Potts model (generalizing Ising), XY, and Heisenberg spin systems.

**Key observables.** Autocorrelation function $C(r)$, critical exponents ($\eta$, $
u$, $\alpha$, $eta$, $\gamma$), and fractal dimensions ($d_R$, $d_f$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality of the space ($d$), the number of states in the Potts model ($q$), and the fractional derivative order ($\zeta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach is limited to systems with local order parameters, failing for topological phase transitions like the 2D XY model. It also relies on specific scaling relations connecting various exponents.

**Figures summary.** Table I presents calculated values for critical exponents and fractal dimensions for the 2D Potts model across different $q$. Table II and III provide similar data for other models, comparing results with literature values.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon Fisher's correlation function analysis by introducing fractional calculus. It derives key scaling relations linking $\eta$ to geometric parameters ($d_R$) and then verifies these relationships across multiple lattice models (Ising, Potts, XY, Heisenberg).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Statistical methods are essential for understanding thermodynamic systems with many degrees of freedom. For systems in equilibrium, a very useful method is that of correlation functions, which establish a correlation between a field phi(x), which depends on the spatial position x, and the same field evaluated at another position, phi(x0). Fisher [Journal of Mathematical Physics 5, 944322 (1964)] introduced the autocorrelation function for fluctuations of the order parameter, which has been an important mathematical tool for understanding second-order phase transitions in equilibrium. However, his analysis is restricted to a Euclidean space of dimension d, and an exponent eta is introduced to correct the spatial behavior of the correlation function at T = Tc. In a recent work, Lima et al. [Phys. Rev. E 110, L062107 (2024)] demonstrated that a modern fractional differential analysis is necessary for a complete description of the correlation function at Tc. In this study, we highlight the deep connection among scaling behavior, critical exponents, and fractal geometry. Our results provide a unified geometric interpretation of critical exponents and fractal dimensions, broadly applicable to thermodynamic phase transitions. However, the approach does not apply to topological phase transitions, which lack local order parameters and the associated scale-invariant fractal geometry. We verify its predictions for several cornerstone thermodynamic models: the Ising, Potts, XY, and Heisenberg systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This review surveys the field of indefinite quantum causality, proposing that physical processes can operate when the order of operations is in superposition. It introduces the process matrix formalism to mathematically describe these non-standard causal relationships. The work establishes that this indeterminacy is a quantifiable resource with potential applications in quantum computation and information theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful theoretical toolset for analyzing quantum processes where temporal or causal assumptions break down, which is relevant for understanding open systems dynamics in curved spacetime or complex quantum networks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses quantum processes where the causal order of operations is not fixed, moving beyond standard sequential circuit models.

**Main result.** Indefinite causal order has been established as a feasible and potentially advantageous resource for quantum information processing tasks.

**Method.** It develops formalisms like the process matrix formalism to describe joint probabilities when the causal sequence of events is itself in superposition.

**Model / system.** The focus is on abstract quantum processes, modeled using Hilbert spaces, density operators, and generalized quantum channels/instruments. Key models include the 'quantum switch' mechanism.

**Key observables.** Joint probabilities $P(a, b)$, process matrices ($W$), and causal witnesses used to detect indefinite order.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relative strengths of different causal structures (e.g., sequential vs. superposed).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that the physical description can be generalized by treating causality itself as a quantum resource, rather than a fixed background structure.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the bipartite quantum switch mechanism and the mathematical relationship between different formalisms (state-channel duality).

**Paper structure.** The review progresses from foundational concepts of indefinite causality to developing generalized probability rules, detailing the process matrix formalism, and discussing experimental certification methods.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In recent years, operational approaches to quantum foundations have been developed as a means of understanding the core principles and distinctive features of quantum theory. Such approaches typically view physical processes as sequences of operations, with earlier operations serving as causes of later effects. However, a growing literature is emerging on the possibility of relaxing this assumption and allowing for quantum indefiniteness in the causal order. This development stems from a variety of motivations, both fundamental and applied, including exploring the role of causality in quantum theory, the interplay between quantum theory and general relativity, and higher-order quantum computing. A prominent offshoot of this development is the emergence of indefinite causal order as a feasible resource for quantum information processing. This review provides an overview of the current state of the art in the field, covering the methodology underlying indefinite quantum causality within the so-called "process matrix formalism", outlining key results and experimental implementations, and discussing recent advances.

</details>

**Summary.** This work applies advanced algebraic topology and geometric analysis to model financial networks as simplicial complexes. The core contribution is using curvature, derived from the heat kernel expansion, as a superior diagnostic tool for detecting structural shocks caused by external laws compared to traditional measures like Euler characteristic or torsion. It demonstrates that this geometric approach accurately pinpoints the timing of regulatory impacts on network structure.

**Why it may be interesting.** While applied to finance, the methodology—using spectral geometry (heat kernel) on high-order complexes to detect non-equilibrium structural changes—is conceptually transferable to analyzing phase transitions or sudden regime shifts in complex quantum many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a robust geometric measure, derived from the heat kernel expansion, capable of detecting structural shocks in high-order financial networks, surpassing the limitations of traditional topological invariants.

**Main result.** Curvature dynamics provide a sensitive indicator for external forcing or legislative changes (shocks), showing distinct features like inflection points and minima that raw network counts fail to capture.

**Method.** The authors employ algebraic topology on simplicial complexes, calculating curvature via the heat kernel expansion of higher-order Laplacians. This geometric measure is compared against standard topological tools like Betti numbers and torsion.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a financial network in Norway over nine years, represented as a simplicial complex where directors are nodes and company affiliations form faces (simplices).

**Key observables.** Curvature ($ar{R}(t)$), Euler characteristic ($\chi$), Reidemeister torsion ($	au_R$), and higher-order clustering coefficients ($ar{C}_k(t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time ($t$) evolution, network order (dimension of the complex), and specific external forcing events (e.g., legislation dates).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that geometric curvature derived from the heat kernel expansion retains local information about structural changes better than integrated topological measures.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the temporal evolution of curvature ($ar{R}(t)$) showing shock markers coinciding with legislation dates, alongside comparisons of $\chi$, torsion, and higher-order clustering coefficients for male/female groups.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the limitations of standard topological measures (Euler characteristic loses local info; torsion is insensitive). It then introduces curvature from the heat kernel expansion as a superior geometric invariant. Finally, it applies this method to analyze real-world financial data, demonstrating its predictive power regarding external shocks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This work follows the evolution of financial networks in Norway over a period of nine years at a monthly rate. The data consist of board directors and their affiliations to companies, which we model as simplicial complexes. In this framework, directors are represented as nodes and companies as faces of the complex. To characterize the latter, we focus on three topological measures: the Euler characteristic, computed through the Betti numbers, torsion computed through the reduced determinant of the higher-order Laplacians, and higher-order clustering coefficients. The first two fail to capture the effect of imposed law on representation, unlike our notion of curvature which is a geometrical measure computed from the coefficients of the series expansion of the heat kernel in powers of time, which is our major contribution in this work. In particular, the Euler characteristic integrates curvature, and thus local information is lost. Subsequently, not every topological measure can reliably capture shocks in networks. Further, the number of spanning trees may undergo significant changes at the lowest order, yet these changes need not be reflected in the torsion. Conversely, the change in the curvature revealed variation in the board interlock due to legislation, and serves as a sensitive measure for detecting shocks in networks. Inflection points in curvature are associated with external forcing, and minima with shock arrival times. Sharp transitions are also observed in the components of torsion, while smooth changes are observed in higher-order clustering.

</details>
