**Summary.** The paper presents directional tile codes as an advanced alternative to surface codes for fault-tolerant quantum computation on planar grids. By dynamically generating required long-range connections using only nearest-neighbour iSWAP gates, these codes maintain the high efficiency of qLDPC while respecting hardware locality. This breakthrough brings low-overhead quantum memory closer to realization in near-term superconducting devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, hardware-aware pathway to realizing high-rate quantum memory by engineering code structures that respect strict 2D planar connectivity constraints inherent in superconducting architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop high-performance quantum low-density parity-check (qLDPC) codes for fault tolerance that overcome the connectivity limitations of surface codes while remaining implementable using only nearest-neighbour gates on planar hardware.

**Main result.** The proposed directional tile codes successfully translate the efficiency advantages of qLDPC codes into strictly planar, nearest-neighbour circuits, achieving logical error rate reductions up to three orders of magnitude compared to rotated surface codes at comparable overheads.

**Method.** The authors introduce 'directional tile codes' implemented on a square grid. Syndrome extraction is achieved dynamically using only nearest-neighbour iSWAP gates via controlled walks that define both the stabilizer supports and perform the measurement.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as superconducting qubits arranged on a planar square grid, utilizing directional tile codes defined by specific parity conditions (CSS code structure). The implementation relies entirely on local nearest-neighbour interactions (iSWAP gates) for syndrome extraction circuits.

**Key observables.** Code efficiency ratio ($\eta_{circ}$), per-logical per-round logical error rate ($p_L$), and circuit qubits required per logical qubit ($n_{circ}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Physical error rate ($p=0.001$); code size parameters like [[323, 14, 15]]; linear scaling of routing overhead $O(d)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that iSWAP interactions can serve as a 'spacetime routing primitive' to define measurements locally; comparisons are made using a uniform circuit-level noise model.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structure of directional tile codes, compare logical error rates vs. physical overheads against surface codes (Figure 2), and detail the optimization process for routing qubits (Figure 7).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the concept of directional tile codes to address the locality/efficiency trade-off. It details the nearest-neighbour syndrome extraction circuit, analyzes the scaling of physical overheads (routing) through optimization techniques, and finally demonstrates performance gains via error rate comparisons and logical operation implementation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

High-performance quantum low-density parity-check codes promise substantial reductions in the overhead of fault-tolerant quantum computation, but most constructions require long-range connectivity or qubit shuttling, both of which are difficult to realise in superconducting architectures. Here we introduce a family of quantum low-density parity-check codes that, for the first time, combines planar open-boundary layouts, finite-size advantages over surface codes, and syndrome extraction using only nearest-neighbour gates on a square grid of qubits. The key idea is to generate check-data connectivity dynamically: nearest-neighbour iSWAP walks both define the stabiliser supports and implement their measurement, avoiding the need for a long-range hardware graph. The resulting circuits achieve optimal constant-depth stabiliser measurement, independent of code size, and naturally remove leakage from the system by exchanging the role of check and data qubits at each syndrome extraction round. We find finite-size instances such as a [[323,14,15]] code, whose code-efficiency ratio is nearly an order of magnitude larger than that of rotated surface-code patches. At around 30 circuit qubits per logical qubit, the best directional tile-code layouts reduce the per-logical per-round logical error rate by up to a factor of 1000 relative to rotated surface-code memories. These results show that the advantages of quantum low-density parity-check codes can survive compilation into strictly planar nearest-neighbour circuits, bringing low-overhead fault-tolerant memories closer to near-term hardware.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper rigorously analyzes the properties of sparse positive maps on qutrits by examining their associated Choi matrices. It determines exact mathematical thresholds that delineate regimes such as positivity, complete positivity, and entanglement types like PPT-entanglement. This provides a highly controlled theoretical framework for understanding quantum resource boundaries.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools to map out complex quantum resource boundaries (like separability vs. bound entanglement) in a controlled, low-dimensional system (qutrits), which is fundamental knowledge for understanding quantum communication protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to explicitly characterize positivity, decomposability, and associated PPT entanglement transitions for a specific family of sparse positive maps acting on qutrits.

**Main result.** The paper derives exact mathematical thresholds defining the boundaries between different physical regimes (e.g., separability vs. bound entanglement) based on the map's parameters.

**Method.** The analysis heavily relies on analyzing the Choi matrix of the quantum map, reducing positivity conditions to solving constraints involving Hermitian biquadratic forms over a simplex.

**Model / system.** The system is defined by a family of linear maps $\Phi: 	ext{B}(\mathbb{C}^3) 	o 	ext{B}(\mathbb{C}^3)$ acting on qutrits. The mathematical structure is parameterized by non-negative real numbers ($a, b, c$) and complex variables ($w, z$).

**Key observables.** Positivity, complete positivity (CP), decomposability thresholds, and the detection of PPT entanglement.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The parameters $a, b, c$ (non-negative real) and $w, z$ (complex) govern the map's properties; specific regimes are analyzed for these variables.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis proceeds by specializing to three distinct parametric families (Case 1, Case 2, and Case 3) to derive explicit boundary functions. It also compares results against spectral bounds for higher-order positivity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the boundaries in parameter space showing regions corresponding to positivity, complete positivity, and decomposability for different parametric cases (e.g., Fig. 1, Fig. 2).

**Paper structure.** The paper first reviews definitions of positive maps, introduces the qutrit map family $\Phi$ and its Choi matrix $C\Phi$, derives general conditions for CP based on the spectrum, and then analyzes specific parametric cases (Case 1, 2, 3) to determine explicit thresholds for decomposability and entanglement.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study a family of sparse positive maps on qutrits for which positivity, decomposability, and PPT entanglement can all be analysed explicitly. The block structure of the associated Choi matrices reduces positivity to a Hermitian biquadratic form and leads to exact positivity boundaries for three representative parametric families. For the same families we determine the exact transition between decomposable and non-decomposable maps and construct associated PPT states of two classes. The first consists of witness-adapted deformations naturally tied to the non-decomposability analysis. The second consists of analytically tractable families whose full PPT-entangled branch is detected by fixed positive maps, yielding exact thresholds between separability and bound entanglement. For the trace-preserving subclass, we further compare positivity with a recent eigenvalue bound for 2-positive maps, thereby making the gap between positivity and higher-order positivity fully explicit within this family.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a robust finite-volume scheme for extracting the continuum limit of the step-scaling function in a Hamiltonian U(1) gauge theory. By carefully controlling the ratio between physical scales during extrapolation, the authors demonstrate stable results free from uncontrolled lattice artifacts. This provides a powerful numerical methodology for studying running couplings in quantum field theories.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on lattice gauge theory, the rigorous application of MPS and the detailed analysis of controlling systematic errors (finite size/truncation) provide advanced numerical techniques applicable to other strongly correlated quantum systems where continuum limits are required.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop and apply a finite-volume scheme to perform controlled continuum extrapolations of the lattice step-scaling function for a Hamiltonian U(1) gauge theory.

**Main result.** The proposed fixed-ratio finite-volume scheme successfully yields a stable continuum limit for the step-scaling function, demonstrating control over systematic errors from both finite volume and local basis truncation.

**Method.** The study employs Matrix Product States (MPS) to simulate the lattice Hamiltonian and uses controlled extrapolation procedures by fixing the ratio between physical reference scales along the continuum sequence ($a 	o 0$).

**Model / system.** The system is a pure U(1) gauge theory in (2+1) dimensions, formulated using a dual Hamiltonian approach derived from Kogut-Susskind lattice gauge theory. The simulation utilizes an operator basis represented by MPS.

**Key observables.** Step-scaling function ($\Sigma_s$), static potential ($V(r)$), force-based renormalized coupling ($g_2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Lattice spacing ($a$), bare coupling constant ($g_2$), local basis dimension ($\Lambda$), and the fixed ratio of physical scales used for extrapolation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that fixing the ratio between reference distance and lattice extent controls finite-volume effects, and convergence is achieved by analyzing systematic errors from both truncation and volume size.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate dualization processes (link to plaquette variables), the necessity of fixing physical ratios for continuum extrapolation, and plots showing potential fits and convergence checks against basis size ($\Lambda$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the Hamiltonian formulation via duality, then detailing the step-scaling procedure, and finally implementing the finite-volume scheme to extrapolate results in a controlled manner while analyzing systematic errors from truncation and volume.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a finite-volume scheme to perform controlled continuum extrapolations of the lattice step-scaling function, a key ingredient for determining the running coupling in a Hamiltonian lattice gauge theory in small volumes. As a testbed, we employ a dual Hamiltonian formulation of pure U(1) gauge theory in (2+1) dimensions and an operator basis that remains efficient toward weak coupling. We describe the implementation of static external charges on the spatial lattice and study, using matrix product states, the resulting confining string, from which we extract the static potential and a force-based renormalized coupling. Using the proposed finite-volume scheme, we demonstrate a stable continuum limit of the step-scaling function on the lattice sizes accessible to present Hamiltonian simulations. The method is readily extendable to other gauge groups and dimensions, providing a pathway toward Hamiltonian step-scaling studies in other theories.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a quantum enhancement for the ring all-reduce primitive used in distributed machine learning. It demonstrates that quantum resources provide provable advantages by halving online communication costs and enabling information-theoretic privacy guarantees unattainable classically. Furthermore, it establishes exponential quantum separations when detecting gradient conflicts.

**Why it may be interesting.** The paper provides a rigorous framework showing how fundamental quantum communication primitives (like entanglement assistance) can solve resource limitations in large-scale computation, which is highly relevant for understanding the limits of distributed quantum algorithms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To enhance distributed machine learning training by making the foundational communication primitive, ring all-reduce, both more communication-efficient and information-theoretically private using quantum communications.

**Main result.** The authors present a quantum ring all-reduce protocol that achieves a provably optimal factor of two reduction in online communication via superdense coding and enables privacy guarantees impossible classically through Verified GHZ Aggregation (VGA).

**Method.** The work combines techniques like pre-shared entanglement, superdense coding for bandwidth savings, and encoding gradients into the phases of shared GHZ states to achieve security proofs.

**Model / system.** The core model is the quantum ring all-reduce primitive applied to $N$ workers holding local gradients. The analysis uses concepts from communication complexity, including comparing classical bit requirements against qubit resources derived from entanglement and GHZ states.

**Key observables.** Communication cost (qubits vs. bits), information leakage ($I(g_j; V_E)$), Quantum Fisher Information ($F_Q$), and the separation factors for conflict detection tasks ($	ext{GapIP}_	au$ and $	ext{TieAudit}_\epsilon$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of workers $N$, the gradient dimension $P$, the privacy error $\epsilon$, and the margin threshold $	au$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the availability of pre-shared entanglement (EPR pairs) and relies on asymptotic bounds for security proofs, requiring finite-key corrections for practical deployment.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the superdense-coding protocol for $N=3$ workers, detailing both classical bit costs and quantum qubit costs across different rounds of the all-reduce process.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing bandwidth savings via superdense coding in the ring all-reduce. It then introduces VGA for information-theoretic privacy guarantees. Finally, it analyzes two specific conflict detection tasks ($	ext{GapIP}_	au$ and $	ext{TieAudit}_\epsilon$) to demonstrate exponential quantum advantages.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Machine learning models have scaled to unprecedented sizes, making training across distributed devices the de facto standard in the field. In this work, we explore how quantum communications can make distributed training both more communication-efficient and information-theoretically private, for both classical and quantum learning models. Ring all-reduce is the foundational communication primitive for large-scale distributed training. We present a quantum version that reduces per-link online communication by a provably optimal factor of two using pre-shared entanglement and superdense coding, without requiring the learning model or gradient computation to change. Beyond bandwidth, the primitive enables privacy guarantees that are information-theoretically impossible for any classical protocol, achieving composable ε-secure aggregation, via verified entanglement, at a 2x overhead in GHZ copies. Our hybrid quantum-classical communication architecture yields simultaneous communication and security advantages for large scale distributed training, regardless of whether the learning itself is quantum or classical. Finally, we characterise quantum advantages in gradient conflict detection for server-to-client communication under bandwidth constraints, a setting that arises after ring all-reduce is completed, when full gradient broadcast to external clients is infeasible. Two variants of the problem admit different separations. For margin-based alignment testing (\textsc{GapIP}_τ), the quantum advantage is quadratic in the margin parameter: \widetilde{O}(τ^{-1}\log P) qubits versus \widetilde{O}(\min(\τ^{-2},P)) bits. For sign-consistency auditing against a private parameter matching (\textsc{TieAudit}_ε), the advantage represents an exponential separation in communication complexity: Ω(\sqrt{P}) bits whereas O(ε^{-2}\log P) qubits suffice.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces 'Vine Codes,' a novel class of low-overhead Quantum LDPC codes designed specifically for implementation on planar superconducting qubit grids. By utilizing nearest-neighbor gates and incorporating routing qubits, these codes significantly reduce the physical qubit overhead compared to established methods like the surface code while maintaining high error correction performance under realistic noise conditions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses hardware constraints in quantum computing by proposing a resource-efficient alternative to the standard surface code architecture for fault tolerance.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing quantum error-correcting codes (QEC) that significantly reduce qubit overhead compared to the surface code while remaining physically implementable on superconducting hardware.

**Main result.** Vine Codes are introduced as a novel class of qLDPC codes that achieve up to ~28% reduction in data/measure qubits and fewer total qubits than the surface code at comparable circuit distances, even including routing overheads.

**Method.** The authors use extensive numeric searches and systematic construction techniques to define these planar codes, generalizing previous work by incorporating open boundary conditions via 'routing qubits'.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using superconducting qubits on a planar square grid, restricted to nearest-neighbor two-qubit gates (iSWAP and CZ). The code structure involves defining data and measure qubits supporting X and Z stabilizers.

**Key observables.** Qubit overhead reduction percentage, circuit distance ($d_{	ext{circuit}}$), total number of required qubits, and performance under realistic noise models.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Near-term noise rate ($10^{-3}$), circuit distance (e.g., 7 or 10), and the code structure parameters (e.g., [[121,4,6]]).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a superconducting platform favoring nearest-neighbor gates; specific assumptions are made regarding the non-existence of certain error vectors to ensure code validity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate vine codes on a square lattice, show qubit overhead scaling comparisons against surface codes for various boundary geometries (parallelograms), and detail circuit construction steps using SWAP/CXSWAP gates.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from introducing the need for low-overhead planar QEC codes to defining Vine Codes, detailing their structure via step sequences, analyzing boundary conditions (Pauli boundaries), optimizing circuits by removing redundant gates, and finally simulating performance under noise.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The surface code is a promising route towards large-scale quantum computing, requiring only nearest-neighbour gates amenable to superconducting hardware. However, surface codes incur large qubit overheads. Novel quantum low-density parity check (qLDPC) codes promise to reduce overheads but require long-range connections that are difficult to achieve on superconducting platforms. Here, we introduce "Vine Codes" - qLDPC codes that are implementable on a planar square grid through nearest-neighbour, two-qubit gates native to superconducting platforms (iSWAP and CZ). Our approach generalises "Directional Codes" recently introduced by Gehér et. al. (2025) which are constrained to a torus. In contrast, vine codes have open boundary conditions constructed with the aid of routing qubits. We perform extensive numeric searches and find promising candidate vine codes, e.g. [[121,4,6]], [[221,6,7]], and [[234,9,6]] codes. We verify the circuit distances and show that data and measure qubits required can be reduced by up to ~28% relative to the surface code at a circuit distance of 7. Even including routing qubits, vine codes require fewer total qubits than the surface code (e.g. ~18% reduction at circuit distance 10) and benefits are expected to increase at higher distances. We perform circuit-level noise simulations to demonstrate that under a realistic noise model and at a near-term noise rate of $10^{-3}$, vine codes can perform better than the surface code while using fewer qubits. We give an exhaustive list of all unique vine codes up to stabiliser-weight 9. We additionally introduce "Flip-Vine Codes" which possess single-qubit transversal Clifford gates useful for fault-tolerant logic and magic state cultivation. We furthermore construct examples of generalised open boundaries for vine codes that go beyond the familiar X/Z boundaries of the surface and tile codes.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports on anomalous transport in the magnetic nodal-line semimetal $	ext{Mn}_3	ext{SnC}$. By analyzing both AHE and ANE, the authors demonstrate that these effects originate primarily from the intrinsic Berry curvature mechanism. This finding is significant because it provides strong evidence for topological contributions to electronic transport in magnetically ordered materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism and topology, the rigorous separation of transport mechanisms (intrinsic vs. extrinsic) using scaling arguments is a powerful diagnostic tool applicable across many-body physics systems where subtle quantum corrections are expected.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates anomalous transport behaviors, specifically the Anomalous Hall Effect (AHE) and Anomalous Nernst Effect (ANE), in a magnetic nodal-line semimetal.

**Main result.** Both AHE and ANE are found to be predominantly governed by intrinsic Berry-curvature effects. The ratio of ANE/AHE conductivity is a sizable fraction of k_B/e, confirming this topological origin.

**Method.** The authors combine magnetotransport measurements with theoretical analysis, using scaling relations ($ho_{A,xy} \propto ho^{\gamma}_{xx}$) to separate intrinsic (Berry curvature) and extrinsic scattering contributions.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the antiperovskite $	ext{Mn}_3	ext{SnC}$, which functions as a nodal-line semimetal exhibiting coexisting antiferromagnetic (AFM) and ferromagnetic (FM) magnetic ordering.

**Key observables.** Anomalous Hall resistivity ($ho_{A,xy}$), Anomalous Nernst conductivity ($\alpha_{A}^{xy}$), the ratio $|\alpha_{A}^{xy}/\sigma_{A}^{xy}|$, and temperature/field dependence of these quantities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature range ($2 - 300 	ext{ K}$), magnetic field up to $8 	ext{ T}$, and the characteristic energy scale set by $k_B/e$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that anomalous transport can be decomposed into intrinsic (Berry curvature) and extrinsic scattering contributions, and utilizes Mott's relation to link AHE and ANE.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the temperature dependence of magnetizations, $ho_{Axy}$ vs $ho_{xx}$ plots separating skew and intrinsic contributions, and Nernst signal dependencies on magnetic field and temperature.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes transport measurements to separate AHE origins (skew vs. intrinsic) using scaling laws, then extends this analysis to the ANE by relating it to AHE via Mott's relation, concluding that Berry curvature dominates both effects.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The interplay of topological surface states and magnetism gives rise to unconventional transport behaviors such as the anomalous Hall effect (AHE) and anomalous Nernst effect (ANE). Antiperovskites such as Mn$3$SnC, which are nodal-line semimetals and exhibit concurrent antiferromagnetic (AFM) and ferromagnetic (FM) ordering, provide a fertile ground for anomalous transport phenomena. Here, we report that the anomalous transport (AHE and ANE) in this compound is predominantly governed by the intrinsic Berry-curvature effect. We establish a unified relationship between the AHE and ANE using Mott's relation. Electron scattering by both AFM and FM magnons is manifested in the anomalous Nernst signal. The ratio of the anomalous Nernst conductivity to the anomalous Hall conductivity, $|α^A{xy}|/σ^A_{xy}$, constitutes a sizable fraction of $k_B/e$, indicating a strong Berry-curvature contribution to the ANE.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces critical percolation as a principled synthetic data model to test the limits of neural network interpretability. By generating data from sparse, self-similar fractal clusters whose structure is governed by random trees, the authors demonstrate that ground-truth hierarchical latent variables can be successfully decoded using linear probes on trained MLPs.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous connection between statistical physics concepts (critical phenomena, fractal geometry) and machine learning diagnostics provides a powerful, physically motivated benchmark for understanding feature representations in deep networks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Existing synthetic datasets for interpretability lack the complex, multi-scale structure found in natural data. The goal is to create a principled testbed that incorporates realistic structural features.

**Main result.** Critical percolation provides such a testbed because its inherent properties—sparsity, self-similarity, and power-law statistics—allow ground-truth latent variables to be linearly decoded from neural network activations.

**Method.** The authors generate data by embedding nodes on critical mean-field percolation clusters, whose structure is mathematically mapped to random trees using an efficient cyclic coalescent algorithm. They then train MLPs and test linear decoding of the underlying hierarchical latent variables.

**Model / system.** The model uses site percolation on a lattice approximated by the Bethe lattice in the critical regime ($p=p_c$). Data points are derived from sparse, low-dimensional fractal clusters whose target values are generated by a hierarchical latent variable decomposition based on the cluster's underlying random tree structure.

**Key observables.** Linear decoding performance ($	ext{R}^2$, MSE) of ground-truth latent variables; Cluster size distribution (power law $\propto s^{-5/2}$); Fractal dimension ($D < d$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Critical probability $p_c$; Embedding dimension $d$; Latent variable depth; Figure of Merit ($	ext{FOM} \equiv s_{	ext{latent}} / \sqrt{s_{	ext{cluster}}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model relies on the mean-field approximation ($d \gg u$) and assumes that targets follow a simple hierarchical construction. It also notes limitations regarding modeling compositional relationships.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the percolation data generation (fractal clusters, latent decomposition), validation of the cyclic coalescent process using Prüfer sequences and degree distributions, and probe performance comparing one-cluster vs. multi-cluster datasets.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the need for structured synthetic data. It then details the mathematical construction based on critical mean-field percolation clusters and their equivalence to random trees via coalescent processes. Finally, it applies this model by generating datasets (one/multi-cluster) and testing interpretability methods using linear probing of trained MLPs.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Neural networks learn features that reflect the hierarchical, multi-scale structure of natural data. Synthetic datasets used to evaluate interpretability methods typically lack this structure, limiting their value as realistic toy models. To close this gap, we introduce a family of synthetic datasets consisting of hierarchical functions defined on critical mean-field percolation clusters embedded in a high-dimensional data space. The percolation data consists of sparse, low-dimensional fractal clusters with a power-law size distribution. Latent variables modeling a taxonomic hierarchy generate each data point's target value. The data model is analytically tractable with known critical exponents that fix its properties without requiring hyperparameter tuning. We leverage a mapping between percolation clusters, random trees, and additive coalescence to propose an almost linear-time algorithm to jointly sample a random tree and its hierarchical latent decomposition, enabling data generation at arbitrary scale. Using probing experiments, we find that the model's ground-truth latent variables can be linearly decoded from neural network activations. Together, sparsity, self-similarity, power-law statistics, and analytical tractability make critical percolation a principled testbed for interpretability research.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper addresses the difficulty of distinguishing complex, multi-time quantum processes due to exponentially scaling computational requirements. It introduces 'Machines for Autonomous Distinction' (MADs), which restrict testing strategies based on a bounded coherent memory dimension ($d_A$). This results in a scalable distinguishability measure that provides an operational lower bound on the true optimal discrimination capability.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial bridge between theoretically optimal but computationally intractable measures (like the strategy norm) and practically measurable quantities by quantifying the role of finite quantum memory in process characterization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing a computationally tractable measure for distinguishing between two multi-time quantum processes ($P_N$ vs $Q_N$) when the optimal strategy requires an exponentially large coherent memory.

**Main result.** The introduction of Machines for Autonomous Distinction (MADs) provides a scalable, memory-constrained distinguishability measure that is proven to saturate the full strategy-norm benchmark as the available coherent memory dimension increases.

**Method.** The authors restrict the set of admissible probing strategies to those compatible with bounded coherent memory ($d_A$), leading to a memory-parametrized distinguishability measure $d^{(N)}_{\mathsf{MAD}}$.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes multi-time quantum processes, modeled using process tensors or quantum combs ($	ext{P}_N$), which represent the input-output behavior over sequential time steps via CPTP maps.

**Key observables.** Memory-parametrized distinguishability measure ($d^{(N)}_{\mathsf{MAD}}$), strategy-norm distance ($d_{	ext{str}}$), and the success probability of discrimination ($p^*_{	ext{succ}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coherent memory dimension ($d_A$), number of time steps ($N$), and the process tensors ($\mathbf{P}^N, \mathbf{Q}^N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that restricting the tester to bounded coherent memory ($d_A$) provides a useful operational lower bound on the true distinguishability, which can be shown to converge to the full benchmark for finite $N$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate multi-time process discrimination as an operational task involving sequential probing and memory storage; one figure shows the interaction between a quantum comb ($	ext{PN}$) and a multi-time tester ($\{	ext{T}^j_N\}_j$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the limitations of the full strategy-norm distance, then introduces MADs as a memory-constrained alternative. It develops theoretical results showing monotonicity and completeness, derives single-step descriptions for recurrent processes, and concludes with numerical evidence demonstrating scalability.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Distinguishing multi-time quantum processes is a fundamental task underlying the diagnosis, benchmarking, and learning of temporally correlated quantum dynamics. The standard benchmark for distinguishing two processes is the strategy-norm distance, which optimizes over arbitrary adaptive probing strategies but can require large coherent memory and time-dependent control. We introduce machines for autonomous distinction~($\mathsf{MAD}$s): probing strategies that apply the same quantum instrument at each time step, retain the full classical outcome record, and carry a coherent memory of dimension $d_A$. Optimizing over these strategies defines a memory-parametrized distinguishability measure, $d^{(N)}_{\mathsf{MAD}}(\mathbf{P}^N,\mathbf{Q}^N;d_A)$. We show that the resulting hierarchy is monotone in coherent memory and complete at finite times. Specifically, any admissible $N$-step probing strategy can be compiled into a single $\mathsf{MAD}$ with an internal counter and sufficiently large coherent memory, so the hierarchy saturates the strategy-norm benchmark. For recurrent processes generated by repeated system--environment interactions, we derive a single-step description that separates the generation of new distinguishing information from the propagation and decay of information generated at earlier times. Numerical results in a repeated-interaction model show that increasing coherent memory systematically improves the $\mathsf{MAD}$ success probability and closes the gap to the strategy-norm distance while remaining substantially more tractable to evaluate. $\mathsf{MAD}$ distinguishability therefore provides an operational and scalable framework for quantifying what can be learned about genuinely multi-time quantum processes with bounded coherent memory.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.20519v1>

**Summary.** This work uses GPU-accelerated Semidefinite Programming to study quantum correlations violating definite causal order via the GYNI game. The authors test if increasing local dimension improves the achievable bound but find diminishing returns. This suggests that improving the theoretical upper bound or finding fundamentally different strategies is necessary.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides advanced computational techniques (GPU-accelerated SDP solving) to probe fundamental questions about causality in quantum mechanics, which is highly relevant for understanding resource theories beyond standard entanglement.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates whether increasing the local dimension ($d$) can narrow the gap between the best known process-matrix strategy value ($\approx 0.6218$) and the strongest upper bound for correlations violating definite causal order in the Guess-Your-Neighbour's-Input (GYNI) game.

**Main result.** The study found no significant improvement in the winning probability when increasing the local dimension up to $d=8$, suggesting that either qualitatively different strategies are needed or the known upper bound is not tight.

**Method.** Optimization relies on formulating the problem as a sequence of Semidefinite Programs (SDPs) solved using a see-saw optimization scheme, significantly accelerated by offloading the projection onto the positive-semidefinite cone to a GPU.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes quantum correlations described by the process matrix formalism, which models scenarios lacking a fixed causal order between local laboratories. The paradigmatic example is the bipartite GYNI game used to test for violations of definite causal orders.

**Key observables.** Winning probability ($\omega_{GYNI}$) in the GYNI game; violation of causal inequalities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Local dimension ($d$); best known strategy value ($\approx 0.6218$); upper bound ($\approx 0.7592$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the optimal correlations can be captured within the process matrix formalism and that SDP solvers provide sufficient accuracy.

**Figures summary.** Table I summarizes $\omega_{GYNI}$ as a function of $d$, showing slow growth; Table IV compares computational time for solving representative SDPs across increasing dimensions, highlighting GPU speedups.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework using process matrices and causal games, details the optimization via see-saw SDPs, implements this efficiently on GPUs, and concludes by analyzing the scaling of the achievable bound with local dimension $d$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The process matrix formalism describes quantum correlations in scenarios without a fixed causal order between local laboratories. Operational signatures of such correlations can be investigated through causal games. A paradigmatic example is the Guess-Your-Neighbour's-Input game, in which two parties attempt to guess each other's inputs. Correlations compatible with any definite, or probabilistically mixed, causal order cannot achieve a winning probability exceeding $1/2$. The best process-matrix strategy currently known attains a value of approximately $0.6218$ using local dimension $d=5$, while the strongest known dimension-independent upper bound is $0.7592$. In this work, we investigate whether increasing the local dimension beyond $d = 5$ can narrow this gap. To this end, we employ a see-saw optimization scheme in which each step is formulated as a semidefinite program. For scalability, we develop a custom implementation of the SCS solver in which the dominant computational cost, the projection onto the positive-semidefinite cone, is offloaded to a GPU, yielding a six-fold speedup. Using this implementation, we explore local dimensions up to $d = 8$, and we do not find significant improvements over the value at $d=5$. Our results suggest that either qualitatively different strategies are required to approach the known upper bound, or that the bound itself is not tight.

</details>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 3.</b> FIG. 3. Schematic of the no-string proof strategy. A large connected support has stabilizer-violation regions shown as red cubes. Corner and good-edge erasing deform the rep- resentative to an axis-aligned corridor connecting the same endpoint charges. Further erasing gives the two flat rectangles. The confusing constraint then removes rows from the exposed edges: the white erased interval is longest on the outer row and shortens row by row as the induction moves inward, even- tually cutting the flat segment open if it is sufficiently long. The disconnected representative contradicts nontriviality of a sufficiently long fixed-width logical string segment.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">

The translation-invariant QtHC reference model exhibits arithmetic finite-size fluctuations, including the large spike k = 54 at L = 18. The retained minimal- degeneracy QtRCC collection follows the parity pattern of Eq. (22), with k = 2 for odd L and k = 4 for even L.</figcaption>
</figure>

The left panel gives a three-dimensional view of the operator support on the plane x = 0, and the right panel shows the same support projected to the transverse y-z plane. Blue markers denote first-qutrit factors XI, green markers denote second-qutrit factors IX, and the two shades distinguish exponents 1 and 2 in F3. This selected quotient-basis representative has weight 120: it occupies 90 of the 102 lattice sites in the plane, with 30 sites carrying both qutrit factors.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper introduces qutrit random cubic codes ($	ext{QtRCC}$) as an alternative to traditional topological QECs. By adapting complex geometric proofs from Haah's work to the qutrit setting, the authors prove that $	ext{QtRCC}$ remains free of string logical operators while simultaneously eliminating the self-similar fractal operators found in uniform models. This demonstrates that constrained randomness can yield novel and potentially superior quantum error-correcting phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a concrete example of how introducing controlled randomness into highly structured quantum codes can fundamentally alter their underlying physical properties, moving beyond purely translation-invariant models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop robust Quantum Error-Correcting Codes (QECs) that are passive self-correcting, specifically by proving the absence of string logical operators in three dimensions.

**Main result.** The proposed qutrit random cubic codes ($	ext{QtRCC}$) retain the no-string property and exhibit distinct properties from translation-invariant fracton codes, suggesting improved self-correction capabilities via constrained randomness.

**Method.** The authors employ rigorous algebraic proofs by adapting geometric techniques (like corner/edge erasing) used in Haah's original work to the qutrit ($\mathbb{F}_3$) setting. They also use numerical diagnostics like charge-push protocols and GSD calculations.

**Model / system.** The system is a family of local qutrit Calderbank-Shor-Steane stabilizer Hamiltonians defined on a cubic lattice, generalizing Haah's Code 1 structure but incorporating spatially varying stabilizers (QtRCC).

**Key observables.** Ground-state degeneracy exponent ($k$), minimum weight of plane-supported logical operators ($	ext{w}_{\min}$), and the absence of fractal recurrence in charge-push diagnostics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The lattice size $L$, the qutrit nature ($\mathbb{Z}_3$ coefficients), and the parity dependence of the GSD exponent ($k=2$ for odd $L$, $k=4$ for even $L$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the random models are sampled only from the 'admissible set' defined by global stabilizer relations and local commutation constraints, and that all relevant coefficients are nonzero.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the ground-state degeneracy exponent ($k$) between uniform (QtHC) and random (QtRCC) codes, showing a stable parity pattern for QtRCC. They also visually demonstrate the absence of power-of-three fractal recurrence in charge-push diagnostics for QtRCC compared to QtHC.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds logically by first establishing the necessary algebraic framework for qutrit checks, then proving the no-string theorem via geometric reduction (corner/edge erasing), and finally using numerical diagnostics (plane scans, charge-push) to confirm the absence of fractal operators and characterize the resulting topological order.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum error-correcting codes (QECs) are essential components quantum computation and have deep connections to quantum phases of matter. A key obstruction to passive self-correcting QECs is the presence of string logical operators, which can generate logical errors through constant-energy-barrier processes. Haah's Codes (fracton codes) showed that three-dimensional stabilizer codes can forbid such string logical operators, but their translation-invariant structure supports self-similar fractal logical operators with a logarithmic energy barrier. We introduce the qutrit random cubic codes, a family of local qutrit Calderbank-Shor-Steane stabilizer Hamiltonians with similar cube-check structure as Haah's Code 1 but built from spatially varying stabilizers. We prove that these models retain the no-string property and numerically observe that they have properties distinct from translation-invariant fracton codes: the smallest ground-state degeneracy exponent is $k=2$ for odd $L$ and $k=4$ for even $L$; noncontractible plane-logical operators span the entire logical space; and charge-push diagnostics show that the self-similar fractal operators are absent. These results demonstrate that constrained randomness can fundamentally change the nature of stabilizer codes and improve their self-correction properties. They further point to broader families of quantum error-correcting codes and quantum phases beyond canonical topological and fracton orders.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study investigates $	ext{Mo}_{0.75}	ext{V}_{0.25}	ext{Te}_2$ as a promising 2D platform for valleytronics by using DFT calculations. The authors show that alloying breaks the symmetry of pristine $	ext{MoTe}_2$, inducing valley polarization, which can then be significantly enhanced and tuned using external biaxial strain or electric fields. This work establishes a robust method for engineering materials with controllable valley degrees of freedom.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter physics rather than quantum information protocols, the concept of controlling valley pseudospin via external fields or mechanical strain is a key area in topological/spintronics materials research that interfaces with quantum control concepts.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to overcome the valley degeneracy inherent in pristine TMDs, which limits their use in valleytronic devices, by engineering materials that exhibit large and tunable valley polarization.

**Main result.** V-alloying $	ext{MoTe}_2$ to form $	ext{Mo}_{0.75}	ext{V}_{0.25}	ext{Te}_2$, combined with external electric fields or biaxial strain, successfully induces and enhances valley polarization up to $160.8 	ext{ meV}$.

**Method.** The study relies entirely on first-principles Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations, including non-collinear treatments for Spin-Orbit Coupling (SOC), phonon dispersion analysis, and modeling the effects of strain and electric fields.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the $	ext{Mo}_{0.75}	ext{V}_{0.25}	ext{Te}_2$ monolayer, a transition metal dichalcogenide alloy derived from $	ext{MoTe}_2$. The theoretical framework models electronic structure changes due to alloying (introducing magnetic exchange interaction) and external perturbations like strain ($\epsilon$) and electric fields ($\mathbf{E}$).

**Key observables.** Valley polarization ($\Delta E = |E_{K^+} - E_{K^-}|$), spin splitting, formation energy ($E_{	ext{formation}}$), phonon frequencies.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Strain levels (e.g., $4\%$ tensile strain), electric field strengths (e.g., $0.4 	ext{ V}/\AA$), and alloying concentrations ($	ext{Mo}_{0.75}	ext{V}_{0.25}	ext{Te}_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations assume the validity of DFT approximations, including PBE functional and PAW pseudopotentials, to model complex electronic interactions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate band structures under varying strain/fields, showing polarization enhancement; phonon dispersion confirms dynamic stability for both pristine $	ext{MoTe}_2$ and the alloy; schematic diagrams propose device concepts utilizing this tunability.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically progresses from establishing the limitations of pristine TMDs to demonstrating the structural and electronic stability of the V-alloyed material, followed by quantitative analysis of how alloying breaks symmetry, and finally detailing the enhancement mechanisms using external fields and strain for practical device realization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Valley polarization in 2D TMDs is promising for low-power valleytronic and spin-valley information processing, but time-reversal symmetry in pristine nonmagnetic TMDs keeps the K+ and K- valleys degenerate, limiting device applications. In this work, we investigated the structural stability, electronic properties, and tunable valley polarization of V-alloyed MoTe2 monolayer, Mo0.75V0.25Te2, using first-principles density functional theory (DFT) calculations. Substitutional alloying of MoTe2 with V introduced magnetic exchange interaction, which, together with spin-orbit coupling (SOC), lifted the valley degeneracy at the unequal valleys. The alloyed structure was found to be energetically and dynamically stable due to the absence of imaginary phonon modes. In pristine MoTe2, SOC produced spin splittings of 34.0 meV and 218.9 meV in the conduction bands and valence bands, respectively, but no valley polarization was observed. In contrast, Mo0.75V0.25Te2 exhibited spontaneous valley polarization of 37.3 meV in the conduction band and 78.2 meV in the valence band. The valley polarization was further enhanced by external electric fields and biaxial strain. A transverse electric field along the crystal c axis produced the maximum valley splitting of 132.8 meV in the valence band, whereas biaxial tensile strain increased the valence band valley splitting up to 160.8 meV. The maximum conduction band valley splitting reached 54.4 meV under 2% biaxial compressive strain. These results demonstrated that V alloying, combined with electric-field and strain engineering, provides an effective strategy for achieving large and tunable valley polarization in MoTe2. Thus, Mo0.75V0.25Te2 can be considered a promising 2D platform for tunable valleytronic device applications, such as transistors and sensors.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a robust finite-size security analysis for discrete-modulated CVQKD protocols. It overcomes limitations of previous proofs by integrating dimension reduction and using the MEAT framework. The results confirm positive key rates for realistic block sizes, advancing the field toward practical quantum communication.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides advanced theoretical tools for quantifying security in practical quantum communication systems, which is crucial for realizing scalable quantum networks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work develops a finite-size security analysis for discrete-modulated continuous variable Quantum Key Distribution (CVQKD) protocols under general attacks.

**Main result.** Positive key rates are reported in the finite-size regime for relevant block sizes on the order of $10^8$, successfully bridging the gap between theoretical proofs and practical implementations.

**Method.** The security analysis combines dimension reduction, a proof based on the Marginal-Constrained Entropy Accumulation Theorem (MEAT), and modeling receiver imperfections using a trusted detector model.

**Model / system.** The system is CVQKD utilizing discrete modulations of coherent states, modeled by discretizing phase space measurements into finite registers ($Z_j$). The analysis framework extends established quantum information security metrics ($\epsilon$-security).

**Key observables.** Secret key rate (bits/pulse), dimension reduction penalty ($\zeta_\alpha$), and the final achievable key length $\ell$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Block sizes ($n \sim 10^8$ to $10^{10}$), modulation parameters ($\Delta, \Delta_s$), detection efficiency ($\eta_d$), and reconciliation efficiency ($eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on continuity bounds to justify dimension reduction from infinite-dimensional states to finite subspaces, and uses the MEAT framework.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase space representation of modulations (Figure 1) and show the dependence of the dimension reduction penalty ($\zeta_\alpha$) and the final finite-size secret key rates on channel transmittance ($\chi$) for various block sizes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the discrete protocol, formalizing security using $\epsilon$-security metrics, developing mathematical tools like concentration inequalities, and finally applying MEAT combined with dimension reduction to derive a rigorous lower bound on the secret key rate.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Continuous variable quantum key distribution via discrete modulations ensures information-theoretic security using standard telecom technologies, providing affordable and scalable quantum communications with simplified classical postprocessing. However, existing security proofs against general attacks often rely on restrictive assumptions, such as a bounded dimension for coherent states, or require impractically large block sizes. In this work, we develop a finite-size security analysis that removes these limitations while incorporating realistic experimental features. Our approach combines the dimension reduction technique, a security proof based on the marginal-constrained entropy accumulation, and a trusted detector model accounting for the receiver imperfections. We report positive key rates in the finite-size regime for relevant block sizes of the order of $10^8$. These results contribute to narrowing the gap between theoretical security proofs and practical implementations of discrete-modulated continuous variable quantum key distribution protocols.

</details>

### [Passive-User Bell-State Loop-Back Key Establishment without Quantum Detectors at the User Nodes](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19551v1)

**Authors:** Luis Adrián Lizama-Pérez  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.19551v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Figure 1: Conceptual representation of the passive-user Bell-state Loop-Back architecture. The traveling subsystem passes sequentially through B1 and B2, which apply local Pauli operations U1 and U2. The lower panels provide an operator-level visualization of these Pauli operations acting on a test Bloch vector ⃗r. In the actual Bell-state protocol, the traveling subsystem is locally maximally mixed, ρB = I/2, so the physical information is not carried by a local Bloch vector but by the Bell correlations recovered at Alice’s station. The figure illustrates the effective composition Ueff = U2U1, which is the only transformation inferred by Alice from the final Bell-state measurement.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2606.19551_figures/2606.19551_fig2.jpg" alt="Figure 2">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 2.</b> Figure 2: Effective error rate Qeff as a function of the perturbation probability p under two perturbation models, obtained from Qiskit simulations with the AerSimulator. Markers denote the simulated values for the Pauli-error and Bell-substitution models, respectively. The dashed and dotted lines represent the theoretical behaviors Qeff = p and Qeff = 3p/4. The close overlap between the simulated points and the theoretical curves confirms the consistency of the inference rule under both perturbation models.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** The paper proposes a Bell-state extension of the Loop-Back QKD protocol to enable secret key establishment between two passive users. By using entangled states and having an active station measure the resulting global Pauli operation ($U_{	ext{eff}}$), the system allows users to derive their private raw keys from the decomposition of $U_{	ext{eff}}$. This advances quantum cryptography by removing the need for complex quantum hardware at the end-user nodes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it explores novel quantum communication protocols that circumvent hardware limitations (no detectors/transmitters at user nodes) by leveraging entanglement structure, which has implications for future quantum network architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to establish a secret key between two passive users ($B_1$ and $B_2$) without requiring them to possess quantum transmitters or detectors.

**Main result.** The protocol successfully transfers the distributed-transformation mechanism of Loop-Back QKD from single qubits to entangled Bell states, allowing for key generation based on the private decomposition of a global Pauli operation.

**Method.** It analyzes the effective Pauli operation ($U_{	ext{eff}} = U_2 U_1$) observed by an active station (Alice) after two passive users apply local Pauli operations to a traveling entangled subsystem.

**Model / system.** The system uses a Bell-state extension of the Loop-Back QKD architecture. Alice prepares and measures Bell pairs, while $B_1$ and $B_2$ act as passive modulators applying local Pauli gates ($U_1, U_2$).

**Key observables.** The effective Pauli operation ($U_{	ext{eff}}$) measured by Alice; the private local operations ($U_1, U_2$) known only to $B_1$ and $B_2$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The success probability of the Bell-state measurement ($p_{	ext{BSM}}$); the effective error rate ($Q_{	ext{eff}}$) under various attack models.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol assumes that individual local operations remain algebraically hidden from Alice, and security relies on non-colluding users and physical characterization of components (not device-independent).

**Figures summary.** Figures conceptually illustrate the path $A 	o B_1 	o B_2 	o A$ showing how local operations compose to form $U_{	ext{eff}} = U_2 U_1$. Tables summarize ideal rounds and quantify error rates under different noise models.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the Bell-state extension, analyzes the algebraic structure of the effective operation ($U_{	ext{eff}}$), derives key inference rules for users based on $U_{	ext{eff}}$, discusses security against attacks (like separable substitutions), and concludes with generalizations to multi-user settings.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose and analyze a Bell-state extension of the Loop-Back quantum key distribution architecture for secret-key establishment between two passive users that do not require quantum transmitters or quantum detectors. In the proposed setting, a single active station, Alice, provides the entangled-state infrastructure, retains one qubit of an initially prepared Bell pair, and sends the traveling subsystem through two passive users, denoted by $B_1$ and $B_2$. Each passive user applies a local Pauli operation to the same traveling subsystem, so that the operation observed by Alice is only the effective composition $U_{\mathrm{eff}}=U_2U_1$. After the subsystem returns, Alice performs a Bell-state measurement and, using her private knowledge of the initial Bell state, deterministically identifies the effective Pauli operation. However, the individual factors $U_1$ and $U_2$ remain algebraically hidden from Alice whenever the local choices are uniformly and independently selected. The public effective operation acts as a parity-like constraint: each passive user can infer the operation applied by the other from its own private choice, while the active station learns only the global composition. This construction transfers the essential distributed-transformation mechanism of passive-user Loop-Back QKD to the entangled-state regime. Unlike single-qubit passive-user schemes, whose useful events are intrinsically post-selected, the Bell-state version is limited primarily by the success probability of the Bell-state measurement. We discuss the algebraic structure of the protocol, its interpretation as an infrastructure-assisted mediated key-establishment mechanism, and the physical assumptions required to protect passive Pauli modulators against active injection or Trojan-horse-type attacks.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proves that quantum kernels can be mathematically recast as spectral tensor networks by analyzing their Fourier representations. It introduces Kernel Target Alignment (KTA) and measures the resulting 'spectral compressibility' using bond dimensions. This provides a diagnostic tool to determine if evaluating a quantum kernel is likely to remain classically tractable.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful mathematical tool (tensor networks) to analyze quantum information processing tasks (kernel methods), offering a concrete measure of when quantum computation might be classically simulable.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is identifying quantum kernels, derived from feature maps, as spectral tensor networks to understand their computational complexity.

**Main result.** Quantum kernels can be exactly represented by spectral tensor networks, and the compressibility of their Fourier coefficient tensors serves as a diagnostic for classical tractability.

**Method.** The authors use Fourier analysis to decompose quantum kernels into coefficients ($	ilde{c}_{\omega,\omega'}$), which are then mapped onto Matrix Product Operator (MPO) factorizations characteristic of spectral tensor networks.

**Model / system.** The model involves quantum feature states $|\Phi(x)angle$ generated by layered data-encoding quantum circuits. The kernel is $\kappa(x, x') = |\langle\Phi(x)|\Phi(x')angle|^2$.

**Key observables.** Spectral compressibility (rank structure of Fourier coefficients), Bond Dimension ($\chi$), and Kernel Target Alignment (KTA).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond dimension ($\chi$), circuit depth ($L$), and the frequency-resolving grid ($\mathcal{G}\Omega$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on assuming a specific structure for data-encoding gates and utilizing the approximation that KTA simplifies to the Frobenius cosine similarity on a frequency-resolving grid.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the transition from quantum feature maps to spectral tensor networks using $\hat{C}$, compare gate-level vs. feature-wise scaling, and show KTA converging to unity as bond dimension increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing identities linking Fourier expansions to ETK contractions, deriving a compact grouped Fourier form, defining KTA as the bridge metric, and finally using numerical experiments to demonstrate spectral compressibility diagnostics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum kernels admit Fourier representations whose frequencies are determined by the data-encoding gates of the underlying feature map. We show that entangling tensor kernels are matrix product operator factorizations of the corresponding Fourier coefficient tensors, thereby identifying quantum kernels as spectral tensor networks. By grouping gate-level frequency configurations that yield the same feature-wise frequency, we obtain a grouped Fourier form that induces a more compact spectral tensor network representation of the kernel. We further show that kernel target alignment serves as a bridge between the Fourier and tensor network views. On a grid that resolves the accessible Fourier modes, it becomes the Frobenius cosine similarity between Fourier coefficient tensors. Our numerical experiments show that layered quantum kernels admit accurate representations with small bond dimension, revealing a compressibility governed by correlations between Fourier modes. This compressibility provides a diagnostic of classical representability and of whether kernel evaluation is likely to remain classically tractable.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops theoretical frameworks to achieve Heisenberg Limit scaling in quantum metrology by employing subsystem Quantum Error Correction (QEC). It demonstrates that these codes simplify protocols significantly compared to standard QEC by allowing noise absorption into gauge degrees of freedom. The key achievement is showing how this approach can maintain coherent logical evolution even for time-dependent signals using syndrome-free methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on resource-efficient QEC for metrology is highly relevant. By showing how gauge degrees of freedom can absorb noise while preserving the logical signal coherence, it suggests practical pathways for implementing high-precision quantum sensors using current hardware capabilities.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To enhance quantum metrology precision beyond classical limits by suppressing noise using Quantum Error Correction (QEC) techniques.

**Main result.** Subsystem error correction provides a resource-efficient method to achieve the Heisenberg Limit, even for time-dependent signals, by protecting only the coherent logical evolution rather than the entire state.

**Method.** The authors derive effective Hamiltonian dynamics ($	ext{Heff}$) governing the logical evolution within the code space, utilizing subsystem stabilizer codes and syndrome-free protocols.

**Model / system.** The system involves estimating an unknown parameter $	heta$ via noisy quantum channels. The theoretical framework uses subsystem stabilizer codes (e.g., ladder codes) to encode information into a logical subspace protected from noise absorbed by gauge degrees of freedom.

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information ($	ext{QFI}$), which must scale as $N^2$ for the Heisenberg Limit; the effective Hamiltonian ($H_{	ext{eff}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of queries ($N$), the error rate ($p$), and the structure of the noise operators $\{E_i\}$ relative to the signal Hamiltonian $H$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes low error rates for successful decoding, and that the logical evolution can be approximated by an effective unitary dynamics within the code space.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate QEC circuits for phase estimation, showing syndrome-free vs. standard methods; Figure 2 shows the ladder code architecture; Algorithm 1 details the Floquet code metrology protocol steps.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for subsystem codes over full QEC (Chunk 1), detailing protocols for time-dependent signals using the ladder code (Chunk 2), deriving effective dynamics for both standard and subsystem codes (Chunk 4), generalizing the formalism to arbitrary Pauli generators via symplectic methods (Chunk 5), and finally proving HL achievability with syndrome-free, ancilla-less techniques (Chunk 6).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum error correction has been successfully applied to enhance the precision of parameter estimation in the presence of noise. Nonetheless, existing methods require a number of noiseless, controllable ancillae and lack efficient encoding and decoding procedures. In this Letter, we demonstrate that subsystem error correction provides a new direction that can substantially simplify the metrological protocol. We derive general conditions under which subsystem stabilizer codes achieve the Heisenberg limit and show that, for broad classes of noise, this can be realized by syndrome-free protocols using at most a single ancilla qubit. Furthermore, we extend this framework to dynamical error correction and show that Floquet codes can protect time-dependent metrological signals in reaching the Heisenberg limit.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a robust method to calculate variational energies for infinite Matrix Product States when the underlying Hamiltonian has algebraically decaying interactions. By reformulating the tail summation as a matrix function evaluated via Krylov methods, it bypasses the need for artificial finite-pole approximations common in existing techniques. This advancement allows for accurate study of critical phenomena and long-range physics in 1D quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, systematic way to handle long-range physics in 1D quantum systems using tensor networks, which is crucial for modeling materials where interactions decay slowly (e.g., dipolar systems or RKKY coupling).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing variational energy calculations for infinite Matrix Product States (iMPS) when the target Hamiltonian features algebraically decaying interactions, while avoiding artificial approximations.

**Main result.** A novel transfer-matrix-function formulation allows direct summation of algebraic tails, preserving correct critical signatures that are lost when using standard finite-pole exponential surrogates.

**Method.** The method involves calculating the variational energy by representing the algebraic tail via a matrix function $F_{\alpha,Q}(\widetilde{T}_A)$ and evaluating this action using a Krylov subspace projection combined with Fréchet adjoint differentiation for stable gradients.

**Model / system.** The work applies to one-dimensional quantum systems with long-range interactions, such as inverse-square spin chains (Haldane–Shastry model) and general algebraic tails $J(r) \propto 1/r^\alpha$.

**Key observables.** Variational energy, correlation length ($\xi_D$), connected correlators $\langle\vec{S}_0 \cdot \vec{S}_rangle_c$, and critical diagnostics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond dimension ($D$) of the MPS, Krylov subspace dimension ($m$), algebraic decay exponent ($\alpha$), and interaction coupling constants ($Q$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes a unique dominant fixed point for defining the connected transfer matrix $e^{TA}$, and the accuracy is controlled by the choice of Krylov dimension $m$.

**Figures summary.** Figures demonstrate that moderate Krylov dimensions stabilize energy calculations for the Haldane–Shastry model, show convergence to exact thermodynamic limits for free fermions, and critically illustrate that the matrix-function approach maintains correct critical behavior where finite-pole approximations fail.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem of algebraic tails in iMPS. It then details the transfer-matrix-function formulation using Krylov methods and gradient techniques. Benchmarks are presented across several models (free fermions, Heisenberg chain, Ising chain) to validate the method's stability and accuracy against known limits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Variational infinite matrix product state (iMPS) calculations usually make Hamiltonians with algebraically decaying interactions compatible with standard MPO algorithms by first replacing the target Hamiltonian with a finite-pole sum-of-exponentials surrogate, thereby introducing a Hamiltonian-representation residual. We formulate the fixed-$D$ variational energy without introducing such a surrogate. For a fixed finite-$D$ MPS, the algebraic tail can be summed directly through the connected transfer matrix: the tail $e^{\mathrm{i} Qr}/r^α$ is represented by the matrix function $F_{α,Q}(\widetilde{T}_A)$, with $F_{α,Q}(z)=\operatorname{Li}_α(e^{\mathrm{i} Q}\,z)/z$. We evaluate the resulting matrix-function action using a Krylov method and obtain stable gradients by combining a Fréchet adjoint with implicit fixed-point differentiation. Benchmarks on long-range free fermions and the inverse-square Heisenberg family, including the Haldane--Shastry point, validate the transfer-matrix-function formulation. A long-range Ising-chain calculation illustrates a practical consequence of avoiding a finite-pole Hamiltonian representation. At a fixed, independently known critical field, finite-pole surrogate Hamiltonians can bias a critical diagnostic away from criticality, whereas the matrix-function calculation retains the expected critical signatures of the target algebraic Hamiltonian.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper improves the formulation of constrained combinatorial optimization problems like TSP for NISQ devices by introducing the AVS-HOBO encoding. It uses VQE on simulated and real quantum hardware to demonstrate that AVS-HOBO significantly enhances solution feasibility and stability compared to existing methods. This work advances the practical viability of quantum computing for complex, real-world optimization tasks.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, near-term application of quantum algorithms to NP-hard optimization problems, directly comparing encoding efficiency and robustness against realistic noise models relevant for current quantum hardware development.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses constrained combinatorial optimization problems, specifically formulating and solving the Traveling Salesperson Problem (TSP) using quantum computation.

**Main result.** The proposed All-valid-state HOBO (AVS-HOBO) encoding significantly enhances the practical reliability and scalability of VQE for TSP on NISQ devices compared to standard HOBO or QUBO encodings.

**Method.** The core method is using the Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE) algorithm, which minimizes the expectation value of a problem Hamiltonian constructed via specialized binary encoding schemes.

**Model / system.** The model involves mapping the TSP to an Ising/Hamiltonian form. The system utilizes VQE on simulated and real NISQ quantum hardware, comparing QUBO, HOBO, and AVS-HOBO encodings.

**Key observables.** Energy convergence behavior, Approximation Ratio (AR), Length Ratio (LR), Feasibility Ratio (FR), and Residual Energy ($	ext{Eres}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** TSP instances up to 20 cities; penalty coefficients ($A$); error mitigation techniques like Matrix-free measurement mitigation ($	ext{Mthree}$) and Dynamical Decoupling ($	ext{DD}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the applicability of VQE on NISQ devices, acknowledging that hardware noise degrades performance but AVS-HOBO mitigates this better.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare convergence (Eres) and solution quality metrics (AR, LR, FR) across different encoding schemes (HOBO vs. AVS-HOBO) for increasing TSP sizes, showing AVS-HOBO's superior stability.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem (TSP), compares QUBO/HOBO encodings, details the novel AVS-HOBO scheme using cyclic mapping, and validates its performance through extensive simulations and tests on real quantum hardware while analyzing error mitigation effects.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Continued advancements in quantum computing have stimulated growing interest in translating quantum technologies into real-world applications. Consequently, the investigation of practically motivated NP-hard problems is of significant value. This study investigates the performance of a variational quantum eigensolver (VQE) in addressing the traveling salesperson problem (TSP) through noiseless simulations representative of noisy intermediate-scale quantum (NISQ) devices using higher-order binary optimization (HOBO) encodings. We construct a HOBO Hamiltonian with an efficient binary representation and propose an all-valid-state HOBO (AVS-HOBO) scheme based on cyclic mapping that eliminates one penalty term and reuses states that would otherwise be invalid. Using TSP instances of up to 20 cities, we compare the original HOBO and AVS-HOBO encodings from multiple perspectives, including the energy convergence behavior and the approximation, tour-length, and feasibility ratios. In addition to simulations, we perform computations on real quantum hardware with different device architectures, where we not only compare the performances of different chips but also investigate the effects of different error-mitigation methods on actual quantum machines. The results indicate that AVS-HOBO encoding enhances the practical reliability of VQE on NISQ devices and improves scalability for larger TSP instances, with broader applicability to constrained quantum optimization problems.

</details>
