**Summary.** The paper introduces the Frontier decoder, an efficient approximation algorithm designed to solve the computationally hard problem of optimal quantum LDPC decoding. By pruning the state space during a dynamic programming traversal, it achieves near-optimal performance for various quantum error-correcting codes while keeping the computational complexity manageable and low-latency.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly scalable algorithmic tool for decoding complex quantum error-correcting codes. For AMO physicists working on quantum computation, it offers concrete benchmarks and insights into the feasibility of fault-tolerant quantum memory and processing using structured codes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop an efficient method for approximating the optimal decoding of quantum Low-Density Parity Check (LDPC) codes.

**Main result.** The proposed Frontier decoder achieves state-of-the-art performance, reaching thresholds close to optimal for surface and color codes under various noise models while maintaining a very small average computational overhead.

**Method.** It employs a pruned dynamic-programming approach that processes error variables in an ordered sequence, merging states with identical residual syndromes and logical labels while retaining only the most promising 'frontier' of possibilities based on a heuristic score.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on quantum error correction using LDPC codes (e.g., surface code, color code). The decoding process is modeled as finding the most probable recovery operation given an observed noisy syndrome record, which involves tracking syndromes and logical labels across variable processing steps.

**Key observables.** Full Logical Frame Error Rate (FER), average/peak retained list size ($|F_t|$), and the final maximum log-mass for each possible logical class ($\log bZ(F)_\lambda(s)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The noise level ($p$), the score gap ($\Delta$), and the hard memory cap ($K$) are crucial parameters controlling the approximation quality versus computational cost.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method approximates exact inference by pruning states based on a heuristic score, which assumes that the best path remains within a narrow frontier. The suffix-compatibility score relies on approximations of row marginals.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the decoding process steps (e.g., transition diagrams for BB codes), compare FER vs. noise level ($p$) against other decoders, and analyze computational efficiency metrics like average versus peak retained list sizes and latency tail distributions.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the Frontier decoder mechanism by detailing its ordered recursion structure, establishing its exactness when unpruned, and then presenting results comparing its performance (thresholds, FER) against established methods for various quantum codes under realistic noise models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce the Frontier decoder, a pruned dynamic-programming decoder for sparse quantum decoding problems. Frontier processes error variables in a chosen order, merges prefixes with the same residual syndrome and logical label, and approximates logical-coset posterior masses by retaining only a narrow scored frontier. Without pruning, the recursion is exact ordered inference with exponential complexity.   In the code-capacity setting, the decoder reaches thresholds close to optimal for the surface code and the color code. In the circuit-level noise model, it achieves state-of-the-art performance with a very small average retained list size: less than 100 for the gross code $[[144,12,12]]$ at a physical error rate of $0.001$. When the list size is constant, the decoder has linear complexity, suggesting the possibility of low-latency implementations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel and efficient method for preparing initial states corresponding to Complete Active Space (CAS) wavefunctions on quantum computers. By transforming the problem into a symmetry-adapted basis, they show that CAS states can be represented as Matrix Product States with polynomial bond dimension scaling. This leads to an overall state preparation complexity of $O(d^3)$, representing an exponential improvement over prior art for simulating strongly correlated electronic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The polynomial scaling achieved ($O(d^3)$) represents a major algorithmic breakthrough compared to exponential state preparation methods, making complex molecular simulations feasible on near-term quantum hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is developing quantum algorithms capable of efficiently preparing initial states with high overlap to the target eigenvector for simulating strongly correlated molecules.

**Main result.** The authors show that by utilizing the Quantum Paldus Transform (QPT) into a symmetry-adapted basis, CAS states can be represented as Matrix Product States (MPS) with polynomial bond dimension scaling ($O(d^2)$).

**Method.** They propose a three-step protocol: 1) Representing the CAS state as an MPS in the Unitary Group Algebra (UGA) basis. 2) Loading this MPS onto a quantum computer. 3) Applying the inverse QPT to transform the state into the Fock basis.

**Model / system.** The system is the electronic structure problem of molecules, specifically requiring the accurate description of strong electron correlation using Complete Active Space (CAS) wavefunctions.

**Key observables.** Gate complexity/Circuit depth, which scales polynomially as $O(d^3)$ for state preparation in the Fock basis.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The active space size ($d$) is the key parameter; the exponential scaling of traditional methods is overcome by polynomial scaling.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The efficiency relies on mapping the CAS state to a structured, symmetry-adapted basis (UGA) where an MPS representation with $O(d^2)$ bond dimension is possible.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structure of the MPS representation and the sequential quantum circuit required for preparing the MPS state, involving bond and temporary registers.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from identifying the intractability of CAS states in the Fock basis to introducing the QPT into a symmetry-adapted (UGA) basis. It then proves the efficient MPS representation ($\chi=O(d^2)$), details the quantum circuit for preparing this MPS, and finally combines these steps with the inverse QPT to achieve an overall $O(d^3)$ complexity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum computers promise to solve the electronic structure problem for a large class of molecules. However, the performance of relevant quantum algorithms hinges on preparing initial states with substantial overlap with the target eigenvector. For classically challenging molecules with strong electron correlation, starting from multi-reference states, such as complete active space (CAS) wavefunctions is necessary. Unfortunately, the most advanced state preparation protocols applied to such states result in a gate complexity that scales exponentially with the active space size $d$. In fact, even encoding a CAS state classically is traditionally believed to be intractable for chemically relevant systems. Here, we draw insights from the recently introduced Quantum Paldus Transform (QPT) to show that there exists an efficient classical representation of CAS states and to design a new state preparation routine outperforming previous ones. The QPT represents a transformation from the Fock basis to a friendlier symmetry-adapted basis. Our main contribution consists in showing that CAS states expanded in this basis can efficiently be represented as a matrix product state (MPS) with a bond dimension scaling as $O(d^2)$. One can then efficiently load the MPS on a quantum computer and use the inverse QPT to transform the state to the Fock basis. Moreover, our method can easily be extended to the efficient preparation of CAS states in first quantisation with similar complexity. Crucially, we demonstrate that the complexity of both state preparation protocols only grows polynomially as $O(d^3)$ , which constitutes to the best of our knowledge an exponential improvement over the state of the art.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a rigorous mathematical framework to study eigenvector varieties associated with linear families of quantum Hamiltonians. By employing advanced tools from algebraic geometry, it determines the precise geometric structure (dimension and degree) of these varieties for various physical models, such as fermionic and bosonic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep geometric insights into the spectral properties of Hamiltonians. Understanding $E(H)$ helps characterize the set of possible ground states or low-energy excitations in complex quantum systems, linking spectral theory directly to algebraic geometry.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper systematically studies the geometry of 'eigenvector varieties,' $E(H)$, which are subvarieties in projective space whose points correspond to eigenvectors for matrices drawn from a linear space $H$ of quantum system Hamiltonians.

**Main result.** Key results characterize these varieties using tools from algebraic geometry, showing that for fermionic systems, the variety is related to Grassmannians, and for bosonic systems, it relates to Veronese or Chow varieties.

**Method.** The analysis heavily employs advanced algebraic geometry techniques, including determinantal theory (minors of matrices), incidence varieties, elimination ideals, and intersection theory using Chow classes.

**Model / system.** The focus is on quantum mechanical systems modeled by Hamiltonians $H$ that are linear combinations of basis operators ($H = \sum t_i H_i$). Specific models include fermionic one-body operators (leading to Grassmannians) and bosonic one-body operators (leading to Veronese varieties).

**Key observables.** The dimension, degree, and irreducible components of the eigenvector variety $E(H)$ are key observables.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality ($n$) of the Hilbert space, the number of parameters ($d$), and the structure of the underlying Lie algebra ($\mathfrak{g}$) govern the geometry.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes generic conditions on the basis matrices $H_i$ or that certain associated varieties are irreducible to derive clean dimension formulas.

**Figures summary.** No specific figure descriptions were extracted, but the notes reference tables providing numerical verification of dimensional conjectures for fermionic operators.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from general algebraic characterizations (defining $E(H)$ via minors) to specialized physical applications. It systematically analyzes cases like generic pencils ($d=2$), 3D spaces ($d=3$), and finally tackles specific quantum models: fermionic one-body operators ($	ext{Gr}(k, m)$), bosonic one-body operators (Veronese varieties), and general Lie algebra representations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Any linear space of square matrices has an associated eigenvector variety. Its points are eigenvectors of matrices from that linear space. We present a systematic study of eigenvector varieties, with focus on Lie algebras and Hamiltonians of quantum systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a thermodynamic model describing macroions confined in a nano-reactor immersed in an electrolyte solution. By coupling Poisson-Boltzmann theory with charge regulation formalism, the authors analyze how surface charge asymmetry affects the potential profile and effective screening length. Key findings show that pressure decreases monotonically with increasing reactor size or salt concentration, providing insights into nanoscale electrostatics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical framework for understanding confined electrochemistry where surface chemistry (charge regulation) dictates macroscopic properties like osmotic pressure and electrostatic screening, relevant to biomolecular confinement.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study formulates a thermodynamic model for macroions confined within a nano-reactor, focusing on how charge regulation equilibria influence the electrostatic potential and pressure.

**Main result.** Surface charge asymmetry strongly dictates the spatial profile of the electrostatic potential, and the effective screening length exhibits non-monotonic behavior due to complex interplay between external solution and macroion charges.

**Method.** The analysis uses a thermodynamic framework minimizing a free energy functional, solved via linearized Debye-Hückel type approximations applied to Poisson-Boltzmann Charge Regulation (PB-CR) equations.

**Model / system.** The system is an electrolyte-permeable nano-reactor containing charge-regulated macroions. The model combines grand canonical ensembles for mobile ions with a fixed number of confined macroions, incorporating Langmuir isotherm descriptions for surface adsorption/desorption equilibria.

**Key observables.** Electrostatic potential profile ($\psi(r)$), ionic density distributions ($n_\pm$), total pressure at the enclosure boundary, and effective screening length ($\lambda_{eff}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Enclosure radius ($R$), bulk salt concentration ($n_0$), surface charge densities ($\sigma_R$, $\sigma_{R+w}$), and the number of confined macroions ($N_p$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on linearizing the full non-linear PB-CR equations (Debye-Hückel approximation) and assumes that the macroion cargo is fixed in number rather than being in bulk equilibrium.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate scaled electrostatic potential profiles ($eta_e\psi(r)$) for varying numbers of macroions, spatial charge density distributions showing asymmetry effects, and plots demonstrating monotonic pressure decrease with increasing radius or salt concentration.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the free energy functional incorporating charge regulation, linearizes the resulting coupled differential equations (PB-CR), solves for radial profiles in different regions (inside/wall/outside), and analyzes key observables like potential, screening length, and pressure dependence on system parameters.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We formulate a thermodynamic model of a nano-reactor containing charge-regulated macroions within an electrolyte-permeable enclosure. The model is then formalized within the Poisson-Boltzmann electrostatics augmented by the consistent inclusion of the charge dissociation of molecular groups residing on the surface of the entrapped macroions via charge regulation formalism. By solving the basic equilibrium equations in the linearized Debye-Hückel type approximation, we analyze the salient features of the inhomogeneous electrolyte distribution and macroion charge. We found that the surface charge asymmetry/symmetry of the macroions strongly affects the spatial profile of electrostatic potential. The effective screening length shows the non-monotonic behavior, arising from the complex interplay between the bathing external solution and macroion effective charges, which govern charge regulation equilibria. The total pressure at the nano-reactor enclosure boundary decreases monotonically as the enclosure radius and the ionic bulk salt concentration increase. Also, the resulting pressure is strongly influenced by the surface charge densities of the nano-reactor and the number of confined macroions.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study uses an itinerant multi-orbital framework to map out the magnetic ground states in multilayer nickelates. By calculating spin excitation spectra for various SDW symmetries, the authors argue that these excitations serve as a sensitive probe distinguishing between competing magnetic orders. The results strongly support the mirror-odd SDW state and confirm the itinerant nature of magnetism in these high-$T_c$ materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the itinerant nature of the model and the use of orbital degrees of freedom place it within the broader context of strongly correlated electron physics relevant to unconventional superconductivity mechanisms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the magnetic ground states and transverse spin excitations in high-temperature superconducting multilayer nickelates to understand the microscopic origin of magnetism.

**Main result.** The calculations favor the mirror-odd Spin Density Wave (SDW) state in trilayer systems, while for bilayers, the single-stripe state's excitation spectrum shows good qualitative agreement with experimental RIXS and neutron scattering data.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis using a multi-orbital itinerant framework Hamiltonian solved via Hartree-Fock mean-field theory, followed by Random Phase Approximation (RPA) calculations for spin susceptibility.

**Model / system.** The system models bilayer and trilayer nickelates in the Ruddlesden–Popper phase, described by an itinerant model incorporating onsite Hubbard interactions and Hund's coupling on Ni $e_g$ orbitals. The analysis compares different SDW symmetries (mirror-odd vs. mirror-even).

**Key observables.** Transverse spin excitation spectra ($	ext{Im}[\chi^{+-}]$), magnetic order types (single/double stripe, mirror-odd/even SDW), and optical interlayer modes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strengths ($U, J$), layer number (bilayer vs. trilayer), and momentum transfer points like $Q_{	ext{BL}}$ and $	ilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{	ext{TL}}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mean-field decoupling (Hartree-Fock) and the Random Phase Approximation (RPA) for excitations, which may neglect certain correlation effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate Fermi surfaces, real-space magnetic patterns for different SDW states, and momentum/energy spectra ($	ext{Im}[\chi^{+-}]$) comparing various magnetic orders.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the bilayer case (single vs. double stripe), then moves to the trilayer case by comparing mirror-odd and mirror-even SDW candidates, culminating in a comparison of excitation signatures against experimental data.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the magnetic ground states and transverse spin excitations of bilayer and trilayer nickelates within a multi-orbital itinerant framework. For the bilayer system, although Hartree-Fock calculations slightly favor a double-stripe order, the calculated excitation spectrum of the single-stripe state, characterized by an anisotropic low-energy cone at $Q_{\text{BL}}$ and isotropic high-energy excitations near $Γ$, exhibits good qualitative agreement with recent RIXS and neutron scattering experiments. We further identify mirror-even optical interlayer modes at $Q_{\text{BL}}$ whose energies match the mirror-odd modes at $Γ$. For the trilayer system, both mirror-odd and mirror-even spin-density-wave states can be stabilized near $Q_{\text{TL}}$, with the mirror-odd state lower in energy in the parameter regime studied. The mirror-odd state hosts an additional nearly gapless mode dominated by the middle layer, while the mirror-even state contains only one acoustic branch together with two gapped optical modes. Comparison with available RIXS data favors the mirror-odd spin-density-wave scenario. Our results show that magnetic excitations provide a sensitive probe of the magnetic order and support a common itinerant origin of magnetism in multilayer nickelates.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a comprehensive methodology to optimize Variational Quantum Algorithms (VQAs) like VQE by balancing accuracy against computational resources in noisy environments. It develops scaling relations that pinpoint an optimal balance between quantum circuit size and the number of required iterations. This provides essential, practical guidelines for deploying these algorithms on real-world, imperfect quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial resource management framework for near-term quantum computation, directly addressing the practical limitations of NISQ devices by quantifying the necessary trade-off between circuit depth and iterative optimization steps.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing methodologies to optimize Variational Quantum Algorithms (VQAs) to achieve a target output accuracy while minimizing the total algorithmic resource cost.

**Main result.** The authors establish scaling relations that allow identifying an optimal balance between quantum circuit size ($N_g$) and number of iterations ($N_{it}$) to minimize resources for a desired accuracy, even on noisy hardware.

**Method.** They develop a Metric–Noise–Resource (MNR) framework, deriving phenomenological scaling relations by analyzing the convergence behavior of VQE under global depolarizing noise models.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE), exemplified using the 5-spin Heisenberg chain Hamiltonian. The analysis treats the algorithm's performance as a function of circuit depth and iteration count.

**Key observables.** Algorithmic accuracy ($\approx 1 - \delta_E$), Algorithmic Resource Cost ($\Delta = N_g N_{it}$), and Efficiency ($\eta = M/\Delta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise strength ($\epsilon$), optimal gate count ($N_{	ext{opt},g}$), convergence rate parameters ($\mu, \kappa$), and the total resource budget ($\Delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes a global depolarizing noise model for tractability, although it is shown to provide practical guidelines for hardware with error mitigation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate VQE energy convergence vs. iterations/circuit size (Fig. 4), the optimal gate count dependence on noise (Fig. 6), and heatmaps showing resource cost and efficiency contours across $N_{it}$ and $N_g$ (Fig. 7).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the optimization goal using VQE, deriving scaling relations for noiseless convergence, incorporating noise effects to find optimal trade-offs ($N_{	ext{opt},g}$), and finally validating these theoretical guidelines on simulated noisy hardware.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

For quantum algorithms to achieve their full potential, we need methodologies to optimize them, such as reaching a given output accuracy with minimal resource costs. Here, we develop such a methodology for a class of Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) algorithms. We leverage simulations of a Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE) to propose a phenomenological model of such algorithms that captures the complex relationship between algorithmic accuracy, algorithmic resource costs, and the noise that exists in realistic quantum hardware. For this, we take the algorithmic resource cost to be the total number of quantum gate-operations in the algorithm; minimizing this cost typically makes the algorithm faster and more energy-efficient. We consider the subtle trade-off between quantum circuit size (small circuits are too imprecise, but large ones are too noisy), and the number of iterations of that quantum circuit for the full algorithm to sufficiently converge. Using a noise-metric-resource methodology, we identify the sweet spot (of circuit size versus iterations) that minimizes the algorithmic resource costs for a desired algorithm accuracy. It also gives the circuit size that maximizes algorithm accuracy for a fixed resource cost. Our methodology provides a practical guideline for near-term deployment of variational algorithms on realistic noisy hardware, including hardware that uses error mitigation.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper addresses the experimental difficulty of measuring high-order quantum moments using multiple state copies. It proposes replacing full coherent measurements with random projections onto smaller subspaces, which allows researchers to tune the protocol's resource cost. This framework provides a scalable method to estimate complex quantum properties on near-term hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, resource-aware roadmap for implementing complex quantum tomography protocols on NISQ devices by systematically trading off full coherence requirements for increased statistical sampling overhead.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is estimating nonlinear properties of quantum states, such as multivariate traces $	ext{tr}(ho_1 \cdots ho_K)$, when standard multi-copy measurement protocols require unfeasible coherent operations on large Hilbert spaces.

**Main result.** The authors introduce a framework using random projections onto lower-dimensional subspaces to achieve a tunable tradeoff between required coherent resources and statistical sampling overhead, enabling optimization for near-term hardware.

**Method.** They derive explicit formulas relating Haar-averaged projected moments ($\overline{\sigma}_K$) to the target traces, utilizing tools from character theory and Weingarten calculus to reconstruct high-order spectral moments recursively.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on quantum states $ho$ (density matrices) acting on $n$-qubit systems. The methodology is general but tested using concepts applicable to thermal states of models like the transverse-field Ising model.

**Key observables.** Multivariate traces ($	ext{tr}(ho^K)$), Haar-averaged projected moments ($\overline{\sigma}_K^{(d,m)}$), and estimation error $\epsilon_p$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size ($n$), projection rank ($q$ or $m$), number of copies ($K$), and the ratio of coherent to sampled resources.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximating Haar-random unitaries using shallow random circuits (brickwork approximation) and assumes that the error sources (unitary sampling vs. shot noise) can be independently analyzed and combined.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare estimation errors across different protocols (fully local vs. projected), showing how increasing projection rank ($q$) reduces required sample complexity, and illustrate the scaling of standard deviation with the number of measurements $N$.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the problem limitation (full coherence needed) to proposing random projections as a solution. It then develops theoretical tools using character theory to calculate projected moments, establishes a moment reconstruction hierarchy, and finally analyzes the scaling of estimation error based on resource allocation ($N$ vs $q$).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Estimating nonlinear properties of quantum states is a central task in quantum information science. Multivariate traces, $\mathrm{tr}(ρ_1 \cdots ρ_K)$, and nonlinear observables such as $\mathrm{tr}(ρ^K)$, for integer $K$, can be accessed through collective measurements on multiple state copies, but standard protocols based on swap tests require coherent operations on the full Hilbert space and become experimentally unfeasible for large systems. In this work, we introduce a framework for multi-copy measurements based on random projections onto lower-dimensional subspaces prior to the collective measurement, which is then performed only on the reduced Hilbert space. This procedure yields a tunable tradeoff between coherent quantum resources and statistical sampling overhead, allowing the amount of coherent processing to be matched to the capabilities of the underlying hardware. We derive explicit formulas relating the Haar-averaged projected moments to multivariate traces of the original states and analyze the sampling overhead induced by the projection procedure. Specifically, after compressing an $n$-qubit state to a reduced $q$-qubit subspace, estimating $\mathrm{tr}(ρ^K)$ requires approximately $O(2^{(n-q)(K-1)})$ copies of $ρ$, with each qubit projected out increasing the sampling cost by a factor of $2^{K-1}$. Our results establish how coherent multi-copy operations can be traded for additional state copies, enabling multi-copy quantum protocols to be optimized for the available hardware resources.

</details>

### [Ricci flow for the Bures--Helstrom qubit metric](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19493v1)

**Authors:** Andrew Lesniewski  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.19493v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `entanglement & information structure` **1/5**

</div>

**Summary.** This paper applies Ricci flow theory to analyze the geometry of the qubit state space, specifically using the Bures--Helstrom metric. It shows that while the unnormalized flow collapses in finite time, the volume-normalized flow stabilizes at the Bures--Helstrom metric, confirming its role as a geometrically stable fixed point within the cone of monotone metrics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The application of geometric evolution equations like Ricci flow to quantum state spaces provides deep insights into how physical constraints (like monotonicity) dictate the long-term dynamics of quantum information measures. The explicit solvability of this specific metric is a major theoretical achievement.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper describes the intrinsic Ricci flow of the Bures--Helstrom metric on the state space of a qubit.

**Main result.** The unnormalized flow exhibits finite-time collapse as a homothetic shrinker, while the volume-normalized flow fixes this scale and shows the Bures--Helstrom metric is an asymptotically stable fixed point.

**Method.** The analysis employs Ricci flow techniques, utilizing gauge fixing (Hamilton--DeTurck) to reduce the evolution equations for the warping factor ($\Phi$) to a linear forced heat equation in specific coordinates.

**Model / system.** The system is the state space of a single qubit, whose geometry is endowed with the Bures--Helstrom metric. This metric is characterized as the minimal monotone Riemannian metric on this space.

**Key observables.** Warping function ($\Phi$) and its square ($\Psi$), scalar curvature ($R$), and the flow's evolution relative to the homothetic shrinker $g(t)=(1-4t)g_{\mathrm{BH}}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The extinction time for the unnormalized flow is $T=1/4$. The spectral gap of the linearized normalized flow is 5.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes rotational symmetry for the metric and relies on choosing specific gauges, such as the Hamilton--DeTurck gauge, to simplify the differential equations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the contraction of the warping profile ($\Phi$) and the divergence of scalar curvature ($R/6 \propto 1/(1-4t)$) at $T=1/4$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the Bures--Helstrom metric as an Einstein manifold. It then derives the coupled Ricci flow equations for general rotationally symmetric metrics, fixes the gauge to obtain a single scalar equation for $\Psi$, and finally analyzes both the unnormalized (collapsing) and volume-normalized (stable fixed point) flows.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Bures--Helstrom metric is the minimal monotone Riemannian metric on the state space of a qubit. With the quantum Fisher normalization used here, it identifies the Bloch ball with a geodesic hemisphere of the unit round three--sphere. We describe its Ricci flow explicitly. In a general rotationally symmetric gauge the flow is a coupled system for the radial lapse and warping factor; a single scalar equation appears only after a Hamilton--DeTurck gauge choice. In the corresponding moving DeTurck frame the squared warping function $Ψ=Φ^2$ satisfies the linear forced heat equation \begin{equation*} D_tΨ=Ψ_{ss}-2, \end{equation*} while the fixed-lapse coordinate form contains the associated transport term. Since the Bures--Helstrom metric is Einstein, the geometric flow itself is the homothetic shrinker \begin{equation*} g(t)=(1-4t)g_{\mathrm{BH}}, \end{equation*} with scalar curvature $6/(1-4t)$ and extinction time $T=1/4$. Thus the metric remains inside the monotone cone for all $t<T$ and leaves the cone of nondegenerate Riemannian metrics only through the collapsed limit. We also record the volume--normalized flow, for which the Bures--Helstrom metric is a fixed point. Its linearization is the shifted round--sphere Laplacian $Δ_{\mathbb S^3}+3$, with spectrum \begin{equation*} σ_\ell=-(\ell-1)(\ell+3), \end{equation*} and spectral gap $5$ after removal of the scaling mode.

</details>

### [$K$-Theoretic Obstructions to Linearizing QCA Representations](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19657v1)

**Authors:** Mattie Ji, Bowen Yang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.19657v1>

Here we place an alternating sequence of A ⊗A′ ∼= Mn(F) and B ⊗B′ ∼= Mm(F) on the metric space Z, and α simultaneously transports the tensor factor B to the left and the tensor factor A to the right.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Summary.** This paper develops an advanced obstruction theory using K-theory to determine when Quantum Cellular Automata (QCA) representations can be linearized into standard linear group actions. By analyzing universal obstruction classes derived from the homotopy type of QCA spaces, it establishes precise mathematical criteria for physical systems modeled by QCA to possess simple, linear dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep mathematical framework connecting quantum computation models (QCA) to fundamental algebraic topology invariants. The explicit calculation of obstruction classes over number fields suggests powerful, non-trivial constraints on physically realizable quantum dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is developing an obstruction theory to determine when a Quantum Cellular Automata (QCA) representation, which models quantum spin systems with locality constraints, can be linearized into a standard linear group representation.

**Main result.** The authors establish that the linearizability of QCA representations is governed by the vanishing of specific universal obstruction classes derived from algebraic K-theory spectra. They provide explicit conditions for this vanishing in various settings (e.g., finite groups, rational coefficients).

**Method.** The methodology heavily employs advanced algebraic topology, specifically utilizing stable homotopy theory, K-theory spectra, and cohomology theories to construct and analyze obstruction cocycles.

**Model / system.** The model is the QCA representation $\varphi: G 	o Q(X, q)$, where $G$ is a group acting on quantum spin systems defined over a metric space $X$. The locality constraints are encoded in the structure of the algebra of local observables $A(X, q)$.

**Key observables.** Universal obstruction classes ($u_i(\varphi)$), stabilization maps ($\varphi_{st}$), and specific cohomology/homotopy groups (e.g., $	ext{H}^k(X; \mathbb{F}_p)$, $\pi_i(	ext{K}(C(X)))$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The field of coefficients ($\mathbb{F}$, $\mathbb{Q}$), the structure of the metric space $X$ (e.g., point, line, plane, lattice $\mathbb{Z}^d$), and the group $G$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory relies on establishing equivalences between different notions of linearizability (linearizable vs. stably linearizable) and assuming certain algebraic properties for the underlying spaces $X$ and groups $G$ (e.g., $G$ being rationally acyclic).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate specific examples of QCA ($\alpha$) associated with central simple algebras or annular shifts on $\mathbb{Z}^2$, used to demonstrate non-circuit, non-linearizable cases.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the single-particle case (Theorem 1.1) to generalizing the obstruction theory for QCA representations over arbitrary fields. It systematically defines various notions of linearizability and derives necessary and sufficient conditions using K-theoretic obstructions across different coefficient rings ($\mathbb{C}$, $\mathbb{R}$, finite fields).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Projective representations arise naturally in physics and representation theory, and determining whether they can be linearized has been a fundamental problem. In this work, we study the analogous problem for quantum cellular automata (QCA) representations, which incorporate locality constraints imposed by a metric space $X$. Over an arbitrary field $\mathbb{F}$, we develop an obstruction theory for the linearization of QCA representations, using the algebraic $K$-theory spectrum of QCA constructed in previous work of the authors. The resulting obstructions are governed by the homotopy type of the QCA spaces, from which we extract universal obstruction classes to linearization. In the complex algebraic and unitary case, we also fully compute the homotopy types of the QCA spaces over a point, a line, and a plane.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors successfully applied a modified Quantum Annealer Eigensolver (QAE) algorithm on D-Wave hardware to calculate magnetic dipole hyperfine structure constants for several light atoms. By benchmarking these results against high-level classical calculations (GRASP), they demonstrated that quantum annealing can accurately determine complex atomic properties, marking an extension of the technique beyond ground-state energy determination.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly tests the capability of quantum annealers to solve complex, physically relevant many-body problems beyond simple energy minimization, providing a crucial benchmark for quantum simulation in atomic physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to calculate magnetic dipole hyperfine structure (HFS) constants for several light atoms using quantum annealing techniques, extending the application of these methods beyond simple ground-state energy calculations.

**Main result.** Quantum hardware demonstrations yielded HFS constant results completely consistent with established classical relativistic atomic structure calculations (GRASP) at the specified precision.

**Method.** The problem is mapped onto a QUBO formulation suitable for quantum annealing, utilizing a modified Quantum Annealer Eigensolver (QAE) approach combined with a zooming-and-sigma-annealing technique.

**Model / system.** The calculations focus on neutral and alkali-like atoms ($	ext{Li}, 	ext{Be}, 	ext{Na}, 	ext{Mg}$) using the relativistic Dirac-Coulomb Hamiltonian. The methodology involves constructing matrix elements from Configuration State Functions (CSFs).

**Key observables.** Magnetic dipole HFS constants ($A$) and ground-state eigenvalues/eigenvectors of the $	ext{HDC}$ matrix.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The QAE precision is limited to three decimal places, corresponding to up to 10 qubits; hardware constraints limit the number of CSFs used (e.g., $B=11$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** A CSF truncation scheme is employed for larger basis sets, estimating contributions based on approximations derived from the independent electron pair model.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the iterative 'zooming' process used to improve eigenvalue precision during quantum annealing simulations.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents comparisons of calculated CSF coefficients and HFS constants across multiple atomic species ($	ext{Li}, 	ext{Be}^+, 	ext{Na}, 	ext{Mg}^+$) using three methods: GRASP (reference), Simulated Annealing (SA), and Quantum Annealing (QA).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report the first results of the magnetic dipole hyperfine structure (HFS) constants of neutral $\mathrm{Li}$, Li-like $\mathrm{Be}$, neutral $\mathrm{Na}$, and Na-like $\mathrm{Mg}$ using a modified version of the Quantum Annealer Eigensolver (QAE) algorithm on D-Wave's quantum hardware. The results are benchmarked against relativistic configuration interaction with multiconfiguration Dirac Hartree-Fock (MCDHF) calculations using the General-purpose Relativistic Atomic Structure Package (GRASP), and simulated annealing. In our modified QAE, a zooming-and-sigma-annealing approach with a floating-point encoding scheme is adopted to estimate the ground-state eigenvalue and eigenvector of the relativistic Dirac-Coulomb Hamiltonian matrices ($H_{\mathrm{DC}}$) constructed from 11 or fewer configuration state functions (CSFs). For calculations with extended correlation orbital sets, we applied a CSF truncation scheme, retaining only CSFs (up to 12) that make significant contributions to the ground-state wavefunction. Our modified QAE precision is kept limited to three decimal places (up to 10 qubits). Hardware demonstrations on the D-Wave quantum processing unit (QPU) yielded results that were completely consistent with GRASP (at the chosen precision) in determining the magnetic dipole HFS constants, with accuracy varying across systems and $H_{\mathrm{DC}}$ matrix dimensions.

</details>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 3.</b> Fig. 3. Controls and ablations. (a) Parameter-matched random sharing underperforms group-orbit sharing. (b) Ablations are anchored by the labelled edge/D4 ansatz; the strongest model combines edge interactions with both line channels. (c) Accuracy is not explained by parameter count alone.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** The paper investigates how to optimally constrain Variational Quantum Circuits by leveraging problem symmetries in VQML. By testing on Tic-Tac-Toe, the authors find that while symmetry defines a necessary admissible space, achieving peak performance requires supplementing this constraint with trainable interactions directly targeting known, critical features of the task.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete framework for incorporating domain knowledge (symmetry) into quantum circuit design, which is crucial for building practical, resource-efficient quantum algorithms beyond simple pattern matching.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is designing Variational Quantum Machine Learning (VQML) models that are not just expressive, but optimally constrained by the underlying symmetries of the problem to improve generalization.

**Main result.** While enforcing symmetry provides a significant benefit over unconstrained models, the largest performance gains come from adding trainable interactions specifically tailored to known, decisive task motifs, suggesting task knowledge guides effective ansatz design within the admissible symmetric space.

**Method.** The authors compare different parameter sharing strategies (varying subgroup constraints) and test the impact of adding specific, symmetry-respecting gates (winning-line gates) on a fully enumerable quantum game model.

**Model / system.** The study uses Variational Quantum Circuits (VQC) applied to the game Tic-Tac-Toe. The input data is encoded using $RX$ rotations, and symmetries are enforced by restricting trainable parameters via parameter sharing based on subgroups of the dihedral group $D_4$.

**Key observables.** Test accuracy (the primary metric), generalization gap (difference between training and test error), and the number of trainable parameters.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The imposed symmetry subgroup ($H \subseteq D_4$), the specific interactions added (e.g., ZZZ or CCRZ gates on winning triples), and optimization hyperparameters like learning rate and epochs.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study relies on Tic-Tac-Toe being a fully enumerable and structurally transparent test case, allowing for precise identification of task-relevant motifs.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 demonstrates that enforcing $D_4$ symmetry improves accuracy over no constraint, and further shows that adding trainable gates corresponding to winning lines provides the largest boost in performance. Figure 3 compares random vs. orbit-based sharing and confirms that parameter count alone does not explain all gains.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need for structure-aware VQML models by first showing how symmetry constrains the design space. It then systematically investigates two axes of freedom: varying the strength of symmetry enforcement, and adding task-specific interactions on top of the base symmetric ansatz.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The success of variational quantum learning models crucially depends on choosing parametrizations that reflect the structure of the problem at hand. Symmetries provide one of the clearest such structures: whenever transformations of the input leave the desired outcome unchanged, this invariance should be built into the model rather than discovered during training. However, imposing a symmetry does not by itself determine a useful ansatz. Even within the symmetry-preserving space, one must decide where the trainable degrees of freedom should be placed. In this work, we study this remaining design freedom in equivariant variational quantum circuits. Building on symmetry-based parameter sharing, we disentangle two architectural choices: how much symmetry should be enforced, and which symmetry-respecting interactions should be trainable. Using Tic-Tac-Toe as a fully enumerable and structurally transparent test case, we find that suitable subgroups preserve most of the generalization benefit. By contrast, the dominant gains arise from gates acting directly on decisive task motifs. Thus, symmetry defines the admissible design space, while effective ansatze require an additional task-informed choice of trainable interactions.

</details>

**Authors:** Maarten Grothus, V. Vilasini  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.20476v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

Here, ↠denotes causal relations between two RVs, while its dashed version stands for alternative causal relations, e.g. Y ↠AB being equivalent to (Y ↠A or Y ↠B), so at least one of the dashed arrows from Y must be present as a causal relation. (b) A compatible, non-degenerate embedding of these affects relations into (1 + 1)-Minkowski spacetime, where all ORVs need be located on certain light-like surfaces.</figcaption>

(a) For d = 1 Minkowski spacetime does not show conicality. This is because L1 := {a, b} and L2 := {x, y} are distinct, yet share the same joint future. (b) For d = 2, Minkowski spacetime is conical. As can be seen from the figure which shows one particular time slice, the joint futures are distinct between L1 and L2 (and the red region is thus not covered by the blue one).</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper investigates the relationship between superluminal signaling constraints (NSS) and causal loops in spacetime. By introducing the concept of 'conicality,' the authors prove that this geometric property forces any compatible causal structure to be non-looping, thereby establishing a deep link between local causality principles and global spacetime geometry.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly related to quantum many-body physics, the rigorous mathematical framework for causality and information flow across curved spacetimes is foundational for theories involving quantum gravity or relativistic quantum field theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is determining whether the principle of No Superluminal Signalling (NSS) inherently rules out all operationally detectable causal loops in spacetimes that possess a 'conical' geometric structure.

**Main result.** The authors prove a general no-go result: in any conical spacetime, NSS rules out all operationally verifiable causal loops, establishing that this relationship depends fundamentally on the geometry of spacetime.

**Method.** The analysis uses an advanced framework combining causal inference (affects relations) with geometric constraints derived from poset theory, specifically leveraging the concept of 'conicality' in the spacetime embedding.

**Model / system.** The model involves describing causality using directed graphs and affects relations, which are then embedded into a partially ordered set (poset) representing the spacetime light cone structure. The focus is on 'conical' spacetimes, including $d>1$ Minkowski space.

**Key observables.** Operationally detectable causal loops (ACLs), compatibility conditions derived from affects relations ($	ext{X} \models 	ext{Y} | 	ext{do}(	ext{Z})$), and the geometric property of conicality.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The spatial dimension $d$ (comparing $d=1$ vs. $d>1$), and the irreducibility conditions ($	ext{Irred1}, 	ext{Irred3}$) applied to affects relations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The entire proof structure relies heavily on assuming a conical embedding of the causal model into spacetime, as this geometric property is key to deriving contradictions that rule out loops.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates complex examples of ACLs in $(1+1)$-Minkowski space. Figure 2 compares light cone representations for $d=1$ (non-conical) versus $d>1$ (conical), showing how joint futures differ.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the framework by first defining affects relations and conical embeddings, then proving necessary lemmas that simplify higher-order conditional affects relations ($	ext{Lemma A.7}$). Finally, it applies these results to prove that any compatible embedding of ACLs in a conical spacetime must be degenerate (Theorem A.9).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In PRL 129, 110401 it was shown that it is theoretically possible to have operationally detectable causal loops without violating the principle of no superluminal signalling (NSS) in (1+1)-Minkowski spacetime. Whether or not such causal loops are also possible in $d > 1$ spatial dimensions, has remained a key open question. We resolve this question by showing that in a wide class of "conical" spacetimes, including Minkowski with d > 1, NSS does rule out all operationally detectable causal loops, in classical, quantum and post-quantum theories. This establishes that the relationship between the relativistic principles of NSS and no causal loops depends inherently on the geometry of spacetime.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.20168v1>

Different colours represent the action of B-operators with different arguments, with x1 = −7</figcaption>

**Summary.** The paper tackles the complex problem of characterizing all eigenstates, not just the highest-weight states, within the Yangian symmetry framework of the Haldane–Shastry spin chain. By developing systematic methods using algebraic Bethe ansatz and determinant formulas, it provides explicit formulae for the norms and overlaps of these descendant states. This advances the theoretical understanding of integrable quantum many-body systems with long-range interactions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep spectral information about an integrable quantum many-body system whose structure is governed by infinite symmetries (Yangian), which is crucial for understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in 1D quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to systematically construct an orthogonal eigenbasis for the Yangian descendant states of the Haldane–Shastry (HS) spin chain and compute their norms and overlaps.

**Main result.** Product and determinant formulae are derived for the norms and overlaps of these descendant states, establishing a complete understanding of the spectral structure beyond the highest-weight states.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced techniques from the Algebraic Bethe Ansatz (ABA), leveraging connections to spin-Calogero–Sutherland systems and utilizing Jack polynomials and determinant formulas (Gaudin/Slavnov).

**Model / system.** The system is the Haldane–Shastry (HS) spin chain, an integrable model with long-range interactions, which serves as a discrete analogue of a conformal field theory. Its integrability is governed by full Yangian symmetry.

**Key observables.** Norms and overlaps of descendant states ($\langle u|uangle$, $\langle v|uangle$), energy spectrum $E(\mu)$, and the structure of Yangian multiplets labeled by motifs $\mu$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The twist parameter $\kappa$ (analyzed in limits like $\kappa=1$ for $\mathfrak{sl}_2$ symmetry, and $\kappa 	o \infty$ for the Gelfand–Tsetlin basis).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on the completeness of the ABA framework and specific mathematical identities related to Jack polynomials.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structure of Yangian multiplets, showing descendant towers in both the extreme twist (GT) limit and the periodic ($\kappa=1$) limit, analogous to known spin chain structures.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews existing knowledge on HS symmetry, details the connection to spin-CS systems via freezing limits, constructs the descendant states using ABA techniques, and concludes by deriving explicit formulas for norms and overlaps.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Haldane-Shastry spin chain is a prototypical integrable model with long-range interactions, notable for hosting quasiparticles with fractional statistics and serving as a discrete analogue of a conformal field theory. Its remarkable simplicity is closely tied to a full Yangian spin symmetry. While the highest-weight states for this symmetry are known explicitly, a systematic treatment of the descendant states, needed for the computation of various physical quantities, has remained incomplete. In this work, we provide a detailed construction of these descendants in terms of the algebraic Bethe ansatz following recent work of Ferrando et al. In the limit of extreme twist, it includes the Gelfand-Tsetlin basis. As an application, we derive product and determinant formulae for norms and overlaps of these states.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes a comprehensive variational framework to compile arbitrary classical operators—from those governing quantum dynamics to those from fluid mechanics—into compact quantum circuits. By using backpropagation and block encoding, it overcomes the limitations of standard Trotterization and tensor network methods. This universal approach establishes a powerful programming layer for realizing complex physical simulations on near-term quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a universal, hardware-agnostic compilation layer that bridges continuous mathematical models (PDEs, flow) directly into discrete quantum gate sequences, which is crucial for near-term quantum advantage demonstrations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Efficient quantum algorithm implementation is limited by the lack of efficient primitives for arbitrary operators and state preparation.

**Main result.** The framework successfully learns compact quantum circuits to approximate complex, general-purpose operators with improved resource scaling over standard Trotter expansions.

**Method.** A full-stack variational approach uses backpropagation and a cost function to transform arbitrary operators (unitary or non-unitary) into trainable quantum circuits via block encoding.

**Model / system.** The framework is tested across diverse domains: simulating time evolution in quantum chemistry, solving PDEs using the Laplace operator, and modeling potential flow around an airfoil.

**Key observables.** Relative error ($\epsilon$), success probability ($p$), objective function value ($J$), and resource scaling (circuit depth/qubits).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time step ($\delta t$), system size ($n$ or $n_s$), regularization strength ($ho$), and ancilla qubit count ($n_a$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the target operator can be approximated by a quantum circuit ansatz, and it utilizes techniques like SVD projection to enforce constraints.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the overall pipeline (discretization $ightarrow$ encoding $ightarrow$ hardware), compare error scaling against ST methods for time evolution, and show optimization strategy comparisons for PDEs and fluid dynamics.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the block-encoding methodology for non-unitary operators, presents the hierarchical optimization protocol using automatic differentiation and SVD projection, and validates this approach across quantum simulation, PDE solving, and classical fluid mechanics problems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

An efficient implementation of quantum algorithms is often hindered by the lack of efficient primitives for operators and state preparation. This limits both the ability of near-term quantum hardware to simulate complex problems and the potential of fault-tolerant algorithms to achieve practical quantum advantage. To address this, we propose a full-stack variational framework that transforms arbitrary operators to compact quantum circuits. The resulting variational circuits can be tailored to the connectivity and long-range interaction of the target hardware. The learning process employs backpropagation together with a cost function that efficiently optimizes unitary operators and non-unitary -- dense or sparse -- operators using only a single ancilla qubit for block encoding. Additionally, we introduce a regularization term that reduces the approximation error. The approach is validated for both quantum mechanical and engineering applications. In the former case, we learn propagators that arise in native quantum problems -- such as quantum simulation and quantum chemistry -- and achieve improved resource scaling in comparison to standard Suzuki-Trotter expansions. In the latter case, we demonstrate the approach's ability to implement the second-order central finite difference approximation of the Laplace operator -- relevant for solving partial differential equations -- while improving upon current error metrics. The final example deals with learning a dense, non-unitary operator that arises in the analysis of inviscid potential flow around an airfoil. This universality of the framework opens the door for solving general problems beyond prototypical engineering and quantum applications.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes the Grover Adaptive Search Self-Consistent Field (GAS-SCF) algorithm to solve electronic structure problems by treating the SCF minimization as a QUBO optimization problem. It leverages quantum arithmetic and amplitude amplification to achieve a theoretical quadratic speedup over classical search methods. While demonstrating potential on small systems, its full utility is contingent upon fault-tolerant, large-scale quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly tackles a fundamental problem in quantum chemistry—the SCF calculation—by mapping its optimization structure onto a known quantum speedup primitive (Grover's search), providing a concrete roadmap for near-term quantum simulation algorithms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a quantum algorithm, GAS-SCF, to accelerate the Self-Consistent Field (SCF) calculation, which is an optimization problem in quantum chemistry.

**Main result.** GAS-SCF theoretically offers a quadratic speedup for the SCF optimization by using Grover's search mechanism within a hybrid classical/quantum framework. The authors demonstrate its potential on small molecular systems, finding energy improvements over classical benchmarks.

**Method.** The method combines quantum arithmetic (using QFT) to construct an oracle that marks target Fock states satisfying energy criteria, followed by amplitude amplification via Grover's algorithm. This is wrapped in a classical outer loop that iteratively updates the Molecular Orbital basis set.

**Model / system.** The model is electronic structure calculation using the Hartree-Fock approximation, formulated as minimizing an expectation value over a Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) problem defined by molecular orbital occupancies. Systems tested include H3-, LiH, and O2/O3.

**Key observables.** Minimum energy expectation value ($\langle\vec{x}| H_{	ext{QUBO}} |\vec{x}angle$), overlap between true wavefunctions and reference determinants, and the probability of measuring the optimal bitstring as a function of search repetitions ($L$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The required number of qubits scales with the size of the system (up to 330 qubits mentioned), and the complexity depends on the structure of the QUBO coefficients.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Achieving chemically relevant results requires large-scale, fault-tolerant quantum hardware. The analysis relies on classical simulation of the quantum steps and assumes specific circuit constructions for arithmetic operations (e.g., QFT-based addition).

**Figures summary.** Figures detail the full GAS-SCF algorithm flow, the quantum circuits for implementing necessary components like quantum addition in the Fourier basis, state marking based on energy thresholds, and reflection operators.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the SCF problem mapped to QUBO. It then details the GAS-SCF hybrid algorithm, showing circuit implementations for arithmetic (QFT/Fourier Basis) and constraint enforcement (particle number). Finally, it presents results comparing GAS-SCF performance against classical benchmarks on various molecules.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present the Grover adaptive search self-consistent field (GAS-SCF) algorithm. GAS-SCF leverages quantum arithmetic to construct an efficient oracle that marks target states (Fock states) which improve upon some initial classical energy estimate. Amplitude amplification then increases the probability of measuring these states. This approach offers a theoretical quadratic speed-up for the optimization problem encountered in SCF quantum chemistry and establishes a baseline against which structured optimization algorithms, such as QAOA and DQI may be compared. In this work, we classically simulate three examples as proofs of concept of the algorithm, the largest consisting of 26 qubits. We then extend our analysis to two larger systems, with O3 representing the largest case at 330 qubits. These examples are chosen to probe classically challenging SCF regimes. Achieving chemically relevant applications of GAS-SCF will require large-scale, fault-tolerant quantum hardware.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops Sparse Configuration Interaction (SCI) methods to solve the electronic Schrödinger equation by rigorously analyzing function spaces using Meyer wavelets. It proves that SCI mitigates the curse of dimensionality in basis set expansions, leading to convergence rates independent of electron number $N$. This breakthrough offers a path toward simulating strongly correlated systems with drastically reduced computational scaling for both classical and quantum computing platforms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical framework showing how advanced functional analysis techniques can overcome fundamental exponential scaling barriers in many-body physics simulations, directly impacting the feasibility of simulating complex chemical systems on both classical and quantum hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop rigorous, scalable approximation methods for solving the electronic Schrödinger equation by mitigating the curse of dimensionality inherent in full configuration interaction (FCI) expansions.

**Main result.** The proposed Sparse Configuration Interaction (SCI) method achieves convergence rates to the complete basis set limit that are independent of the number of electrons ($N$), significantly reducing computational scaling compared to conventional methods.

**Method.** The work combines advanced mathematical tools, including Meyer wavelet analysis on weighted Sobolev spaces defined over the $N$-particle domain, with quantum chemistry techniques like SCI and sparse grid approximations.

**Model / system.** The system is governed by the electronic Schrödinger equation for interacting electrons in a molecular or atomic environment. The Hamiltonian involves electron-electron repulsion, electron-nucleus attraction, and nuclear repulsion terms.

**Key observables.** Convergence rate to the Complete Basis Set (CBS) limit; approximation error ($\|\Psi_{	ext{s}} - \Psi_{	ext{BFCI}}\|_{H^1}$); required number of qubits for quantum encoding ($n_{SCI}^q$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of electrons ($N$); size of the one-particle basis set ($K$ or $L$); smoothness indices ($t, r$); and the degree of approximation (related to grid level $L$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on establishing norm equivalences using Meyer wavelets in mixed-regularity weighted Sobolev spaces; it assumes that the underlying eigenfunctions possess sufficient regularity properties.

**Figures summary.** Notes reference comparisons showing SCI dramatically reduces determinant space dimensions and required qubits compared to FCI across various molecules (e.g., H2, FeMoco).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds mathematically by first characterizing function spaces using Meyer wavelets on the $N$-particle domain (Chunks 3 & 4), then applies these tools to derive error bounds for sparse grid approximations (Chunk 7), and finally translates these mathematical findings into computational scaling improvements for both classical solvers and quantum algorithms via SCI (Chunks 1, 2, 5, 6).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this article we revisit regularity results for eigenfunctions in the discrete spectrum of the electronic Schrödinger equation and study their consequences for approximation complexity. In particular, for the convergence to the complete basis set limit, it can be shown that the curse of dimensionality in the leading algebraic exponent can be mitigated. That is, for general sparse grid constructions, the main term of the convergence rate with respect to the number of degrees of freedom is independent of the number of electrons. These insights indicate potential benefits for classical numerical solvers of the electronic Schrödinger equation and also for quantum-computing approaches through new qubit-efficient wavefunction encodings.

</details>

### [Stalls and Spequlation: Pipelined Execution for Fault Tolerant Quantum Computation](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19593v1)

**Authors:** Aditi Awasthi, Gokul Subramanian Ravi, Jonathan Mark Baker  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.19593v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This paper proposes a pipelined execution framework for fault-tolerant quantum computation by decomposing operations into Control, Execute, and Decode stages. By introducing speculative scheduling strategies, the authors demonstrate that overlapping these sequential steps significantly reduces overall computational time compared to treating operations as atomic units. This work provides crucial architectural insights for building practical, high-throughput quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum computation architecture rather than AMO physics directly, the underlying concept of managing sequential dependencies across heterogeneous subsystems—especially when dealing with error correction overheads—is highly relevant to designing complex quantum control systems built from physical platforms like trapped ions or superconducting circuits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Current scheduling models for fault-tolerant quantum computation treat logical operations as atomic, failing to account for the sequential idle time across classical control, quantum hardware, and error decoding subsystems.

**Main result.** Implementing a pipelined execution framework with speculation reduces total pipeline steps by 20-40% compared to non-speculative baselines, significantly improving throughput.

**Method.** The authors decompose logical operations into sequential stages (Control $ightarrow$ Execute $ightarrow$ Decode) and apply speculative scheduling techniques analogous to classical pipelining.

**Model / system.** The model simulates fault-tolerant quantum computation using surface codes/lattice surgery, treating the process as a three-stage pipeline. The system is analyzed by comparing different speculation strategies (Aggressive, Commute-Aware, T-Cautious).

**Key observables.** Reduction in total pipeline steps (20-40% saving), stage utilization smoothing, and the performance hierarchy among scheduling strategies.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of stage durations (assumed 1:1:1 for simplicity) and the frequency/cost associated with speculative rollbacks are key parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include exploiting algebraic properties (Clifford group commutation) to enable speculation, and initially assuming balanced hardware timing across stages.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the three-stage pipeline structure; Figure 4 quantifies percentage savings for different strategies; Figure 5 shows how speculation smooths out bursty utilization across subsystems.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of atomic operation modeling, proposes the pipelined framework (Control/Execute/Decode), details speculative scheduling mechanisms, evaluates performance against baselines using various benchmarks, and concludes by discussing load balancing benefits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fault-tolerant quantum computation requires the coordinated action of three distinct systems: classical control logic, quantum hardware, and classical error decoders. Current scheduling models treat logical operations as atomic, hiding the fact that these subsystems operate sequentially and spend significant time idle. We present a pipelined execution framework that decomposes each logical operation into its component stages i.e. Control, Execute, and Decode. Building on this, we discuss some speculation strategies that allow successor operations to begin processing before their predecessors have completed decoding. We evaluate our framework on several common benchmarks and show that pipelining with speculation reduces total pipeline steps by 20-40% compared to a no-speculation baseline. The most aggressive strategy consistently outperforms conservative alternatives, even though partial rollback is needed at times, because the per-rollback penalty is small relative to the parallelism gained. We further show that speculation facilitates load balancing by distributing work more evenly across the heterogeneous subsystems of a fault-tolerant quantum computer, converting idle time into useful computation while also saving on execution time.

</details>

**Summary.** This work addresses the difficulty of separating intrinsic spin splitting from scattering effects in altermagnets by formulating an inverse transport problem. The authors developed a Physics-Informed Neural Network (PINN) acting as a differentiable Boltzmann solver to enforce strict physical constraints. This novel methodology allows for the robust, simultaneous extraction of both key material parameters ($\alpha$ and $	au_0$) from measured conductance data.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter transport, the core methodology—using PINNs to enforce complex physical conservation laws (like particle/current continuity) within a non-linear PDE framework for parameter identification—is highly relevant to solving open quantum system dynamics or many-body problems where exact analytical solutions are intractable.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is resolving the parameter degeneracy when separating intrinsic spin splitting ($\alpha$) from extrinsic scattering ($	au_0$) in transport measurements of d-wave altermagnets.

**Main result.** By employing a Physics-Informed Neural Network (PINN) constrained by local and global conservation laws, the authors successfully lift this degeneracy to accurately extract both $\alpha$ and $	au_0$.

**Method.** The study uses a differentiable Boltzmann solver implemented via PINNs to solve the semiclassical transport equation in an inverse mode optimization framework.

**Model / system.** The system is a two-dimensional d-wave altermagnet described by a Hamiltonian leading to spin-dependent dispersions. Transport is modeled using the semiclassical Boltzmann equation, bridging ballistic and diffusive regimes.

**Key observables.** Longitudinal conductance ($G$), local electrochemical potential profile ($ar{g}(x)$), and spin-resolved non-equilibrium distributions ($g_{\uparrow/\downarrow}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Altermagnetic coupling ($\alpha$) and momentum relaxation time ($	au_0$). The analysis spans diffusive, crossover, and ballistic transport regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes a semiclassical regime, linear response expansion, and that the local electrochemical potential is shared across spin channels due to non-magnetic contacts. It neglects SOC, magnetic domains, and coherent interference.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the successful reproduction of Boltzmann solutions across regimes (forward mode) and show how the PINN approach allows joint inversion by constraining the fit trajectory away from the compensation valley towards the true physical solution.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework using the semiclassical Boltzmann equation, develops a differentiable solver via PINNs to handle forward calculations, and then applies this solver in an inverse mode optimization loop constrained by current continuity to simultaneously solve for $\alpha$ and $	au_0$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Altermagnets exhibit spin-split electronic structure without requiring spin-orbit coupling, but transport measurements generally mix intrinsic spin splitting with extrinsic scattering. We examine this identifiability problem for a two-dimensional d-wave altermagnet within a unified semiclassical framework spanning ballistic to diffusive transport. The spin-dependent Fermi-surface anisotropy produces a pronounced size effect, where vastly different longitudinal velocities cause the two spin channels to exhibit markedly different effective relaxation lengths within the same device geometry. However, the altermagnetic coupling $α$ and the momentum relaxation time $τ_0$ strongly compensate each other in longitudinal conductance, creating a severe parameter degeneracy. To lift this degeneracy, we formulate a physics-informed neural network (PINN) to act as a differentiable Boltzmann solver that strictly enforces contact injection, local particle conservation, and global current continuity. Driven by sparse conductance spectra, this neural solver leverages the Fermi-level dependence of transport to unlock the coupled parameters simultaneously, achieving sub-percent accuracy even under moderate measurement noise. These results show that combining the Fermi-level dependence of transport with strict physical constraints provides a robust route to separating spin splitting from scattering in altermagnetic conductors.

</details>
